\displaystyle=n_{1}+n_{2}\left(\frac{\Delta_{0}+\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right)

for some
n_{1}
,
n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Hence
f_{j}=n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

To prove (
4.4
), observe that it follows from
Definition

1.24
that

(4.10)

\displaystyle d_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{2j}-\varepsilon^{-2j}}{\varepsilon^{j}-\varepsilon^{-j}}+1=\varepsilon^{j}+\varepsilon^{-j}+1,

from which it also follows that
d_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
. We then have

(4.11)

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta_{j}}{\Delta_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\varepsilon^{j}+\varepsilon^{-j}-2)(\varepsilon^{j}+\varepsilon^{-j}+2)}{\Delta_{0}}=\frac{(\varepsilon^{j}-\varepsilon^{-j})^{2}}{\Delta_{0}}=f_{j}^{2},

(4.12)

\displaystyle d_{2j}+1

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2j}+\varepsilon^{-2j}+2=(\varepsilon^{j}+\varepsilon^{-j})^{2}=(d_{j}-1)^{2},

(4.13)

\displaystyle d_{2j}-3

\displaystyle=(d_{j}-1)^{2}-4=\Delta_{j},

(4.14)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\varepsilon^{j}+\varepsilon^{-j})+(\varepsilon^{j}-\varepsilon^{-j})}{2}=\frac{d_{j}-1+f_{j}\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}=\frac{\sqrt{d_{2j}+1}+\sqrt{d_{2j}-3}}{2},

completing the proof.
∎

The next result is needed for the proof of
Theorem

7.24
.

Lemma 4.4
.

For any pair of integers
j\geq 1

n>1
,

(4.15)

\displaystyle d_{nj}+1

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(d_{\frac{nj}{2}}-1)^{2}\qquad&n\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),\\
(d_{j}+1)\left(1+\sum_{r=1}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}(-1)^{r}d_{rj}\right)^{2}\qquad&n\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right),\\
(d_{j}+1)\left(2+\sum_{r=1}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}(-1)^{r}d_{rj}\right)^{2}\qquad&n\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right).\end{cases}

Proof.

If
n
is even, the result follows from (
4.6
).
If
n=2m+1
, then (
4.4
) implies

\displaystyle\frac{d_{nj}+1}{d_{j}+1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{\left(m+\frac{1}{2}\right)j}+\varepsilon^{-\left(m+\frac{1}{2}\right)j}}{\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}+\varepsilon^{-\frac{j}{2}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\varepsilon^{mj}-\varepsilon^{(m-1)j}+\dots-\varepsilon^{-(m-1)j}+\varepsilon^{-mj}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left((-1)^{m}+\sum_{r=0}^{m-1}(-1)^{r}(d_{(m-r)j}-1)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\left(1+\sum_{r=0}^{m-1}(-1)^{r}d_{(m-r)j}\right)^{2}\quad&\text{$m$ even,}\\
\left(-2+\sum_{r=0}^{m-1}(-1)^{r}d_{(m-r)j}\right)^{2}\quad&\text{$m$ odd,}\end{cases}

(4.16)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\left(1+\sum_{r=1}^{m}(-1)^{r}d_{rj}\right)^{2}\quad&\text{$m$ even,}\\
\left(2+\sum_{r=1}^{m}(-1)^{r}d_{rj}\right)^{2}\quad&\text{$m$ odd,}\end{cases}

giving the last two cases of (
4.15
).
∎

The next result proves the monotoncity of the sequences of
d_{j}
and
f_{j}
.

Theorem 4.5
.

The sequences of
d_{j}
and
f_{j}
satisfy

(4.17)

\displaystyle 4

\displaystyle\leq d_{1}<d_{2}<\cdots,

(4.18)

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\leq f_{1}<f_{2}<\cdots.

Proof.

The fact that
\varepsilon>1
means that, if
x>0
, then

(4.19)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}(\varepsilon^{x}+\varepsilon^{-x}+1)

\displaystyle=\log\varepsilon(\varepsilon^{x}-\varepsilon^{-x})>0,

and

(4.20)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{x}-\varepsilon^{-x}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\log\varepsilon\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{x}+\varepsilon^{-x}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}\right)>0.

Thus, the sequences
d_{j}=\varepsilon^{j}+\varepsilon^{-j}+1
and
f_{j}=\frac{\varepsilon^{j}-\varepsilon^{-j}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}
are monotonically increasing. It is also clear that
d_{1}>3
and
f_{1}>0
, so since they are integers,
d_{1}\geq 4
and
f_{1}\geq 1
.
∎

We now give some special properties of the sequence of dimensions
d_{j}
associated to real quadratic fields with a unit of negative norm.

Theorem 4.6
.

The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\Nm(\varphi)=-1
,

(2)

d_{j}-3
is a perfect square for all odd values of
j
,

(3)

d_{j}-3
is a perfect square for one odd value of
j
.

In that case

(4.21)

\displaystyle\varphi^{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{d_{j}-3}+\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}{2}

for all
j\geq 1
. One has,

(a)

If
j
is odd, then
d_{j}-3
and
\frac{d_{j}+1}{\Delta_{0}}
are perfect squares;

(b)

If
j
is even, then
\frac{d_{j}-3}{\Delta_{0}}
and
d_{j}+1
are perfect squares.

Remark
.

As we will see, on the assumption that the Stark–Tate Conjecture and the Twisted Convolution Conjecture are both true,
Theorem

4.6
explains the empirical observation
[
91
]
that, in every case calculated, the minimal multiplet in dimension
d
has an anti-unitary symmetry if and only if
d-3
is a perfect square, and that it contains a real
r
-SIC fiducial if in addition
d-3
is even.

Proof.

For the equivalence of statements (
1
), (
2
), (
3
), see
[
113
, Thm. 1]
. If
j
is even, (
4.21
) is proved in
Lemma

4.3
. Suppose
j
is odd. Then

(4.22)

\displaystyle\varphi^{j}

\displaystyle=m_{1}+m_{2}\left(\frac{\Delta_{0}+\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right)

for some pair of integers
m_{1},m_{2}
such that
2m_{1}+m_{2}\Delta_{0}
,
m_{2}
are both positive. The fact that
\Nm(\varphi^{j})=-1
means

(4.23)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2m_{1}+m_{2}\Delta_{0}}{2}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{m_{2}\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right)^{2}=-1

while the fact that
\varphi^{2j}+\varphi^{-2j}+1=d_{j}
means

(4.24)

\displaystyle 2\left(\frac{2m_{1}+m_{2}\Delta_{0}}{2}\right)^{2}+2\left(\frac{m_{2}\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right)^{2}=d_{j}-1.

Hence

(4.25)

\displaystyle\left(2m_{1}+m_{2}\Delta_{0}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=d_{j}-3;

(4.26)

\displaystyle\left(m_{2}\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=d_{j}+1.

Hence

(4.27)

\displaystyle\varphi^{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{d_{j}-3}+\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}{2}.

This also shows that, if
j
is odd, then
d_{j}-3
and
\frac{d_{j}+1}{\Delta_{0}}
are perfect squares. For the proof that
\frac{d_{j}-3}{\Delta_{0}}
and
d_{j}+1
are perfect squares when
j
is even, see
Lemma

4.3
.
∎

We will now define
variant Chebyshev polynomials
and use them to express the pair
(d_{nj},f_{nj})
as a function of the pair
(d_{j},f_{j})
for any natural number
n
, as promised.

Definition 4.7

(variant Chebyshev polynomials)
.

For all
j\in\mathbb{N}
define

(4.28)

\displaystyle T^{*}_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=1+2T_{j}\left(\frac{x-1}{2}\right),

(4.29)

\displaystyle U^{*}_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=U_{j-1}\left(\frac{x-1}{2}\right)

where the
T_{j}
and
U_{j}
are respectively Chebyshev polynomials of the first and second kind.

Theorem 4.8
.

For
n
,
j\in\mathbb{N}

(4.30)

\displaystyle d_{nj}

\displaystyle=T^{*}_{n}(d_{j}),

(4.31)

\displaystyle f_{nj}

\displaystyle=f_{j}U^{*}_{n}(d_{j})

Proof.

For the proof of (
4.30
), see
[
14
, eq. (13)]
.
To prove (
4.31
), let
\theta=\log\varepsilon
. Then

(4.32)

\displaystyle f_{nj}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\sinh(nj\theta)}{\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}=\frac{2U_{n-1}(\cosh j\theta)\sinh j\theta}{\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}=f_{j}U^{*}_{n}(d_{j}),

which is (
4.31
).
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

T^{*}_{j}(x)
,
U^{*}_{j}(x)
satisfy the recursion relations

(4.33)

\displaystyle T^{*}_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=x,

\displaystyle T^{*}_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=x(x-2),

\displaystyle T^{*}_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=3-x+(x-1)T^{*}_{j-1}(x)-T^{*}_{j-2}(x),

(4.34)

\displaystyle U^{*}_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle U^{*}_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=x-1,

\displaystyle U^{*}_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=(x-1)U^{*}_{j-1}(x)-U^{*}_{j-2}(x).

For all
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

(4.35)

\displaystyle T^{*}_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}3+x^{2}\left(-\frac{j^{2}}{3}+\Ord(x)\right)\quad&\text{$j\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$,}\\
x\left(j+\frac{j(j-1)}{6}x+\Ord(x^{2})\right)\quad&\text{$j\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$,}\\
x\left(-j+\frac{j(j+1)}{6}x+\Ord(x^{2})\right)\quad&\text{$j\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$,}\end{cases}

(4.36)

\displaystyle U^{*}_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}x\left(-\frac{2j}{3}+\frac{j}{3}x+\Ord(x^{2})\right)\quad&\text{$j\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$,}\\
1+x\left(\frac{j-1}{3}-\frac{(j-1)(j+2)}{6}x+\Ord(x^{2})\right)\quad&\text{$j\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$,}\\
-1+x\left(\frac{j+1}{3}+\frac{(j+1)(j-2)}{6}x+\Ord(x^{2})\right)\quad&\text{$j\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Straightfoward consequence of the recursion relations for the Chebyshev polynomials.
∎

We are now in a position to prove some congruence and divisibility properties of the
d_{j}
and
f_{j}
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
j,n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(1)

d_{j}
is a divisor of
d_{nj}
if and only if
n\not\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
.

(2)

If
d_{j}
is odd, then

(a)

\Delta_{0}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
if
f_{j}
is odd,

(b)

\Delta_{nj}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
for all
n
,

(c)

d_{nj}
is odd for all
n
,

(d)

if
f_{j}
is even, then
f_{nj}
is even for all
n
,

(e)

if
f_{j}
is odd, then
f_{nj}
is odd if and only if
n
is odd.

(3)

If
d_{j}
is even, then

(a)

\Delta_{0}\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
,

(b)

\Delta_{nj}\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
if and only if
n\not\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
,

(c)

d_{nj}
is even if and only if
n\not\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
,

(d)

f_{nj}
is odd if and only if
n\not\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
.

Proof.

Item

1
is a consequence of
(
4.35
) and the fact that
d_{j}>3
.

To prove
item

2a
, observe that the fact that
\Delta_{0}
is a discriminant means it is congruent to
0
or
1
modulo
4
. The fact that

(4.37)

\displaystyle f_{j}^{2}\Delta_{0}

\displaystyle=(d_{j}-3)(d_{j}+1)

is even, together with the fact that
f_{j}
is odd, then implies that
\Delta_{0}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
.

To prove
item

2b
and
item

2c
, observe that it follows from (
4.33
) that, if
d_{j}
is odd, then

(4.38)

\displaystyle T^{*}_{1}(d_{j})

\displaystyle\equiv T^{*}_{2}(d_{j})\equiv 1\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),

(4.39)

\displaystyle\text{and }\ T^{*}_{n}(d_{j})

\displaystyle\equiv T^{*}_{n-2}(d_{j})\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right).

Consequently
d_{nj}=T^{*}_{n}(d_{j})
is odd for all
n
. It then follows that
\Delta_{nj}=(d_{nj}-3)(d_{nj}+1)
is even for all
n
. Since
\Delta_{nj}
is a discriminant, it follows that we must in fact have
\Delta_{nj}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
.

Item

2d
is an immediate consequence of
(
4.31
).

To prove
item

2e
observe that it follows from
(
4.34
) that
U^{*}_{n}(d_{j})
is odd if and only if
n
is odd. Since
f_{j}
is odd, it follows from
(
4.31
) that
f_{nj}
is odd if and only if
n
is odd.

To prove
item

3a
, observe that if
d_{j}
is even, then

(4.40)

\displaystyle f_{j}^{2}\Delta^{\vphantom{2}}_{0}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\vphantom{2}}_{j}=(d_{j}-3)(d_{j}+1)\equiv 1\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),

implying that
f_{j}
,
\Delta_{0}
,
\Delta_{j}
are all odd. The statement follows from this and the fact that
\Delta_{0}
is a discriminant, which means
\Delta_{0}
is congruent to
0
or
1
modulo
4
.

Item

3c
is an immediate consequence of (
4.35
). It then follows that

(4.41)

\displaystyle\Delta_{nj}

\displaystyle=(d_{nj}-3)(d_{nj}+1)

is odd if and only if
n\not\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
.

Item

3b
is a consequence of this and the fact that
\Delta_{nj}
is a discriminant (and thus congruent to
0
or
1
modulo
4
).

Finally, (
4.37
) together with the fact that
d_{j}
is even means
f_{j}
is odd. In view of
(
4.36
),

(4.42)

\displaystyle f_{nj}

\displaystyle=f_{j}U^{*}_{n}(d_{j})\equiv\begin{cases}0\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\quad&\text{if $n\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$},\\
1\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\quad&\text{if $n\not\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$}.\end{cases}

This establishes
item

3d
and completes the proof.
∎

4.2.
Unit group of an order

As we will see,
under our conjectures,
there is a natural correspondence between
r
-SIC multiplets and orders of the real quadratic field
K
.
In this subsection we prove some facts about the unit group of an order which will be needed in the sequel.

Definition 4.11

(unit group, and positive norm unit group of conductor
f
)
.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{f}
be the order of conductor
f
in the real quadratic field
K
(see
Definition

1.47
).
Define

(4.43)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{f}

\displaystyle=\{w\in\mathcal{O}_{f}\colon\Nm(w)=\pm 1\}=\mathcal{O}_{f}^{\times}

to be the unit group of
\mathcal{O}_{f}
, and

(4.44)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{+}_{f}

\displaystyle=\{w\in\mathcal{O}_{f}\colon\Nm(w)=1\}

to be the subgroup of
\mathcal{U}_{f}
consisting of all positive norm units.

Remark
.

To avoid cluttering the notation, we do not indicate the field
K
explicitly. It will always be clear from context.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
K
be a real quadratic field ,and let
f_{1},f_{2},\dots
be its associated sequence of conductors. Then, for each positive integer
f
, there exists a positive integer
j
such that
f\!\mid\!f_{j}
.

Proof.

It follows from the generalization of Dirichlet’s unit theorem to an arbitrary order (see, for example,
[
70
]
) that

(4.45)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{+}_{f}=\{\pm w^{n}\colon n\in\mathbb{Z}\}

for some unit
w\neq\pm 1
. It can be assumed, without loss of generality, that
w>1
. We then have

(4.46)

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{j}=\frac{d_{j}-1-f_{j}\Delta_{0}}{2}+f_{j}\left(\frac{\Delta_{0}+\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right)

for some
j\in\mathbb{N}
. The fact that
w\in\mathcal{O}_{f}
implies
f\!\mid\!f_{j}
.
∎

This result motivates the following definition.

Definition 4.13

(minimum level; fundamental positive norm unit of an order)
.

Let
K
be a real quadratic field, and let
f_{1},f_{2},\dots
be its associated sequence of conductors. For each positive integer
f
, define

(4.47)

\displaystyle j_{\rm{min}}(f)

\displaystyle=\min\{j\in\mathbb{N}\colon f|f_{j}\},

and

(4.48)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{f}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}.

Remark
.

This definition depends on
Theorem

4.12
for its validity. Note that
j_{\rm{min}}(f)
and
\varepsilon_{f}
both depend on
K
as well as
f
, but to avoid cluttering the notation the
K
-dependence is not explicitly indicated.
The identity of
K
will always be clear from context. It follows from the next theorem that
\varepsilon_{f}
is the smallest unit greater than
1
in
\mathcal{U}^{+}_{f}
.

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
K
be a real quadratic field. Then:

(1)

For all
f\in\mathbb{N}
,

(4.49)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{+}_{f}

\displaystyle=\{\pm\varepsilon_{f}^{n}\colon n\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

(2)

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(4.50)

\displaystyle j_{\rm{min}}(f_{k})

\displaystyle=k.

(3)

For all
f,j\in\mathbb{N}
,

(4.51)

\displaystyle f\!\mid\!f_{j}\iff j_{\rm{min}}(f)\!\mid\!j.

(4)

For all
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(4.52)

\displaystyle j\!\mid\!k\iff f_{j}\!\mid\!f_{k}

Proof.

Statement (
1
).
Let
w
,
j
be as in (
4.45
) and (
4.46
). The fact that
f\!\mid\!f_{j}
implies
j_{\rm{min}}(f)\leq j
. On the other hand, the fact that
f\!\mid\!f_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}
implies

(4.53)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-1-f_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}\Delta_{0}}{2}+f_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}\left(\frac{\Delta_{0}+\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right)

is in
\mathcal{O}_{f}
and consequently
\mathcal{U}^{+}_{f}
. Since
\varepsilon^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}>1
we must have
\varepsilon^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}=w^{t}=\varepsilon^{tj}
for some
t\in\mathbb{N}
, implying
j_{\rm{min}}(f)\geq j
. We conclude
j_{\rm{min}}(f)=j
.

Statement (
2
).
It follows from
Theorem

4.5
that
j\geq k
for all
j\in\{j\in\mathbb{N}\colon f_{k}|f_{j}\}
, implying
j_{\rm{min}}(f_{k})\geq k
. Since
k
itself is in
\{j\in\mathbb{N}\colon f_{k}|f_{j}\}
, we must in fact have
j_{\rm{min}}(f_{k})=k
.

Statement (
3
).
Observe that
f\!\mid\!f_{j}
if and only if

(4.54)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{j}=\frac{d_{j}-1-f_{j}\Delta_{0}}{2}+f_{j}\left(\frac{\Delta_{0}+\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right)

is in
\mathcal{O}_{f}
, and consequently in
\mathcal{U}^{+}_{f}
. In view of Statement (
1
) this means
f\!\mid\!f_{j}
if and only if
\varepsilon^{j}=\varepsilon_{f}^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, which in turn is true if and only if
j_{\rm{min}}(f)\!\mid\!j
.

Statement (
4
).
It follows from statements (
2
) and (
3
) that

(4.55)

\displaystyle j\!\mid\!k\iff j_{\rm{min}}(f_{j})\!\mid\!k\iff f_{j}\!\mid\!f_{k},

giving (
4.52
).
∎

We see from this that the conductor
f
is naturally associated to the infinite sequence of dimensions
d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)},d_{2j_{\rm{min}}(f)},d_{3j_{\rm{min}}(f)},\dots
. As we will see, if the Stark Conjecture and Twisted Convolution Conjecture are correct, then the members of the corresponding sequence of
1
-SIC multiplets are related. This is a generalization of the phenomenon of
1
-SIC alignment (see
[
8
,
3
,
17
]
and
Definition

3.22
above).
As we will see,

Theorem

4.6
, combined with the our conjectures, tells us for which minimal multiplets (multiplets corresponding to
f=1
) there is an anti-unitary symmetry. In the sequel we will also address the question of which non-minimal multiplets have such a symmetry.
If
d_{1}-3
is not a perfect square the question is easily answered, as it is then automatic that
\mathcal{U}^{\vphantom{+}}_{f}=\mathcal{U}^{+}_{f}
for all
f
. The next theorem determines for which values of
f
the unit group
\mathcal{U}_{f}
contains a negative norm unit if
d_{1}-3
is a perfect square.

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
f
be any positive integer. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{U}^{\vphantom{+}}_{f}
contains a negative norm unit,

(2)

d_{1}-3
is a perfect square,
j_{\rm{min}}(f)
is odd, and the integer
\sqrt{\frac{d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}+1}{\Delta_{0}}}=\frac{f_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}}{\sqrt{d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-3}}
is divisible by
f
.

In that case,

(4.56)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{f}

\displaystyle=\{\pm\varphi^{nj_{\rm{min}}(f)}\colon n\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

Proof.

(
1
)
\implies
(
2
). The fact that
\mathcal{U}_{f}
contains a negative norm unit means
\varphi
must be negative norm. Theorem
4.6
then implies that
d_{1}-3
is a perfect square. We may assume, without loss of generality, that the negative norm unit is greater than 1, and therefore equal to
\varphi^{j}
for some odd positive integer
j
. Since
\varepsilon^{j}=\varphi^{2j}\in\mathcal{U}^{+}_{f}
we must have
j=\ell j_{\rm{min}}(f)
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. The fact that
j
is odd means
\ell
and
j_{\rm{min}}(f)
are both odd. In particular
\ell=2p+1
for some non-negative integer
p
. Since

(4.57)

\displaystyle\varphi^{2pj_{\rm{min}}(f)}=\varepsilon^{pj_{\rm{min}}(f)}\in\mathcal{U}_{f}

this means
\varphi^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}\in\mathcal{U}_{f}
.
It follows from Theorem
4.6
that

(4.58)

\displaystyle d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-3

\displaystyle=m_{1}^{2},

(4.59)

\displaystyle d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}+1

\displaystyle=m_{2}^{2}\Delta_{0},

(4.60)

\displaystyle f_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-3)(d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}+1)}{\Delta_{0}}=m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2},

and

(4.61)

\displaystyle\varphi^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{1}+m_{2}\Delta_{0}}{2}=\frac{m_{1}-m_{2}\Delta_{0}}{2}+m_{2}\left(\frac{\Delta_{0}+\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right),

for some pair of positive integers
m_{1}
,
m_{2}
. The fact that
\varphi^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}\in\mathcal{U}_{f}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{f}
then implies that
f
is a divisor of
m_{2}=f_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}/m_{1}
.

(
2
)
\implies
(
1
).
The fact that
d_{1}-3
is a perfect square means, in view of
Theorem

4.6
, that
\varphi
is negative norm.
Since
j_{\rm{min}}(f)
is odd
\varphi^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}
is also negative norm.
It follows from Theorem
4.6
that
\varphi^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}
can be written in the form of (
4.61
), with
m_{1}
,
m_{2}
as given by (
4.58
) and (
4.59
).
Since
f\!\mid\!m_{2}
, it follows that
\varphi^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}
is in
\mathcal{O}_{f}
, and therefore in
\mathcal{U}_{f}
.

To prove the last statement, suppose
\mathcal{U}_{f}
contains a negative norm unit. Then it follows from the argument above that
\varphi^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}\in\mathcal{U}_{f}
. So

(4.62)

\displaystyle\{\pm\varphi^{nj_{\rm{min}}(f)}\colon n\in\mathbb{Z}\}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{f}.

Conversely, let
w
be any element of
\mathcal{U}_{f}
. Without loss of generality we may assume
w>1
. Then
w=\varphi^{\ell}
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
\varepsilon^{\ell}=w^{2}\in\mathcal{U}^{+}_{f}
, we must have
\ell=nj_{\rm{min}}(f)
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. So
w\in\{\pm\varphi^{nj_{\rm{min}}(f)}\colon n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.
∎

Definition 4.16

(fundamental unit of an order)
.

Given a real quadratic field
K
and positive integer
f
, define

(4.63)

\displaystyle\varphi_{f}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\varepsilon^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}\quad&\text{if }\mathcal{U}_{f}=\mathcal{U}^{+}_{f},\\
\varphi^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}\quad&\text{if }\mathcal{U}_{f}\supsetneq\mathcal{U}^{+}_{f}.\end{cases}

Corollary 4.17
.

For all
f\in\mathbb{N}

(4.64)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{f}

\displaystyle=\{\pm\varphi_{f}^{n}\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\}.

Proof.

Immediate consequence of Theorem
4.15
.
∎

4.3.
Dimension grid

So far we have been focusing on the dimension tower
d_{1},d_{2},\dots
.
In the sequel we will show that, if the Stark Conjecture and Twisted Convolution Conjecture are correct, these are the dimensions in which there exist
1
-SICs with base field
K
.
By contrast, it will turn out that
r
-SICs with base field
K
and
r>1
are found in dimensions
d_{j,m}
, with
m>1
.
The purpose of this subsection is to establish some properties of these dimensions which will be needed in the sequel.

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
\theta=\log\varepsilon
. Then the following equalities hold for all
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
.

(4.65)

\displaystyle r_{j,m}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh mj\theta}{\sinh j\theta}

(4.66)

\displaystyle d_{j,m}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh\frac{(2m+1)j\theta}{2}}{\sinh\frac{j\theta}{2}}

(4.67)

\displaystyle d_{j}

\displaystyle=1+2\cosh j\theta

Proof.

Straightforward consequence of the definitions.
∎

Proposition 4.19
.

Let
K
be a real quadratic field.
Then the following holds for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

(4.68)

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=r_{j,1}<r_{j,2}<\cdots

(4.69)

\displaystyle 3

\displaystyle<d_{j,1}<d_{j,2}<\cdots

Proof.

Straightforward consequence of Proposition
4.18
.
∎

We now characterize admissible pairs
(d,r)
(see
Definition

1.21
), which come from integer solutions
(d,n,r)
to the three-variable Diophantine equation
nr(d-r)=d^{2}-1
, in terms of rank and dimension grids.

Theorem 4.20
.

This theorem has two parts.

(A)

Let
K
be a real quadratic field, and let
r_{j,m}
,
d_{j,m}
be the associated rank and dimension grids.
Then for all
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
,

(1)

0<r_{j,m}<\frac{d_{j,m}-1}{2}
, and

(2)

(d_{j}+1)r_{j,m}(d_{j,m}-r_{j,m})=d^{2}_{j,m}-1
.

(B)

Conversely, let
r
,
d
be any pair of integers such that

(1)

0<r<\frac{d-1}{2}
, and

(2)

nr(d-r)=d^{2}-1
for some integer
n>4
.

Then there exists a unique real quadratic field
K
and unique natural numbers
j,m
such that
r=r_{j,m}
,
d=d_{j,m}
, and
n=d_{j}+1
where
r_{j,m}
,
d_{j,m}
are the rank and dimension grids associated to
K
.

Proof.

Part (A).
To prove
item

1
, let
\theta=\log\varepsilon
. Then it follows from
Proposition

4.18
that

\displaystyle\frac{d_{j,m}-1}{2}-r_{j,m}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh\frac{(2m+1)j\theta}{2}}{2\sinh\frac{j\theta}{2}}-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\sinh mj\theta}{\sinh j\theta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh mj\theta\cosh\frac{j\theta}{2}+\cosh mj\theta\sinh\frac{j\theta}{2}}{2\sinh\frac{j\theta}{2}}-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\sinh mj\theta}{\sinh j\theta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh mj\theta}{\sinh j\theta}\left(\cosh^{2}\frac{j\theta}{2}-1\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\cosh mj\theta-1\right)

(4.70)

\displaystyle>0.

The fact that
0<r_{j,m}
is immediate. To prove
item

2
, observe
Proposition

4.18
also implies

\displaystyle(d_{j}+1)r_{j,m}(d_{j,m}-1)

\displaystyle=(d_{j}+1)r_{j,m}r_{j,m+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2(1+\cosh j\theta)\sinh mj\theta\sinh(m+1)j\theta}{\sinh^{2}j\theta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh mj\theta\sinh(m+1)j\theta}{\sinh^{2}\frac{j\theta}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\cosh(2m+1)j\theta-\cosh j\theta}{2\sinh^{2}\frac{j\theta}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh^{2}\frac{(2m+1)j\theta}{2}-\sinh^{2}\frac{j\theta}{2}}{\sinh^{2}\frac{j\theta}{2}}

(4.71)

\displaystyle=d^{2}_{j,m}-1.

Part (B).
The fact that
nr(d-r)=d^{2}-1
implies

(4.72)

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=\frac{nr\pm\sqrt{r^{2}n(n-4)+4}}{2}

The fact that
n>4
means
K=\mathbb{Q}\!\left(\sqrt{n(n-4)}\right)
is a real quadratic field. Let
\Delta_{0}
be its discriminant, and
f_{j}
,
d_{j}
,
d_{j,m}
,
r_{j,m}
the associated conductors, dimensions, and ranks.
It then follows from Theorem
3.20
that
n=d_{j}+1
for some
j
. So

(4.73)

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=\frac{r(d_{j}+1)\pm\sqrt{r^{2}f_{j}^{2}\Delta_{0}+4}}{2}

Since
d
is an integer we must have

(4.74)

\displaystyle r^{2}f_{j}^{2}\Delta_{0}+4

\displaystyle=p^{2}

for some
p\in\mathbb{N}
. Let

(4.75)

\displaystyle w_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\frac{p\pm rf_{j}\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}.

Then
w_{\pm}
are positive norm units in
K
, with
0<w_{-}<1<w_{+}
. So
w_{\pm}=\varepsilon^{\pm k}
for some positive integer
k
. We then have

(4.76)

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{k}+\varepsilon^{-k}=d_{k}-1

and

\displaystyle\qquad rf_{j}\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{k}-\varepsilon^{-k}

(4.77)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\qquad rf_{j}

\displaystyle=f_{k}

In view of
Theorem

4.14
, this means
k=jm
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Consequently,

(4.78)

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=r_{j,m}

and

(4.79)

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{j,m}(d_{j}+1)\pm(d_{jm}-1)}{2}.

Setting
\theta=\log\varepsilon
and using Proposition
4.18
the second expression becomes

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh mj\theta\left(1+\cosh j\theta\right)}{\sinh j\theta}\pm\cosh mj\theta

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh mj\theta+\sinh(m\pm 1)j\theta}{\sinh j\theta}

(4.80)

\displaystyle=r_{j,m}+r_{j,m\pm 1}.

The fact that
d\geq 2r
, together with Lemma
4.19
, means we must in fact have

(4.81)

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=r_{j,m}+r_{j,m+1}=d_{j,m}.

It remains to prove uniqueness. Suppose
r=r^{\prime}_{j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}
and
d=d^{\prime}_{j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}
, where
r^{\prime}_{j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}
and
d^{\prime}_{j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}
are in the rank and dimension towers associated to some other real quadratic field
K^{\prime}
. Then it follows from Part (A) that

\displaystyle(d^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}+1)r(d-r)

\displaystyle=(d^{\prime}_{j}+1)r^{\prime}_{j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}(d^{\prime}_{j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}-r^{\prime}_{j^{\prime},m^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=d^{{}^{\prime}2}_{j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}-1

\displaystyle=d^{2}_{j,m}-1

\displaystyle=(d_{j}+1)r_{j,m}(d_{j,m}-r_{j,m})

(4.82)

\displaystyle=(d_{j}+1)r(d-r),

implying
d^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}=d_{j}
. Hence

(4.83)

\displaystyle K^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}\left(\sqrt{(d^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}+1)(d^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}-3)}\right)=\mathbb{Q}\left(\sqrt{(d_{j}+1)(d_{j}-3)}\right)=K

and
j^{\prime}=j
. Consequently
d_{j,m^{\prime}}=d_{j,m}
which, together with the fact that
d_{j,1},d_{j,2},\dots
is a monotonically increasing sequence (see
Proposition

4.19
), implies
m^{\prime}=m
.
∎

This result implies the following expression for
\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
, as an alternative to (
1.56
).

Corollary 4.21
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
be an admissible tuple. Then

(4.84)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\,\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t),

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbf{p}}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\nu_{\mathbf{p}}(s)

for all
\mathbf{p}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
.

Proof.

Immediate consequence of

(4.85)

\displaystyle(d_{j}+1)r_{j,m}(d_{j,m}-r_{j,m})

\displaystyle=d^{2}_{j,m}-1

from
Theorem

4.20
(A)(2).
∎

Corollary 4.22
.

d_{j,m}
is coprime to
r_{j,m}
for all
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Immediate consequence of (
4.85
).
∎

The next lemma collects together various technical results which will be needed in the sequel.

Lemma 4.23
.

The following inequalities and equations hold for all
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
.

(4.86)

\displaystyle r_{j,m}

\displaystyle<r_{j,m+1}-1

(4.87)

\displaystyle r_{j,m}

\displaystyle<\frac{d_{j,m}-1}{2}

(4.88)

\displaystyle r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{d_{(2m+1)j}+1}{d_{j}+1}}

(4.89)

\displaystyle r^{2}_{j,m+1}-r^{2}_{j,m}

\displaystyle=r^{\vphantom{2}}_{j,2m+1}

(4.90)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{(2m+1)j}-1

\displaystyle=d_{j,m}\varepsilon^{mj}(\varepsilon^{j}-1)

(4.91)

\displaystyle d_{j,m+2}

\displaystyle=(d_{j}-1)d_{j,m+1}-d_{j,m}

If
d_{j}
is even, then

(1)

r_{j,m}
is even if and only if
m\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
;

(2)

d_{j,m}
is even if and only if
m\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
.

If
d_{j}
is odd, then

(1)

r_{j,m}
is odd if and only if
m
is odd;

(2)

d_{j,m}
is odd for all
m
.

If
d_{j,m}
is even, then the following congruences hold.

(4.92)

\displaystyle d_{(m+1)j}-d_{mj}

\displaystyle\equiv d_{j}\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)

(4.93)

\displaystyle d_{j,m}

\displaystyle\equiv d_{j}\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)

(4.94)

\displaystyle r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m}

\displaystyle\equiv d_{j}+2\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)

(4.95)

\displaystyle f_{j(m+1)}-f_{jm}

\displaystyle\equiv d_{j}+2\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
4.18
that

(4.96)

\displaystyle r_{j,m+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh j(m+1)\theta}{\sinh j\theta}=\frac{\sinh jm\theta\cosh j\theta}{\sinh j\theta}+\cosh jm\theta>r_{j,m}+1,

(4.97)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{j,m}-1}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{j,m+1}+r_{j,m}-1}{2}>r_{j,m},

\displaystyle r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh j(m+1)\theta-\sinh jm\theta}{\sinh j\theta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\cosh\frac{(2m+1)j\theta}{2}}{\cosh\frac{j\theta}{2}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\cosh(2m+1)j\theta+1}{\cosh j\theta+1}}

(4.98)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{d_{(2m+1)j}+1}{d_{j}+1}},

\displaystyle r^{2}_{j,m+1}-r^{2}_{j,m}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh^{2}(m+1)j\theta-\sinh^{2}mj\theta}{\sinh^{2}j\theta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\cosh 2(m+1)j\theta-\cosh 2mj\theta}{2\sinh^{2}j\theta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\cosh 2mj\theta\cosh 2j\theta+\sinh 2mj\theta\sinh 2j\theta-\cosh 2mj\theta}{2\sinh^{2}j\theta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\cosh 2mj\theta\sinh^{2}j\theta+\sinh 2mj\theta\sinh j\theta\cosh j\theta}{\sinh^{2}j\theta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh(2m+1)j\theta}{\sinh j\theta}

(4.99)

\displaystyle=r_{j,2m+1},

\displaystyle d_{j,m}\varepsilon^{mj}(\varepsilon^{j}-1)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh\frac{(2m+1)j\theta}{2}e^{mj\theta}\left(e^{j\theta}-1\right)}{\sinh\frac{j\theta}{2}}

\displaystyle=e^{(2m+1)j\theta}-1

(4.100)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{(2m+1)j}-1.

It follows from Theorem
4.8
and Lemma
4.9
that

\displaystyle d_{j,m+2}

\displaystyle=r_{j,m+3}+r_{j,m+2}

\displaystyle=U^{*}_{m+3}(d_{j})+U^{*}_{m+2}(d_{j})

\displaystyle=(d_{j}-1)\left(U^{*}_{m+2}(d_{j})+U^{*}_{m+1}(d_{j})\right)-\left(U^{*}_{m+1}(d_{j})+U^{*}_{m}(d_{j})\right)

(4.101)

\displaystyle=(d_{j}-1)d_{j,m+1}-d_{j,m}.

Suppose
d_{j}
is even. Then (
4.31
) and (
4.36
) imply

(4.102)

\displaystyle r_{j,m}

\displaystyle=U^{*}_{m}(d_{j})=\begin{cases}0\ &\text{if }m\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right),\\
1\ &\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

from which it follows that
d_{j,m}=r_{j,m}+r_{j,m+1}
is even if and only if
m\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
.

Suppose
d_{j}
is odd. Then
(
4.34
) implies
U^{*}_{j,1}
is odd,
U^{*}_{j,2}
is even, and
U^{*}_{j,m}\equiv U^{*}_{j,m-2}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
for all
m>2
. Consequently
r_{j,m}=U^{*}_{m}(d_{j})
is odd if and only
m
is odd, and
d_{j,m}=U^{*}_{m+1}(d_{j})+U^{*}_{m}(d_{j})
is odd for all
m
.

Suppose
d_{j,m}
is even. Then it follows from results just proved that
d_{j}
is even and
m\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
. Equation (
4.35
) then implies

(4.103)

\displaystyle d_{(m+1)j}-d_{mj}=T^{*}_{m+1}(d_{j})-T^{*}_{m}(d_{j})

\displaystyle\equiv-(2m+1)d_{j}\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d_{j}^{2}\right)

(4.104)

\displaystyle\equiv d_{j}\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right),

while
(
4.36
) implies

\displaystyle d_{j,m}

\displaystyle=r_{j,m+1}+r_{j,m}

\displaystyle=U^{*}_{m+1}(d_{j})+U^{*}_{m}(d_{j})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{(2m+1)d_{j}}{3}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d_{j}^{2}\right)

(4.105)

\displaystyle\equiv d_{j}\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right),

\displaystyle r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m}

\displaystyle=U^{*}_{m+1}(d_{j})-U^{*}_{m}(d_{j})

\displaystyle\equiv-2+d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d_{j}^{2}\right)

(4.106)

\displaystyle\equiv d_{j}+2\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right).

Finally, it follows from Lemma
4.10
that
f_{j}
is odd, and thus

(4.107)

\displaystyle f_{(m+1)j}-f_{mj}

\displaystyle=f_{j}\left(r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m}\right)\equiv d_{j}+2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right),

completing the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
r^{-1}_{j,m}
be the multiplicative inverse of
r_{j,m}
modulo
\bar{d}_{j,m}
. Then

(4.108)

\displaystyle r^{-1}_{j,m}

\displaystyle\equiv r^{\vphantom{-1}}_{j,m}\left(1+d_{j}+d_{j,m}\right)\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\bar{d}_{j,m}\right).

Proof.

It follows from Theorem
4.20
that

(4.109)

\displaystyle(d_{j}+1)r_{j,m}(r_{j,m}-d_{j,m})\equiv 1-d_{j,m}^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv 1\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\bar{d}_{j,m}\right).

If
d_{j,m}
is even, then it follows from Corollary
4.22
and Lemma
4.23
that
d_{j}+1
and
r_{j,m}
are both odd. So (
4.109
) implies

(4.110)

\displaystyle(d_{j}+1)r_{j,m}^{2}+d_{j,m}\equiv 1\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\bar{d}_{j,m}\right).

Consequently

(4.111)

\displaystyle r_{j,m}^{-1}

\displaystyle\equiv(d_{j}+1)r_{j,m}+d_{j,m}r^{-1}_{j,m}=(d_{j}+1)r_{j,m}+d_{j,m}\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\bar{d}_{j,m}\right).

In view of
Corollary

4.22
we can alternatively write

(4.112)

\displaystyle r_{j,m}^{-1}

\displaystyle\equiv r_{j,m}(1+d_{j}+d_{j,m})\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\bar{d}_{j,m}\right).

∎

Lemma 4.25
.

d_{j}\pm 1
are coprime to
d_{j,m}
for all
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

The fact that
d_{j}+1
is coprime to
d_{j,m}
is an immediate consequence of the relation

(4.113)

\displaystyle(d_{j}+1)r_{j,m}(d_{j,m}-r_{j,m})

\displaystyle=d^{2}_{j,m}-1

proved in Theorem
4.20
.

To prove that
d_{j}-1
is coprime to
d_{j,m}
observe that it follows from Lemma
4.23
that

(4.114)

\displaystyle d_{j,m}\equiv-d_{j,m-2}\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d_{j}-1\right)

for all
m>2
. Consequently

(4.115)

\displaystyle d_{j,m}\equiv\begin{cases}\pm d_{j,1}\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,(d_{j}-1)\right)\quad&\text{if $m$ odd}\\
\pm d_{j,2}\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,(d_{j}-1)\right)\quad&\text{if $m$ even}\end{cases}

Since

(4.116)

\displaystyle d_{j,1}

\displaystyle\equiv d_{j}\equiv 1\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d_{j}-1\right)

and

(4.117)

\displaystyle d_{j,2}

\displaystyle=(d_{j}-1)^{2}+(d_{j}-1)-1\equiv-1\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d_{j}-1\right),

it follows that
d_{j,m}\equiv\pm 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d_{j}-1\right)
, and
d_{j,m}
is thus coprime to
d_{j}-1
, for all
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

4.4.
Representations

An important role in the following is played by what we refer to as
canonical representations
of the real quadratic base field
K
.
These are faithful
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra representations of
K
by
2\times 2
matrices. All such representations are isomorphic, justifying the term
canonical
. However, up to equality, there are multiple canonical representations, and they are in natural bijective correspondence with the set of forms associated to
K
(recall the definition of
form
in
Section

1.3
).
This correspondence in turn leads to a natural correspondence between extended Clifford group orbits of
r
-SICs and equivalence classes of forms.

The purpose of this subsection is to derive the results on canonical representations which will be needed in the sequel.
We begin with some preliminary definitions and lemmas.

Definition 4.26

(matrices; symmetric matrices; trace-zero matrices)
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring with identity. Then

(1)

\mathcal{M}(R)
is the ring of
2\times 2
matrices over
R
,

(2)

\mathcal{M}_{\rm{S}}(R)\subseteq\mathcal{M}(R)
is the subring of symmetric matrices,

(3)

\mathcal{M}_{0}(R)\subseteq\mathcal{M}(R)
is the additive subgroup of trace-zero matrices.

Definition 4.27

(matrix sub-algebra)
.

If
R
is a field and
M_{1},\dots,M_{n}\in\mathcal{M}(R)
, then
R\langle M_{1},\dots,M_{n}\rangle
denotes the
R
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{M}(R)
generated by the matrices
M_{j}
.

Definition 4.28

(generators of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
)
.

Let

(4.118)

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

be the usual generators of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Lemma 4.29
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring with identity.

(1)

If
M\in\mathcal{M}_{0}(R)
, then

M^{2}=-\Det(M)I
.

(2)

The map
M\mapsto SM
is a bijection of
\mathcal{M}_{\rm{S}}(R)
onto
\mathcal{M}_{0}(R)
.

Suppose further that
R
is a field.

(3)

If
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}(R)
are both non-zero, then
MM^{\prime}=M^{\prime}M
if and only if
M^{\prime}=\lambda M
for some non-zero
\lambda\in R
.

(4)

If
M\in\mathcal{M}_{0}(R)
, then

(4.119)

\displaystyle R\langle I,M\rangle

\displaystyle=\{xI+yM\colon x,y\in R\}.

Proof.

Statements (
1
), (
2
) are immediate consequences of the definitions. To prove (
3
) let
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&-\alpha\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
M^{\prime}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha^{\prime}&\beta^{\prime}\\
\gamma^{\prime}&-\alpha^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Then

(4.120)

\displaystyle MM^{\prime}-M^{\prime}M

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}(\beta\gamma^{\prime}-\gamma\beta^{\prime})&2(\alpha\beta^{\prime}-\beta\alpha^{\prime})\\
2(\gamma\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha\gamma^{\prime})&-(\beta\gamma^{\prime}-\gamma\beta^{\prime})\end{pmatrix},

from which the statement follows. To prove (
4
), let
\mathcal{A}=\{xI+yM\colon x,y\in R\}
. In view of (
1
),
\mathcal{A}
is closed under addition and multiplication, and is therefore a sub-algebra which contains
I,M
, and is contained in
R\langle I,M\rangle
.
∎

We now define canonical representations of
K
and prove a proposition giving their basic properties. The following definition and proposition would be exactly the same (with minor modifications to the proof) if
K
were replaced by an arbitrary degree
n
number field and
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Q})
were replaced by the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra of
n\times n
matrices (where the
n
is the same on both sides).

Definition 4.30

(canonical representation)
.

A
canonical representation
of
K
is a map
\chi\colon K\to\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Q})
that is a
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra isomorphism of
K
onto a sub-algebra of
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Q})
.

Proposition 4.31
.

Let
\chi:K\to\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Q})
be a canonical representation. Then
\chi
enjoys the following properties.

(1)

The map
\chi
is isomorphic to the “multiplication map” representation
\chi_{0}:K\to\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{Q}}(K)
given by
(\chi_{0}(\kappa))(\kappa^{\prime})=\kappa\kappa^{\prime}
, where
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{Q}}(K)
is the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra of
\mathbb{Q}
-linear maps from
K
to
K
.

(2)

The map
\chi
turns norms into determinants, so that
\Nm(\kappa)=\Det(\chi(\kappa))
for all
\kappa\in K
.

(3)

The map
\chi
preserves traces in the sense that
\Tr(\kappa)
, the number field trace of
\kappa
, is equal to
\Tr(\chi(\kappa))
, the matrix trace of
\chi(\kappa)
, for all
\kappa\in K
.

Proof.

Write
K=\kappa_{1}\mathbb{Q}+\kappa_{2}\mathbb{Q}
. Identify
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{Q}}(K)
with
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Q})
, so that for any
\kappa\in K
, the linear transformation
\chi_{0}(\kappa)
is represented by a matrix
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}m_{11}&m_{12}\\
m_{21}&m_{22}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Q})
such that
\kappa\kappa_{i}=m_{i1}\kappa_{1}+m_{i2}\kappa_{2}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Fix some
v_{0}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Q})
such that
v_{1}:=\chi(\kappa_{1})v_{0}
and
v_{2}:=\chi(\kappa_{2})v_{0}
are linearly independent.
Then, for any
\kappa\in K
,

(4.121)

\chi(\kappa)v_{i}=\chi(\kappa)\chi(\kappa_{i})v_{0}=\chi(\kappa\kappa_{i})v_{0}=\chi(m_{i1}\kappa_{1}+m_{i2}\kappa_{2})v_{0}=m_{i1}v_{1}+m_{i2}v_{2}.

Thus,
\chi(\kappa)
is represented by
M
in the basis
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
of
\mathbb{Q}^{2}
. So
\chi
is isomorphic to
\chi_{0}
as a
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra representation of
K
; that is, property (1) holds.

Property (2) and (3) follow, because the norm
\Nm(\kappa)
is defined to be the determinant of
\chi_{0}(\kappa)
(or, equivalently, the determinant of
M
), and the number field trace
\Tr(\kappa)
is defined to be the trace of
\chi_{0}(\kappa)
(or, equivalently, the trace of
M
).
∎

We will now use the fact that
K
is a quadratic field to parametrize all canonical representations up to equality by (binary quadratic) forms
Q
.

Definition 4.32

(canonical representation associated to a form)
.

Given a form
Q
associated to
K
with conductor
f
, define
\chi_{Q}\colon K\to\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Q})
to be the map specified by

(4.122)

\displaystyle\chi_{Q}\!\left(x+y\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=xI+\frac{2y}{f}SQ

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{Q}
.

Theorem 4.33
.

For every form
Q
associated to
K
, the map
\chi_{Q}
is a canonical representation of
K
. Conversely, if
\chi
is a canonical representation of
K
, then there exists a form
Q
associated to
K
such that
\chi=\chi_{Q}
.

Proof.

Let
Q
be a form associated to
K
with conductor
f
. It is immediate that
\chi_{Q}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear monomorphism of
K
into
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Q})
. To show that it is a
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra monomorphism, observe that it follows from Lemma
4.29
that

(4.123)

\displaystyle(SQ)^{2}

\displaystyle=-\Det(SQ)I=\frac{1}{4}f^{2}\Delta_{0}I.

Hence

\displaystyle\chi_{Q}\!\left(x+y\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}\right)\chi_{Q}\!\left(x^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=xx^{\prime}I+\frac{4yy^{\prime}}{f^{2}}(SQ)^{2}+\frac{2(xy^{\prime}+x^{\prime}y)}{f}SQ

(4.124)

\displaystyle=\chi_{Q}\!\left(\left(x+y\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}\right)\left(x^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}\right)\right)

for all
x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}
.
So
\chi_{Q}
is a canonical representation of
K
.

Conversely, suppose
\chi
is an arbitrary canonical representation of
K
.
Define

(4.125)

\displaystyle L_{1}

\displaystyle=\chi(1),

\displaystyle L_{2}

\displaystyle=\chi(\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}).

We have
\Tr(L_{1})=2
and
L_{1}^{2}=L_{1}
. Consequently
I-L_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
(I-L_{1})^{2}=I-L_{1}
. In view of Lemma
4.29
this means

(4.126)

\displaystyle I-L_{1}

\displaystyle=-\Det(I-L_{1})I.

So
L_{1}=kI
for some
k
. The fact that
\Tr(L_{1})=2
means
k=1
, implying
L_{1}=I
.

Turning to
L_{2}
, the fact that
\Tr(L_{2})=\Tr(\Delta_{0})
implies, by another application of Lemma
4.29
, that

(4.127)

\displaystyle L_{2}

\displaystyle=SM

for some
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\rm{S}}(\mathbb{Q})
. We can write
M
in the form

(4.128)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=qQ,

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}a&\frac{b}{2}\\
\frac{b}{2}&c\end{pmatrix}

with
q\in\mathbb{Q}
and
a,b,c
coprime integers. The fact that
\Det(L_{2})=\Nm(\sqrt{\Delta_{0}})
means

(4.129)

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{2}\Delta}{4}

where
\Delta=b^{2}-4ac
is the discriminant of
Q
.
Let
D,D^{\prime}
be the square-free parts of
\Delta_{0},\Delta
respectively. Then
n^{2}D=m^{2}D^{\prime}
for some
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
, implying
D^{\prime}=D
. So
Q
is associated to
K
, and
\Delta=f^{2}\Delta_{0}
where
f
is the conductor of
Q
. It follows that
q=2/f
implying

(4.130)

\displaystyle L_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{f}SQ.

Hence
\chi=\chi_{Q}
.
∎

Theorem 4.34
.

Let
Q
be a form associated to
K
and let
f
be its conductor. Let
\mathcal{O}_{f}
,
\mathcal{U}_{f}
be the order and unit group with conductor
f
(see Definition
4.11
). Then
\chi_{Q}
restricts to

(1)

a ring isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}_{f}
onto
\mathbb{Z}\langle I,SQ\rangle\cap\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Z})
,

(2)

a group isomorphism of
\mathcal{U}_{f}
onto

\mathbb{Z}\langle I,SQ\rangle\cap\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

Statement (
1
).
Let
\kappa\in\mathcal{O}_{f}
be arbitrary. Then

(4.131)

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle=x+yf\left(\frac{\Delta_{0}+\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right)

for some
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}
. Setting
Q=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&\frac{b}{2}\\
\frac{b}{2}&c\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, we have

(4.132)

\displaystyle\chi_{Q}(\kappa)

\displaystyle=\left(x+\frac{yf\Delta_{0}}{2}\right)I+ySQ=\begin{pmatrix}x+\frac{y\left(f\Delta_{0}-b\right)}{2}&-yc\\
ya&x+\frac{y\left(f\Delta_{0}+b\right)}{2}\end{pmatrix}.

The fact that
f^{2}\Delta_{0}=b^{2}-4ac
means
f\Delta_{0}\equiv b\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
. So
\chi_{Q}(\kappa)\in\langle I,SQ\rangle\cap\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Conversely, suppose
L\in\mathbb{Z}\langle I,SQ\rangle\cap\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Z})
. Then it follows from Lemma
4.29
that

(4.133)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=xI+ySQ

for some
x
,
y\in\mathbb{Q}
. So

(4.134)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\chi_{Q}(\kappa),

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle=\left(x-\frac{yf\Delta_{0}}{2}\right)+yf\left(\frac{\Delta_{0}+\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right).

Moreover, the fact that

(4.135)

\displaystyle L=\begin{pmatrix}x-\frac{yb}{2}&-yc\\
ya&x+\frac{yb}{2}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Z})

means
ya,yc
and
yb=(x+yb/2)-(x-yb/2)
are all in
\mathbb{Z}
. Since
a,b,c
are coprime, it follows that
y\in\mathbb{Z}
. Also the fact that
x-yb/2\in\mathbb{Z}
, together with the fact that
f\Delta_{0}\equiv b\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
, means

(4.136)

\displaystyle x-\frac{yf\Delta_{0}}{2}

\displaystyle=x-\frac{yb}{2}-\frac{y\left(f\Delta_{0}-b\right)}{2}\in\mathbb{Z}.

So
\kappa\in\mathcal{O}_{f}
.

Statement (
2
).
It follows from Statement (
1
) that
\chi_{Q}
maps
\mathcal{U}_{f}
into a multiplicative subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}\langle I,SQ\rangle\cap\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Z})
. The fact that
\Det(\chi_{Q}(\kappa))=\Nm(\kappa)
for all
\kappa
means that, if
\kappa\in\mathcal{U}_{f}
, then
\Det(\chi_{Q}(\kappa))=\pm 1
implying
\chi_{Q}(\kappa)\in\mathbb{Z}\langle I,SQ\rangle\cap\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. It also means that, if
\chi_{Q}(\kappa)\in\mathbb{Z}\langle I,SQ\rangle\cap\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, then
\Nm(\kappa)=\pm 1
.
Since Statement (
1
) implies
\kappa\in\mathcal{O}_{f}
we must have
\kappa\in\mathcal{U}_{f}
.
∎

Corollary 4.35
.

Let
Q
be a form associated to
K
, and let
f
be its conductor. Let
\kappa\in\mathcal{O}_{f}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
be arbitrary. Then
\kappa\in n\mathcal{O}_{f}
if and only if
\chi_{Q}(\kappa)\in n\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

Necessity is immediate. Suppose, on the other hand, that
\chi_{Q}(\kappa)=nL
for
L\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Z})
. It follows from Theorem
4.34
that
nL\in\mathbb{Z}\langle I,SQ\rangle
. So
L
is also in
\mathbb{Z}\langle I,Q\rangle
. By another application of Theorem
4.34
,
L=\chi_{Q}(a)
for some
a\in\mathcal{O}_{f}
. Hence
\kappa=na\in n\mathcal{O}_{f}
.
∎

4.5.
Stability groups and maximal abelian subgroups of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})

The purpose of this subsection is to prove some results concerning the stability group of a form, which, as we will see, is the same thing as a maximal abelian subgroup of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

In order to treat arbitrary maximal abelian subgroups of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
we need to temporarily relax the restrictions in
Section

1.3
on the definition of
form
.
We continue to assume without comment that the forms with which we deal are binary, quadratic, integral, and primitive.
However, in this subsection (and nowhere else) we drop the assumption that they are irreducible and indefinite.

We now investigate the properties of
\mathcal{S}(Q)
, the stability group of
Q
as specified in Definition
1.20
.

Lemma 4.36
.

For all
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,

(4.137)

\displaystyle\Det(M)M^{\rm{T}}

\displaystyle=-SM^{-1}S.

Proof.

Write
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Then

(4.138)

\displaystyle SM^{-1}S

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Det M}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\delta&-\beta\\
-\gamma&\alpha\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}=-\frac{1}{\Det M}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\gamma\\
\beta&\delta\end{pmatrix},

as desired.
∎

Theorem 4.37
.

Let
Q
be a form and
\mathcal{S}(Q)
its stability group (as in
Definition

1.20
). Then:

(1)

\mathcal{S}(Q)
is the centralizer of
SQ
in
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(2)

\mathcal{S}(Q)=\mathbb{Z}\langle I,SQ\rangle\cap\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(3)

For all
M\in\mathcal{S}(Q)
, there exist unique integers
t,n
such that

(4.139)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\frac{t}{2}I+nSQ.

(4)

\mathcal{S}(Q)
is abelian.

Proof.

Statement (
1
).

For all
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,

(4.140)

\displaystyle\qquad Q_{M}

\displaystyle=Q

(4.141)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\qquad\Det(M)M^{\rm{T}}QM

\displaystyle=Q

(4.142)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\qquad-SM^{-1}SQM

\displaystyle=Q

(4.143)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\qquad SQM

\displaystyle=MSQ,

where
Q_{M}
is as defined in (
1.31
) and where we used Lemma
4.36
in the second step.

Statement (
2
).
Let
M
be any element of the centralizer of
SQ
, and let

(4.144)

\displaystyle M_{0}

\displaystyle=M-\frac{1}{2}\Tr(M)I.

Then
M_{0}
commutes with
SQ
. Since
M_{0}
and
SQ
are both trace-zero, it follows from Lemma
4.29
that

(4.145)

\displaystyle M_{0}

\displaystyle=\lambda SQ

for some non-zero
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}
. So
M
belongs to
\mathbb{Z}\langle I,SQ\rangle
and therefore to
\mathbb{Z}\langle I,SQ\rangle\cap\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Conversely, if
M\in\mathbb{Z}\langle I,SQ\rangle\cap\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
then it follows from Lemma
4.29
that
M
is a linear combination of
I
and
SQ
and is therefore in the centralizer of
SQ
.

Statement (
3
).
Let
Q=\langle a,b,c\rangle
, and let
M\in\mathcal{S}(Q)
. It follows from Statement (
2
) that

(4.146)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{t-nb}{2}&-nc\\
na&\frac{t+nb}{2}\end{pmatrix}

for some
t,n\in\mathbb{Q}
. We must have
t,na,nb,nc\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
a,b,c
are coprime, it follows that
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Suppose that
t^{\prime},n^{\prime}
are any other pair of integers such that
M=(t^{\prime}/2)I+n^{\prime}SQ
. Then

(4.147)

\displaystyle t^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Tr(M)=t

and, consequently,
n^{\prime}Q=nQ
, implying
n^{\prime}=n
.

Statement (
4
).
Immediate consequence of Statement (
2
).
∎

Theorem 4.38
.

For each
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{\pm I\}
there exists a unique form
Q(M)
such that
M\in\mathcal{S}(Q(M))
and
\sgn\left(M\right)=\sgn\left(Q(M)\right)
. Explicitly,

(4.148)

\displaystyle Q(M)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{M}}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma&\frac{\delta-\alpha}{2}\\
\frac{\delta-\alpha}{2}&-\beta\end{pmatrix}

where

(4.149)

\displaystyle n_{M}

\displaystyle=\gcd(\beta,\gamma,\alpha-\delta).

Setting

(4.150)

\displaystyle t_{M}

\displaystyle=\Tr(M),

one then has

(4.151)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{M}}{2}I+n_{M}SQ(M).

Remark
.

When reading
Theorem

4.38
, one should keep in mind that, in this subsection, forms are not assumed to be irreducible or indefinite.

Proof.

Let
Q(M),n_{M},t_{M}
be as defined in (
4.148
)–(
4.150
). The fact that
M\neq\pm I
means
n_{M}
is non-zero, and
Q(M)
is a well-defined form. We have
\sgn(M)=\sgn(\gamma)=\sgn\left(Q(M)\right)
. Moreover

(4.152)

\displaystyle\frac{t_{M}}{2}I+n_{M}SQ(M)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha+\delta}{2}I+\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma&\frac{\delta-\alpha}{2}\\
\frac{\delta-\alpha}{2}&-\beta\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}.

In view of Theorem
4.37
, this means
M\in\mathcal{S}\left(Q(M)\right)
.

Suppose
Q
is any other form such that
M\in\mathcal{S}(Q)
and
\sgn(Q)=\sgn(M)
. In view of Theorem
4.37
, this means

(4.153)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\frac{t}{2}I+nSQ

for some pair of integers
t,n
such that
n>0
.
It is immediate that
t=\Tr(M)=t_{M}
. Hence

(4.154)

\displaystyle nQ

\displaystyle=n_{M}Q(M).

Consequently

(4.155)

\displaystyle na

\displaystyle=n_{M}a_{M}

\displaystyle nb

\displaystyle=n_{M}b_{M}

\displaystyle nc

\displaystyle=n_{M}c_{M}

where we have set
Q(M)=\langle a_{M},b_{M},c_{M}\rangle
,
Q=\langle a,b,c\rangle
.
Since
Q
,
Q(M)
are primitive and
n
,
n_{M}
are both positive, it follows that
Q=Q(M)
.
∎

Corollary 4.39
.

For any pair of distinct forms
Q_{1},Q_{2}
,

(4.156)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(Q_{1})\cap\mathcal{S}(Q_{2})

\displaystyle=\{\pm I\}.

Proof.

Immediate consequence of Theorem
4.38
and the fact that
\pm I\in\mathcal{S}(Q)
for all
Q
.
∎

Lemma 4.40
.

Let
M
,
M^{\prime}
be any pair of elements of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{\pm I\}
. Then
M
commutes with
M^{\prime}
if and only if
Q(M)=Q(M^{\prime})
, where
Q(M),Q(M^{\prime})
are as defined in (
4.148
).

Proof.

Suppose
M
,
M^{\prime}
commute. Then
SQ(M^{\prime})=n^{-1}_{M^{\prime}}\left(M^{\prime}-(t_{M^{\prime}}/2)I\right)
commutes with
SQ(M)=n^{-1}_{M}\left(M-(t_{M}/2)I\right)
. In view of Lemma
4.29
, and the fact that
SQ(M^{\prime})
,
SQ(M)
are both trace-zero, this means

(4.157)

\displaystyle SQ(M^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\lambda SQ(M)

for some non-zero
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}
, implying

(4.158)

\displaystyle m^{\prime}Q(M^{\prime})

\displaystyle=mQ(M)

for some non-zero
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Because the forms are primitive, it follows that
Q(M^{\prime})=Q(M)
.

The converse is immediate.
∎

Definition 4.41
.

Let

(1)

\mathcal{H}_{+}
be the set of all
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{\pm I\}
such that
\left(\Tr M\right)^{2}-4\Det M>4
,

(2)

\mathcal{H}_{-}
be the set of all
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\left(\Tr M\right)^{2}-4\Det M\leq 4
,

and let

(1)

\mathcal{F}_{+}
be the set of all forms which are indefinite and irreducible,

(2)

\mathcal{F}_{-}
be the set of all forms whose discriminant equals
-4,-3,0,1,
or
4
.

Theorem 4.42
.

M\in\mathcal{H}_{\pm}
if and only if
Q(M)\in\mathcal{F}_{\pm}
.

Proof.

Write
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Then

(4.159)

\displaystyle\left(\Tr M\right)^{2}-4\Det M

\displaystyle=(\alpha+\delta)^{2}-4\alpha\delta+4\beta\gamma=(\alpha-\delta)^{2}+4\beta\gamma=n_{M}^{2}\Delta\big(Q(M)\big).

Now suppose
M\in\mathcal{H}_{+}
. Then (
4.159
) means
n_{M}^{2}\Delta(Q(M))>4
. It follows that
Q(M)
is indefinite. It also follows that
Q(M)
is irreducible. Indeed, suppose that were not the case. Then
\Delta(Q(M))=m^{2}
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
, implying

(4.160)

\displaystyle\bigl|\left|\Tr M\right|-mn_{M}\bigr|\big(\left|\Tr M\right|+mn_{M}\big)

\displaystyle=4

Since
\left|\Tr M\right|-mn_{M}\equiv\left|\Tr M\right|+mn_{M}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
, this is only possible if

(4.161)

\displaystyle\bigl|\left|\Tr M\right|-mn_{M}\bigr|=\left|\Tr M\right|+mn_{M}=2.

Since
mn_{M}>0
, this in turn implies
\Tr M=0
, which is inconsistent with the assumption
M\in\mathcal{H}_{+}
. Conversely, if
Q(M)\in\mathcal{F}_{+}
, then
\Delta(Q(M))>4
, implying
\left(\Tr M\right)^{2}-4\Det M>4
.

Suppose, on the other hand, that
M\in\mathcal{H}_{-}
. Then it follows from (
4.159
) that

(4.162)

\displaystyle n_{M}^{2}\Delta(Q(M))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-4\quad&\text{if }\Tr M=0,\,\Det M=+1,\\
-3\quad&\text{if }\Tr M=\pm 1,\,\Det M=+1,\\
0\quad&\text{if }\Tr M=\pm 2,\,\Det M=+1,\\
4\quad&\text{if }\Tr M=0,\,\Det M=-1.\end{cases}

Since a discriminant must be
0
or
1
modulo
4
, it follows that

\Delta(Q(M))=-4,-3,0,1,
or
4
. Conversely, if
Q(M)\in\mathcal{F}_{-}
, then it follows from the first part of the proof that
M
cannot belong
\mathcal{H}_{+}
, and so
M
must belong to
\mathcal{H}_{-}
.
∎

Theorem 4.43
.

Let
Q
be any form.

(1)

If
Q\notin\mathcal{F}_{+}\cup\mathcal{F}_{-}
, then

(4.163)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(Q)

\displaystyle=\{\pm I\}.

(2)

If
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{+}
, then

(4.164)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(Q)

\displaystyle=\chi_{Q}\left(\mathcal{U}_{Q}\right)\cong\left(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\right)\times\mathbb{Z}.

(3)

If
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{-}
, then

(4.165)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(Q)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\langle SQ\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}\qquad&\text{if $\Delta(Q)=-4$},\\
\left<\frac{1}{2}I+SQ\right>\cong\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}\qquad&\text{if $\Delta(Q)=-3$},\\
\langle-I,I+SQ\rangle\cong\left(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\right)\times\mathbb{Z}\qquad&\text{if $\Delta(Q)=0$},\\
\langle-I,2SQ\rangle\cong\left(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\right)\times\left(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\right)\qquad&\text{if $\Delta(Q)=1$},\\
\langle-I,SQ\rangle\cong\left(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\right)\times\left(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\right)\qquad&\text{if $\Delta(Q)=4$}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Suppose
Q\notin\mathcal{F}_{+}\cup\mathcal{F}_{-}
, and let
M
be any element of
\mathcal{S}(Q)
. To show that
M=\pm I
, assume the contrary. It would then follow from Theorem
4.38
that
Q=Q(M)
, which is a contradiction since we know from Theorem
4.42
that
Q(M)\in\mathcal{F}_{+}\cup\mathcal{F}_{-}
.

Suppose
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{+}
. Then it follows from Theorems
4.34
and
4.37
that

(4.166)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(Q)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Z}\langle I,SQ\rangle\cap\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\chi_{Q}\left(\mathcal{U}_{Q}\right)\cong\left(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\right)\times\mathbb{Z}.

Suppose
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{-}
, and let
\pm M\in\mathcal{S}(Q)
. Write
Q=\langle a,b,c\rangle
. It follows from Theorem
4.37
that there exist unique integers
t,n
such that

(4.167)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\frac{t}{2}I+nSQ=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{t-nb}{2}&-nc\\
na&\frac{t+nb}{2}\end{pmatrix},

implying

(4.168)

\displaystyle t^{2}-n^{2}\Delta(Q)

\displaystyle=4\Det M.

If
\Delta(Q)=0
, then it follows from (
4.168
) that
\Det M=+1
and
t=\pm 2
. It follows from Lemma
4.29
that
(SQ)^{2}=0
.
Hence

(4.169)

\displaystyle\left(I+SQ\right)^{n}

\displaystyle=I+nSQ

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Multiplying by
-I
gives the elements with negative trace. We conclude that
I+SQ
is infinite order and
\mathcal{S}(Q)=\langle-I,I+SQ\rangle
.

If
\Delta(Q)=-3
, then it follows from (
4.168
) that
\Det M=+1
and that either
M=\pm I
or
\left|t\right|=\left|n\right|=1
, implying
\mathcal{S}(Q)
is order 6. It follows from Lemma
4.29
that
(SQ)^{2}=-3/4I
.
Putting these facts together, we deduce that
(1/2)I+SQ
is order
6
and consequently that
\mathcal{S}(Q)=\langle(1/2)I+SQ\rangle
.

If
\Delta(Q)=-4
, then it follows from (
4.168
) that
\Det M=+1
and that either
M=\pm I
or
t=0,n=\pm 1
, implying
\mathcal{S}(Q)
is order 4. It follows from Lemma
4.29
that
(SQ)^{2}=-I
. Putting these facts together, we deduce that
SQ
is order
4
and consequently that
\mathcal{S}(Q)=\langle SQ\rangle
.

If
\Delta(Q)=1
(respectively
\Delta(Q)=4
), then it follows from (
4.168
) that either
M=\pm I
or
\Det M=-1
,
t=0
and
n=\pm 2
(respectively
n=\pm 1
), implying
\mathcal{S}(Q)
is order
4
. It follows from Lemma
4.29
that
(SQ)^{2}=(1/4)I
(respectively
(SQ)^{2}=I
). Putting these facts together, we deduce that
2SQ
(respectively
SQ
) is order
2
and consequently that
\mathcal{S}(Q)=\langle-I,2SQ\rangle
(respectively
\mathcal{S}(Q)=\langle-I,SQ\rangle
).
∎

Theorem 4.44
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be any subgroup of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Then
\mathcal{G}
is maximal abelian if and only if
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{S}(Q)
for some
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{+}\cup\mathcal{F}_{-}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a maximal abelian subgroup of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. The fact that
\{\pm I\}
is the centre of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
means that
\{\pm I\}
is properly contained in
\mathcal{G}
. Choose
M\in\mathcal{G}\setminus\{\pm I\}
, and set
Q=Q(M)
. It follows from Theorem
4.42
that
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{+}\cup\mathcal{F}_{-}
. If
M^{\prime}
is any other element of
\mathcal{G}\setminus\{\pm I\}
, then it follows from Lemma
4.40
that
Q_{M^{\prime}}=Q
. So
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(Q)
. Since
\mathcal{S}(Q)
is abelian, it follows that
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{S}(Q)
.

Conversely, let
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{+}\cup\mathcal{F}_{-}
. Then
\mathcal{S}(Q)
is an abelian group. It follows from Theorem
4.43
that we can choose
M\in\mathcal{S}(Q)
such that
M\neq\pm I
. It then follows from Lemma
4.40
that if
M^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{S}(Q)
, then
M^{\prime}
does not commute with
M
. So
\mathcal{S}(Q)
is maximal abelian.
∎

Theorem 4.45
.

Let
M\in\mathcal{H}_{+}
and let

(4.170)

\displaystyle w_{M}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Tr(M)+\sqrt{\left(\Tr M\right)^{2}-4\Det M}}{2}.

Then
w_{M}
is a unit in
\mathcal{U}_{Q(M)}
and

(4.171)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\chi_{Q(M)}(w_{M}).

Proof.

Write
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Then we have from Theorem
4.38
the formula

(4.172)

\displaystyle Q(M)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{M}}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma&\frac{\delta-\alpha}{2}\\
\frac{\delta-\alpha}{2}&-\beta\end{pmatrix},

implying

(4.173)

\displaystyle\Delta(Q(M))

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\Tr M\right)^{2}-4\Det M}{n_{M}^{2}}.

Let
\Delta_{0}
and
f
be the fundamental discriminant and conductor of
Q
. Then it follows that

(4.174)

\displaystyle w_{M}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Tr M-fn_{M}\Delta_{0}}{2}+fn_{M}\left(\frac{\Delta_{0}+\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right).

The fact that
f^{2}n^{2}_{M}\Delta_{0}=\left(\Tr M\right)^{2}-4\Det M
means
\left(\Tr M-fn_{M}\Delta_{0}\right)/2\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
\Nm(w_{M})=\Det M
, it follows that
w_{M}
is a unit in
\mathcal{U}_{Q(M)}
. The fact that
\chi_{Q(M)}(w_{M})=M
is immediate.
∎

4.6.
Additional results

We now revert to the convention explained in
Section

1.3
, according to which a form is always understood to be irreducible and indefinite unless the contrary is explicitly stated.

Lemma 4.46
.

Let
Q
,
Q^{\prime}
be any pair of forms. Then

(4.175)

\displaystyle\rho_{Q,\pm}=\rho_{Q^{\prime},\pm}\,\iff\,Q=Q^{\prime}

Proof.

Sufficiency is immediate. To prove necessity
write
Q=\langle a,b,c\rangle
,
Q^{\prime}=\langle a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}\rangle
. Then

\rho_{Q,\pm}=\rho_{Q^{\prime},\pm}
implies

(4.176)

\displaystyle\frac{b}{a}

\displaystyle=-(\rho_{Q,+}+\rho_{Q,-})=-(\rho_{Q^{\prime},+}+\rho_{Q^{\prime},-})=\frac{b^{\prime}}{a^{\prime}},

(4.177)

\displaystyle\frac{c}{a}

\displaystyle=\rho_{Q,+}\rho_{Q,-}=\rho_{Q^{\prime},+}\rho_{Q^{\prime},-}=\frac{c^{\prime}}{a^{\prime}}.

Also

(4.178)

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{b^{2}-4ac}}{a}

\displaystyle=\rho_{Q,+}-\rho_{Q,-}=\rho_{Q^{\prime},+}-\rho_{Q^{\prime},-}=\frac{\sqrt{b^{\prime 2}-4a^{\prime}c^{\prime}}}{a^{\prime}},

implying
\sgn(a)=\sgn(a^{\prime})
. It follows that

(4.179)

\displaystyle nQ

\displaystyle=n^{\prime}Q^{\prime}

for some pair of positive integers
n
,
n^{\prime}
. Since
Q
,
Q^{\prime}
are primitive, we must in fact have
n=n^{\prime}=1
and
Q=Q^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 4.47
.

Let
Q
be an arbitrary form and let
M\in\mathcal{S}(Q)
be such that
M\neq\pm I
. Then for all
\tau\in\mathbb{C}

(4.180)

\displaystyle M\cdot\tau=\tau\,\iff\,\tau=\rho_{Q,\pm}.

Remark
.

It can be shown that, if
M
and
Q
have the same (resp. opposite) sign, then
\rho_{Q,+}
is the attractive (resp. repulsive) fixed point and
\rho_{Q,-}
is the repulsive (resp. attractive) fixed point of
M
. We omit the proof, since the result is not actually needed for the purposes of this paper.

Proof.

Straightforward consequence of the definitions.
∎

Lemma 4.48
.

For any form
Q
and matrix
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})

(4.181)

\displaystyle M^{-1}\cdot\rho_{Q,\pm}

\displaystyle=\rho_{Q_{M},\pm}.

In particular

(4.182)

\displaystyle M\cdot\rho_{Q,\pm}=\rho_{Q,\pm}\,\iff\,M\in\mathcal{S}(Q).

Proof.

Write
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
Q=\langle a,b,c\rangle
,
Q_{M}=\langle a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}\rangle
.
Then

(4.183)

\displaystyle a^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\Det M)\left(a\alpha^{2}+b\alpha\gamma+c\gamma^{2}\right),

(4.184)

\displaystyle b^{\prime}

\displaystyle=2(\Det M)\left(a\alpha\beta+b\left(\frac{\alpha\delta+\beta\gamma}{2}\right)+c\gamma\delta\right),

(4.185)

\displaystyle c^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\Det M)\left(a\beta^{2}+b\beta\delta+c\delta^{2}\right).

Hence

(4.186)

\displaystyle b^{\prime 2}-4a^{\prime}c^{\prime}

\displaystyle=b^{2}-4ac

and

(4.187)

\displaystyle M^{-1}\cdot\rho_{Q,\pm}

\displaystyle=\frac{-(2a\beta+b\delta)\pm\delta\sqrt{b^{2}-4ac}}{(2a\alpha+b\gamma)\mp\gamma\sqrt{b^{2}-4ac}}=\frac{-b^{\prime}\pm\sqrt{b^{2}-4ac}}{2a^{\prime}}=\rho_{Q_{M},\pm},

which is (
4.181
). Equation (
4.182
) follows immediately.
∎

Definition 4.49

(Level of an admissible tuple)
.

We define the
level
of the admissible tuple
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
to be the integer

(4.188)

\displaystyle n_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{j}{j_{\rm{min}}(f)},

where
f
is the conductor of
Q
.

Theorem 4.50
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
be an admissible tuple, and let
f
be the conductor of
Q
. Then the stabilizers
L_{t}
,
L_{+,t}
,
A_{t}
(see
Definition

1.28
) are given by

(4.189)

\displaystyle L_{t}

\displaystyle=\chi_{Q}(\varphi_{f}),

(4.190)

\displaystyle L_{+,t}

\displaystyle=\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon_{f}),

(4.191)

\displaystyle L_{\rm{z},t}

\displaystyle=L_{+,t}^{n_{t}}=\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon^{j}),

(4.192)

\displaystyle A_{t}

\displaystyle=L_{+,t}^{n_{t}(2m+1)}=\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon^{j(2m+1)}).

The stability groups
\mathcal{S}(Q)
,
\mathcal{S}_{d}(Q)
(see
Definition

1.20
) are then given by

(4.193)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(Q)

\displaystyle=\langle-I,L_{t}\rangle,

(4.194)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{d}(Q)

\displaystyle=\langle A_{t}\rangle.

In particular,
\mathcal{S}_{d}(Q)
is a cyclic group, and every element has positive determinant and trace. Moreover, if
d
is even, then

(4.195)

\displaystyle A_{t}

\displaystyle\equiv\left(d+1\right)I\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d\right).

Remark
.

The fact that
\mathcal{S}_{d}(Q)
contains no negative determinant matrices might at first sight seem to be an artifact of our decision to define
\Gamma(d)
to be a subgroup of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. However, it is easily seen that, if
d>2
, then there are no matrices in
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
but outside
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
that are congruent to
I
modulo
d
. Indeed, suppose
L=I+d\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)
were such a matrix. Then we would have
-1=\det L=1+d(\alpha+\delta+d(\alpha\delta-\beta\gamma))
implying
d\!\mid\!2
).

Proof.

To prove (
4.189
) and (
4.193
), observe that it follows from
Theorem

4.43
and
Corollary

4.17
that

(4.196)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(Q)

\displaystyle=\chi_{Q}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{Q}\right)=\langle-I,\chi_{Q}(\varphi_{f})\rangle.

Consequently,
L_{t}
must be one of the four matrices
\pm\chi_{Q}(\varphi_{f})
,
\pm\chi_{Q}(\varphi^{-1}_{f})
. The requirement that
\Tr(L_{t})
is positive and
\sgn(L_{t})=\sgn(Q)
means we must in fact have
L_{t}=\chi_{Q}(\varphi_{f})
.

If
\Nm(\varphi_{f})=1
, then
\varepsilon_{f}=\varphi_{f}
and
\Det(L_{t})=+1
, implying
L_{+,t}=L_{t}=\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon_{f})
. If, on the other hand,
\Nm(\varphi_{f})=-1
, then
\varepsilon_{f}=\varphi_{f}^{2}
and
\Det(L_{t})=-1
, implying
L_{+,t}=L^{2}_{t}=\chi_{Q}(\varphi^{2}_{f})
, so that we again have
L_{+,t}=\chi(\varepsilon_{f})
.

Equation

4.191
is an immediate consequence of the definition of
L_{\rm{z},t}
.

Now consider (
4.192
) and (
4.194
). The fact that
\mathcal{S}_{d}(Q)
is a subgroup of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
means every element is of the form
\pm L_{+,t}^{l}
, where
l\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
q
be the multiplicative order of
L_{+,t}
modulo
d
. We claim that
q=(2m+1)n
. Indeed, Lemma
4.23
implies

(4.197)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{f}^{(2m+1)n}-1

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{(2m+1)j}-1=zd

where
n=j/j_{\rm{min}}(f)
and
z=\varepsilon^{mj}(\varepsilon^{j}-1)=\varepsilon_{f}^{mn}(\varepsilon_{f}^{n}-1)\in\mathcal{O}_{f}
. It then follows from Corollary
4.35
that
L_{+,t}^{(2m+1)n}\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
. So
q\!\mid\!(2m+1)n
. To show that in fact
q=(2m+1)n
assume the contrary. Then
q\leq(2m+1)n/2
. The fact that
L_{+,t}^{q}\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
means, in view of Corollary
4.35
, that

(4.198)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{f}^{q}-1

\displaystyle=z^{\prime}d

for some
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{f}
.
It follows from Lemma
4.23

(4.199)

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon_{f}^{(2m+1)n}-1}{\varepsilon_{f}^{mn}(\varepsilon_{f}^{n}-1)}.

So

(4.200)

\displaystyle z^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon_{f}^{mn}\left(\varepsilon_{f}^{n}-1\right)\left(\varepsilon_{f}^{q}-1\right)}{\varepsilon_{f}^{(2m+1)n}-1}.

Taking norms on both sides, we deduce

(4.201)

\displaystyle\Nm(z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(2-\varepsilon_{f}^{n}-\varepsilon_{f}^{-n}\right)\left(2-\varepsilon_{f}^{q}-\varepsilon_{f}^{-q}\right)}{2-\varepsilon_{f}^{(2m+1)n}-\varepsilon_{f}^{-(2m+1)n}}.

Setting
\varepsilon_{f}^{n}=e^{\theta}
this becomes

(4.202)

\displaystyle\Nm(z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\sinh^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}\sinh^{2}\frac{q\theta}{2n}}{\sinh^{2}\frac{(2m+1)\theta}{2}}

In view of the assumption that
q\leq(2m+1)n/2
, this means

(4.203)

\displaystyle\left|\Nm(z^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\sinh^{4}\frac{(2m+1)\theta}{4}}{\sinh^{2}\frac{(2m+1)\theta}{2}}=\tanh^{2}\frac{(2m+1)\theta}{4}<1,

contradicting the fact that
\left|\Nm(z^{\prime})\right|
is a positive integer.

We have thus shown that
\langle L_{+,t}^{(2m+1)n}\rangle\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{d}(Q)
. To show that in fact

\langle L_{+,t}^{(2m+1)n}\rangle=\mathcal{S}_{d}(Q)
, assume the contrary. Then there would exist a positive integer
s
such that

(4.204)

\displaystyle L_{+,t}^{s}

\displaystyle\equiv-I\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)

It would follow that
L_{+,t}^{2s}\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
, implying
2s
is a multiple of
(2m+1)n
. If it were an even multiple, it would follow that
s
is a multiple of
(2m+1)n
, implying
L_{+,t}^{s}\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
, contrary the assumption. So
2s=(2k+1)(2m+1)n
for some non-negative integer
k
. In particular
n
would be even. We would then have

(4.205)

\displaystyle L_{+,t}^{\frac{(2m+1)n}{2}}

\displaystyle=L_{+,t}^{k(2m+1)n}L_{+,t}^{\frac{(2m+1)n}{2}}=L_{+,t}^{\frac{(2k+1)(2m+1)n}{2}}=L_{+,t}^{s}=-I\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right).

In view of Corollary
4.35
, this would mean

(4.206)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{f}^{\frac{(2m+1)n}{2}}+1

\displaystyle=z^{\prime\prime}d

for some
z^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{f}
.
By a suitably modified version of the argument leading to inequality (
4.203
), it would follow that

(4.207)

\displaystyle\left|\Nm(z^{\prime\prime})\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}}{\sinh^{2}\frac{(2m+1)\theta}{4}}<1,

contradicting the fact that
\Nm(z^{\prime\prime})
is a non-zero integer.
We have thus shown

\mathcal{S}_{d}(Q)=\langle L_{+,t}^{(2m+1)n}\rangle
, implying
A_{t}=L_{+,t}^{\pm(2m+1)n}
. The requirement that
\sgn(A_{t})=\sgn(Q)
means we must in fact have
A_{t}=L_{+,t}^{(2m+1)n}
. (
4.192
), (
4.194
) now follow.

The fact that the elements of
\mathcal{S}_{d}(Q)
all have positive determinant follows from

(4.208)

\displaystyle\Det\left(L_{+,t}^{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\Nm(\varepsilon_{f}^{k})=1.

The fact that they all have positive trace follows from

(4.209)

\displaystyle\Tr\left(L_{+,t}^{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\Tr(\varepsilon_{f}^{k})=\begin{cases}d_{|k|j_{\rm{min}(f)}}-1\quad&\text{ if}k\neq 0,\\
2\quad&\text{ if}k=0.\end{cases}

It remains to prove (
4.195
). The fact that
A_{t}=\chi_{Q}\left(\varepsilon_{f}^{(2m+1)n}\right)
together with Corollary
4.35
means

(4.210)

\displaystyle A_{t}-(d+1)I

\displaystyle\equiv 0\ \,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d\right)

if and only if

(4.211)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon_{f}^{(2m+1)n}-d-1}{2d}\in\mathcal{O}_{f}.

The fact that
d
is even means, in view of Lemmas
4.10
and
4.23
, that
\Delta_{0}\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
. So we need to show

(4.212)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=n_{1}+n_{2}f\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right)

for some
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
. It follows from Lemma
4.23
that

(4.213)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{mj}(\varepsilon^{j}-1)-1}{2}=\frac{d_{(m+1)j}-d_{mj}-2+(f_{(m+1)j}-f_{mj})\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{4}.

That is,

(4.214)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}f\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right)

where

(4.215)

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(d_{(m+1)j}-d_{mj}-2\right)-\left(f_{(m+1)j}-f_{mj}\right)}{4},

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{(m+1)j}-f_{mj}}{2f}.

We need to show that
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
are both in
\mathbb{Z}
. The fact that
\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
is an immediate consequence of Lemma
4.23
. Moreover,

(4.216)

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{f_{j}}{f}\right)\left(\frac{r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m}}{2}\right).

It follows from Lemma
4.23
that
r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m}
is even. Since
f\!\mid\!f_{j}
, this means
\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.31
.

It follows from
Theorem

4.50
that
\Tr(A^{\vphantom{-1}}_{t})
and
\Tr(A^{-1}_{t})
are both positive which, in view of Lemmas
2.18
and
4.48
, means
\rho_{Q,\pm}\in\mathcal{D}_{A^{\vphantom{-1}}_{t}}\cap\mathcal{D}_{A^{-1}_{t}}
.
∎

5.
Proof of main theorems (1): Existence

In this section, we show that the correctness of our construction of
r
-SICs follows from several number-theoretic conjectures. Specifically, we prove
Theorem

1.45
, which asserts that for every fiducial datum
s
, the matrix
\tilde{\Pi}_{s}
defined in
Definition

1.43
is a ghost
r
-SIC fiducial under the assumption of the Twisted Convolution Conjecture (
Conjecture

1.35
). Furthermore, we show
Theorem

1.46
, which asserts that the matrix
\Pi_{s}
defined in
Definition

1.43
is an
r
-SICs fiducial under the assumption of the Twisted Convolution Conjecture and the Stark Conjecture (
Conjecture

2.7
).

This section first establishes the relationship of the Shintani–Faddeev modular cocycle
\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}
to the Shintani–Faddeev phase
\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
, then uses that relationship in conjunction with the conjectures to prove the theorems on ghost
r
-SIC and “live”
r
-SIC existence.

Section

5.1
and
Section

5.2
proves some properties of the
\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
, in order to connect it to the eta-multiplier character
\psi
and the theta-multiplier character
\chi_{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}
. In
Section

5.3
, we use the properties of the SF phase to establish properties of our candidate ghost overlaps, including the crucial property that they are real numbers. The latter relies of results of
[
72
]
relating the SF cocycle to zeta values. In
Section

5.4
, we complete the proof of
Theorem

1.45
. In
Section

5.5
, we give some remarks on the “shift” appearing in our construction. Finally, in
Section

5.6
, we complete the proof of
Theorem

1.46
.

5.1.
Properties of the Rademacher invariant

For the convenience of the reader the following proposition pulls together some properties of the Rademacher class invariant that we require. Aside from a couple of minor additions they are all well-known.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\Psi
be the Rademacher class invariant as defined in
Definition

1.29
, and let
\eta(\tau)
be the Dedekind
\eta
-function.
Then for all
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
N\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\tau\in\mathbb{H}
we have

(5.1)

\displaystyle\Psi(-M)

\displaystyle=\Psi(M),

(5.2)

\displaystyle\Psi(M^{-1})

\displaystyle=-\Psi(M),

(5.3)

\displaystyle\Psi(NMN^{-1})

\displaystyle=(\Det N)\Psi(M)

(5.4)

\displaystyle\eta(M\cdot\tau)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}e^{\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(M)}\sqrt{\sgn(\Tr(M))j_{M}(\tau)}\eta(\tau)\qquad&\gamma\neq 0,\,\Tr(M)\neq 0\,,\\
e^{\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(M)}\sqrt{-i\sgn(\gamma)j_{M}(\tau)}\eta(\tau)\qquad&\gamma\neq 0,\,\Tr(M)=0\,,\\
e^{\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(M)}\eta(\tau)\qquad&\gamma=0\,.\end{cases}

where in (
5.4
) the principal branch of the square root is taken.
If
\Tr(M)\neq\pm 1
, then we also have

(5.5)

\displaystyle\psi(M^{n})

\displaystyle=n\psi(M)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

(
5.1
), (
5.2
) are proved in
[
86
, Satz 7]

and
[
88
, Chapter 4, Section C]
, as is (
5.3
) if
\det N=1
. To show that (
5.3
) also holds if
\det N=-1
, observe that it follows from (
1.51
) that
\Psi(JMJ^{-1})=-\Psi(M)
, where
J=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
If
N
is any other element of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\Det(N)=-1
, let
N^{\prime}=NJ
. Then applying (
5.3
) to
N^{\prime}
we find
\Psi(NMN^{-1})=\Psi(N^{\prime}JMJ^{-1}N^{\prime-1})=\Psi(JMJ^{-1})=-\Psi(M)
.
Equation

5.4
is a straightforward consequence of results proved in

[
87
, Chapter 9]
.
Finally, if
|\Tr(M)|>1
then (
5.5
) is proved in
[
86
, Satz 9]
. It remains to show that it also holds if
\Tr(M)=0
. To see this observe that if
\Tr(M)=0
then

M^{2}=-I
, implying

(5.6)

\displaystyle M^{n}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(-1)^{\frac{n}{2}}I\qquad&\text{$n$ even,}\\
(-1)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}M\qquad&\text{$n$ odd.}\end{cases}

In particular
M^{-1}=-M
. In view of (
5.1
) (
5.2
) this means
\Psi(M)=-\Psi(-M^{-1})=-\Psi(M)
, implying
\Psi(M)=0
. It is immediate that
\Psi(I)=0
. The result now follows.
∎

We now explicitly relate the metaplectic character
\psi
to the Rademacher invariant
\Psi
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\gamma\neq 0
and
\Tr(M)>0
.
Then, taking
\sqrt{j_{M}(\tau)}
to be the principal branch,

(5.7)

\psi(M,\sqrt{j_{M}})=e^{\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(M)}.

Proof.

By (
2.34
), for any choice of
\tau\in\mathbb{H}
, we have

(5.8)

\eta(M\cdot\tau)=\psi(M,\sqrt{j_{M}})\sqrt{j_{M}(\tau)}\eta(\tau).

By (
5.4
) in
Proposition

5.1
, we also have

(5.9)

\eta(M\cdot\tau)=e^{\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(M)}\sqrt{j_{M}(\tau)}\eta(\tau).

Therefore,

(5.10)

\psi(M,\sqrt{j_{M}})=\frac{\eta(M\cdot\tau)}{\sqrt{j_{M}(\tau)}\eta(\tau)}=e^{\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(M)},

completing the proof.
∎

5.2.
Properties of the Shintani–Faddeev phase

The phase
(\psi^{-2}\chi_{\mathbf{r}}^{-1})(A)
involves only
A
, whereas the phase
\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
involves both
A_{t}
and
Q
. In order the establish a relation, we require some technical lemmas about the quadratic form
Q
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
(K,j,m,Q)
be an admissible tuple, let
f
be the conductor of
Q
, and define

(5.11)

\displaystyle\langle a,b,c\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{j}}{f}Q.

(1)

If
d_{j}
is even, then
a
,
b
,
c
are all odd.

(2)

If
d_{j}\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
, then

either

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right),\ ac\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),

(5.12)

or

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right),\ ac\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right).

(3)

If
d_{j}\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
, then

either

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right),\ ac\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),

(5.13)

or

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right),\ ac\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right).

Proof.

We have

(5.14)

\displaystyle b^{2}-4ac

\displaystyle=\Delta_{j}=(d_{j}-1)^{2}-4.

It follows that if
d_{j}
is even, then

b
is odd. Suppose
ac
is not also odd. Then

(5.15)

\displaystyle b^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv(d_{j}-1)^{2}+4\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right),

which is impossible, because
n^{2}\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right)
for every odd integer
n
.

Next, suppose
d_{j}\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
.
Then
d_{j}=4n+1
for some integer
n
.
Consequently

(5.16)

\displaystyle b^{2}-4ac

\displaystyle=16n^{2}-4.

It follows that
b
is even, and

(5.17)

\displaystyle\left(b/2\right)^{2}-ac

\displaystyle=4n^{2}-1.

implying one of the pair
(b/2,ac)
is even and the other odd.

Finally, suppose
d_{j}\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
.
Then
d_{j}=4n+3
for some integer
n
.
Consequently

(5.18)

\displaystyle b^{2}-4ac
