\displaystyle=16n(n+1).

It follows that
b
is even and

(5.19)

\displaystyle\left(b/2\right)^{2}-ac

\displaystyle=4n(n+1),

implying that the numbers
b/2
,
ac
are either both even or both odd.
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
(K,j,m,Q)
be an admissible tuple, let
f
be the conductor of
Q
, and let

(5.20)

\displaystyle\langle a,b,c\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q.

Suppose
d_{j,m}
is even.
Then
a,b,c
are all odd.

Proof.

It follows from
Lemma

4.23
that
d_{j}
is even,
m\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
, and
r_{j,m}
is odd. We have

(5.21)

\displaystyle\tfrac{f_{jm}}{f}Q

\displaystyle=r_{j,m}\tfrac{f_{j}}{f}Q

It follows from

Lemma

5.3
that the coefficients of
\frac{f_{j}}{f}Q
are all odd. Since
r_{j,m}
is odd, the same must be true of the coefficients of
\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q
.
∎

The next result is the main technical lemma needed to relate the two phases by showing an agreement of signs.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
be an admissible tuple and let
f
be the conductor of
Q
. Then

(5.22)

\displaystyle(-1)^{\frac{f_{j}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{1+\delta^{(2d)}_{A_{t}\mathbf{p},\mathbf{p}}}

for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. (See
Definition

1.28
for the definition of
A_{t}
).

Proof.

We begin by finding an expression for
A_{t}-I
.
Using
Theorem

4.50
together with
Definition

4.32
,

Lemma

4.3
, and
Definition

1.24
,
we have

\displaystyle A_{t}-I

\displaystyle=\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon^{j(2m+1)})-I

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{d_{j(2m+1)}-3}{2}\right)I+\frac{f_{j(2m+1)}}{f}SQ

(5.23)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{d_{j(2m+1)}-3}{2}\right)I+r_{j,2m+1}S\bar{Q}

where

(5.24)

\displaystyle\bar{Q}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{f_{j}}{f}Q.

It follows from
Proposition

4.18
that

\displaystyle\frac{d_{j(2m+1)}-3}{2}

\displaystyle=\cosh(2m+1)j\theta-1

\displaystyle=2\sinh^{2}\frac{(2m+1)j\theta}{2}

\displaystyle=2d^{2}\sinh^{2}\frac{j\theta}{2}

\displaystyle=d^{2}(\cosh j\theta-1)

(5.25)

\displaystyle=d^{2}\left(\frac{d_{j}-3}{2}\right),

while Lemma
4.23
implies
r_{j,2m+1}=r^{2}_{j,m+1}-r^{2}_{j,m}=(r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m})d
. Hence

(5.26)

\displaystyle A_{t}-I

\displaystyle=dH,

where

(5.27)

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=d\left(\frac{d_{j}-3}{2}\right)I+(r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m})S\bar{Q}.

So the problem is to show that, for all
\mathbf{p}
,

(5.28)

\displaystyle\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle H\mathbf{p}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)

Set
\bar{Q}=\langle a,b,c\rangle
.
There are four cases to consider.

Case 1.

d_{j}
even,
m\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
.
It follows from Lemma
5.3
that
a
,
b
,
c
are all odd.
So

(5.29)

\displaystyle\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\equiv p_{1}+p_{1}p_{2}+p_{2}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),

implying
Q(\mathbf{p})\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
if and only if
p_{1}\equiv p_{2}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
.
On the other hand it follows from Lemma
4.23
that
d
is even, which in view of Theorem
4.50
, means
H\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
.
So
H\mathbf{p}\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
if and only if
p_{1}=p_{2}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
.

Case 2.

d_{j}
even,
m\not\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
.
As before
a,b,c
all odd, implying
\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
if and only if
p_{1}\equiv p_{2}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
.
On the other hand it follows from Lemma
4.23
that
d
and
r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m}=d-2r_{j,m}
are odd.
So

(5.30)

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\frac{d(d_{j}-3)-(r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m})b}{2}&1\\
1&\frac{d(d_{j}-3)+(r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m})b}{2}\end{pmatrix}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right).

Since

(5.31)

\displaystyle\frac{d(d_{j}-3)-(r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m})b}{2}+\frac{d(d_{j}-3)+(r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m})b}{2}\equiv 1\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),

H\equiv\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
or
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
. Consequently
H\mathbf{p}\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
if and only if
p_{1}\equiv p_{2}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
.

Case 3.

d_{j}\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
. Then
(d_{j}-3)/2
is odd. It follows from Lemma
4.23
that
d
and
r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m}=d-2r_{j,m}
are also odd.
So

(5.32)

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\equiv I+S\bar{Q}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right).

In view of Lemma
5.3
there are four possibilities:

(1)

b\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
and
a\equiv c\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
. We have

(5.33)

\displaystyle Q(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\equiv p_{1}+p_{2}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\,.

So
\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\iff p_{1}+p_{2}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\iff H\mathbf{p}\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
.

(2)

b\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
,
a\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
,
c\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
.

(5.34)

\displaystyle\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\equiv p_{1}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right).

So
\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\iff p_{1}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\iff H\mathbf{p}\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
.

(3)

b\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
,
a\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
,
c\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
.

(5.35)

\displaystyle\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\equiv p_{2}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\,.

So
\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\iff p_{2}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\iff H\mathbf{p}\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
.

(4)

b\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
and
a\equiv c\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
. We have

(5.36)

\displaystyle\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\equiv 0\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right).

So
\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
and
H\mathbf{p}\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
for all
\mathbf{p}
.

Case 4.

d_{j}\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
. Then
(d_{j}-3)/2
is even. It follows from Lemma
4.23
that
d
and
r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m}=d-2r_{j,m}
are odd.
So

(5.37)

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\equiv S\bar{Q}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\,.

In view of Lemma
5.3
there are four possibilities:

(1)

b\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
,
a\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
,
c\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
. We have

(5.38)

\displaystyle\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\equiv p_{1}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right).

So
\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\iff p_{1}=0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\iff H\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
.

(2)

b\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
,
a\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
,
c\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
.

(5.39)

\displaystyle\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\equiv p_{2}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right).

So
\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\iff p_{2}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\iff H\mathbf{p}\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
.

(3)

b\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
,
a\equiv c\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
.

(5.40)

\displaystyle\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\equiv 0\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right).

So
\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
and
H\mathbf{p}\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
for all
\mathbf{p}
.

(4)

b\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
and
a\equiv c\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
. We have

(5.41)

\displaystyle\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\equiv p_{1}+p_{2}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right).

So
\bar{Q}(\mathbf{p})\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\iff p_{1}+p_{2}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)\iff H\mathbf{p}\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
.

This completes the proof of
Equation

5.27
in all four cases and thus proves the lemma.
∎

The sign calculation we have just finished allows us to establish an equality between the square of the SF phase and a product of eta-multiplier and theta-multiplier values.

Theorem 5.6

(Phase relation)
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)
be an admissible tuple, and let
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}/d\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Then,

(5.42)

\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)^{2}=(\psi^{-2}\chi_{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}^{-1})(A_{t}).

Proof.

Let
f
be the conductor of
Q
.
By
Definition

1.30
, the square of the SF-phase is

(5.43)

\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)^{2}=\left((-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{p})}e^{-\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(A_{t})}\xi_{d}^{-\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})}\right)^{2}=e^{-\frac{\pi i}{6}\Psi(A_{t})}\omega_{d}^{-\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})}.

The matrix
A_{t}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)
satisfies the conditions

\gamma\neq 0
and
\Tr(A_{t})>0
.
By
Proposition

5.2
, we have

(5.44)

\psi^{-2}(A_{t})=\left(e^{\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(A_{t})}\right)^{-2}=e^{-\frac{\pi i}{6}\Psi(A_{t})}

It follows from (
5.26
), (
5.27
), and (
5.24
) that

\displaystyle\langle A_{t}\mathbf{p},\mathbf{p}\rangle=-\langle\mathbf{p},A_{t}\mathbf{p}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{df_{j}(r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m})}{f}\langle\mathbf{p},SQ\mathbf{p}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{df_{j}(d-2r_{j,m})}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})

(5.45)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{d^{2}f_{j}}{f}-\frac{2df_{jm}}{f}\right)Q(\mathbf{p}).

Hence the character value
\chi_{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}(A_{t})
can therefore be written as

\displaystyle\chi_{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}^{-1}(A_{t})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{1+\delta_{A_{t}(d^{-1}\mathbf{p}),d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}^{(2)}}e^{\frac{\pi i}{d^{2}}\langle A_{t}\mathbf{p},\mathbf{p}\rangle}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{1+\delta_{A_{t}\mathbf{p},\mathbf{p}}^{(2d)}}e^{\frac{\pi i}{d^{2}}\left(\frac{d^{2}f_{j}}{f}-\frac{2df_{jm}}{f}\right)Q(\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{1+\delta_{A_{t}\mathbf{p},\mathbf{p}}^{(2d)}+\frac{f_{j}}{f}}\omega_{d}^{-\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})}

(5.46)

\displaystyle=\omega_{d}^{-\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})},

where we have used
Lemma

5.5
in the last step.
Thus, plugging (
5.44
) and (
5.46
) into (
5.43
),

(5.47)

\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)^{2}=\psi^{-2}(A_{t})\chi_{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}^{-1}(A_{t})=(\psi^{-2}\chi_{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}^{-1})(A_{t}),

completing the proof.
∎

5.3.
Properties of the ghost overlaps

We now apply the results on the SF phase, which appears in the definition of the candidate ghost overlaps
\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
, to establish some relations satisfied by the
\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)
be an admissible tuple. Then
for all
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\equiv\mathbf{p}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
and
\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{p}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
,

(5.48)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}^{\prime}}(t)

\displaystyle=\xi_{d}^{\langle\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{p}\rangle}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t).

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbf{p}^{\prime}}(t)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime})-s_{d}(\mathbf{p})}\xi_{d}^{-\frac{f_{jm}}{f}(Q(\mathbf{p}^{\prime})-Q(\mathbf{p}))}\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)

(5.49)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime})-s_{d}(\mathbf{p})}\xi_{d}^{-a(p^{\prime 2}_{1}-p_{1}^{2})-b(p^{\prime}_{1}p^{\prime}_{2}-p_{1}p_{2})-c(p^{\prime 2}_{2}-p_{2}^{2})}\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)

where we have set
\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q=\langle a,b,c\rangle
. If
d
is odd, then

(5.50)

\displaystyle(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime})-s_{d}(\mathbf{p})}\xi_{d}^{-a(p^{\prime 2}_{1}-p_{1}^{2})-b(p^{\prime}_{1}p^{\prime}_{2}-p_{1}p_{2})-c(p^{\prime 2}_{2}-p_{2}^{2})}=1=\xi_{d}^{\langle\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{p}\rangle}.

Suppose, on the other hand, that
d
is even. Then
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\equiv\mathbf{p}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
, implying

(5.51)

\displaystyle(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime})-s_{d}(\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle=1

Also, it follows from Lemma
5.4
that
b
is odd. So, setting
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}=\mathbf{p}+d\mathbf{q}
,

\displaystyle\xi_{d}^{-a(p^{\prime 2}_{1}-p_{1}^{2})-b(p^{\prime}_{1}p^{\prime}_{2}-p_{1}p_{2})-c(p^{\prime 2}_{2}-p_{2}^{2})}

\displaystyle=\xi_{d}^{-ad(p^{\prime}_{1}+p_{1})q_{1}-((p_{1}+dq_{1})(p_{2}+dq_{2})-p_{1}p_{2})-cd(p^{\prime}_{2}+p_{2})q_{2}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{q_{1}p_{2}+q_{2}p_{1}}

(5.52)

\displaystyle=\xi_{d}^{\langle\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{p}\rangle}.

We conclude that

(5.53)

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbf{p}^{\prime}}(t)

\displaystyle=\xi_{d}^{\langle\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{p}\rangle}\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)

irrespective of the value of
d
. Lastly, it follows from
Lemma

2.14
that

(5.54)

\displaystyle\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}^{\prime}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)

\displaystyle=\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right).

Hence

\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}^{\prime}}(t)=\xi_{d}^{\langle\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{p}\rangle}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t).

∎

Proof of
Lemma

1.42
.

Let

(5.55)

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\nu}_{G\mathbf{p}}(t)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{\mathbf{p}},

and let
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be such that
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\equiv\mathbf{p}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
and
\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{p}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
. Setting
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\equiv\mathbf{p}+d\mathbf{q}
, it follows from (
3.4
) and
Lemma

5.7
that

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{p}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(d+1)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}\xi_{d}^{\langle G\mathbf{p}^{\prime},G\mathbf{p}\rangle}f(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(d+1)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}\xi_{d}^{d\det G\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}f(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(d+1)(1+\det G)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}f(\mathbf{p})

(5.56)

\displaystyle=f(\mathbf{p}),

where in the last step we used the fact that
\det G
is coprime to
d
, as follows from (
1.60
). The second statement is an immediate consequence of this.
∎

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
be an admissible tuple, and let
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
If
\mathbf{p}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
, then the numbers
\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
are real and satisfy

(5.57)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\tilde{\nu}_{-\mathbf{p}}(t)=1.

Remark
.

This theorem establishes two of the requirements which must be satisfied if the expression on the right-hand side of (
1.62
) in
Definition

1.43
is to be a ghost fiducial.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be the unique class in
\overline{\Clm}^{\flat}_{d\infty_{2}}(\mathcal{O}_{f})
that maps to the
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-orbit of
(d^{-1}\mathbf{p},\rho_{t})
under the map
\Upsilon_{d\mathcal{O}_{f}}
described in
[
72
, Thm. 3.12]
and at the end of
Section

2.6
.

Theorem

2.19
then gives the formula

(5.58)

(\psi^{-2}\chi_{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}^{-1})(A_{t})\ \shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)^{2}=\exp\!\left(nZ_{d\infty_{2}}^{\prime}(0,\mathfrak{A})\right),

where
Z_{d\infty_{2}}(s,\mathfrak{A})
is the differenced ray class partial zeta function defined in
Definition

2.4
.
By
Definition

1.32
and
Theorem

5.6
, we have

(5.59)

(\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t))^{2}=\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)^{2}\ \shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)^{2}=(\psi^{-2}\chi_{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}^{-1})(A_{t})\ \shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)^{2},

and thus

(5.60)

(\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t))^{2}=\exp\!\left(nZ_{d\infty_{2}}^{\prime}(0,\mathfrak{A})\right).

The partial zeta function
Z_{d\infty_{2}}(s,\mathfrak{A})
is a complex analytic function defined by a Dirichet series with real coefficients for
\Real(s)>1
and by analytic continuation to all
s\in\mathbb{C}
. The equation
(Z_{d\infty_{2}}(s,\mathfrak{A}))^{\ast}=Z_{d\infty_{2}}(s^{\ast},\mathfrak{A})
holds for
\Real(s)>1
and thus for all
s\in\mathbb{C}
(where
∗
denotes complex conjugation). Therefore,
Z_{d\infty_{2}}^{\prime}(0,\mathfrak{A})
is real, so
\exp\!\left(nZ_{d\infty_{2}}^{\prime}(0,\mathfrak{A})\right)
is real and positive, and thus

\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
is real.

We now compute

\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\tilde{\nu}_{-\mathbf{p}}(t)
. Using the definition of
\tilde{\nu}_{\pm\mathbf{p}}(t)
(
Definition

1.32
), we have

(5.61)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\tilde{\nu}_{-\mathbf{p}}(t)=\left(\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\phi_{-\mathbf{p}}(t)\right)\left(\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)\shin^{-d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)\right).

Using
Definition

1.30
, the SF phase satisfies

(5.62)

\phi_{-\mathbf{p}}(t)=(-1)^{s_{d}(-\mathbf{p})}e^{-\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(A_{t})}\xi_{d}^{-\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q(-\mathbf{p})}=(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{p})}e^{-\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(A_{t})}\xi_{d}^{-\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})}=\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t).

Corollary

2.12
and
Theorem

5.6
imply

(5.63)

\displaystyle\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)\shin^{-d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=(\psi^{2}\chi_{\mathbf{r}})(A_{t})=\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)^{-2}.

Consequently

\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\tilde{\nu}_{-\mathbf{p}}(t)=\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)^{2}\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)^{-2}=1
.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
be an admissible tuple. Then

(5.64)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{0}}(t)+\frac{1}{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{0}}(t)}

\displaystyle=-(d-2r)\sqrt{d_{j}+1}.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
2.15
that

(5.65)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{0}}(t)

\displaystyle=\phi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)\shin^{\mathbf{0}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{j_{A_{t}}(\rho_{Q,+})}}

Write
A_{t}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. It follows from
Lemma

4.47
that
A_{t}\rho_{Q,+}=\rho_{Q,+}
. Hence

(5.66)

\displaystyle\gamma\rho_{Q,+}^{2}+(\delta-\alpha)\rho_{Q,+}-\beta

\displaystyle=0.

Consequently

(5.67)

\displaystyle\rho_{Q,+}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha-\delta\pm\sqrt{(\alpha+\delta)^{2}-4}}{2\gamma}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle j_{A}(\rho_{Q,+})

\displaystyle=\frac{\Tr(A)\pm\sqrt{\Tr(A)^{2}-4}}{2}.

It follows from Theorem
4.50
that
\Tr(A_{t})
and consequently
j_{A_{t}}(\rho_{Q,+})
are positive. So

(5.68)

\displaystyle\left(\sqrt{j_{A_{t}}(\rho_{Q,+})}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{j_{A_{t}}(\rho_{Q,+})}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\Tr(A_{t})+2

implying

(5.69)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{0}}(t)+\frac{1}{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{0}}(t)}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\Tr(A_{t})+2}.

It follows from Theorem
4.50
that
A_{t}=\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon^{j(2m+1)})
, which in view of
Lemma

4.3
means

\Tr(A)=\Tr(\varepsilon^{j(2m+1)})=d_{j(2m+1)}-1
. Lemma
4.23
then implies

(5.70)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\Tr(A)+2}

\displaystyle=(r_{j,m+1}-r_{j,m})\sqrt{d_{j}+1}=(d-2r_{j,m})\sqrt{d_{j}+1}.

Together, (
5.69
) and (
5.70
) imply (
5.65
).
∎

Definition 5.10

(function
\Phi_{t}
)
.

Given an admissible tuple
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
, define
\Phi_{t}\colon\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z}
by

(5.71)

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=r(2x+d+d_{j}-1).

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
be an admissible tuple.

(1)

If
d
is odd, then
\Phi_{t}
is a bijection of
\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z}
onto itself.

(2)

If
d
is even, then
\Phi_{t}
is a bijection of
\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z}
onto the set of odd elements in
\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z}
.

The inverse function is given by

(5.72)

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}^{-1}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{(d+1)\big((d_{j}+1)rx-(d_{j}-1)\big)}{2}.

For all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\Phi_{t}(x)
is coprime to
\bar{d}
if and only if
2x+d_{j}-1
is coprime to
d
.

Proof.

Suppose
d
is odd. If
\Phi_{t}(x)=\Phi_{t}(x^{\prime})
, then
2rx\equiv 2rx^{\prime}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
, which in view of
Corollary

4.22
means
x\equiv x^{\prime}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\bar{d}\right)
. So
\Phi_{t}
is injective. Since the domain and codomain have the same cardinality, it follows that it is also surjective. To calculate the inverse, observe that it follows from Lemma
4.24
that, if
y=\Phi_{t}(x)
, then

(5.73)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=2^{-1}\left(r^{-1}y-(d_{j}-1)\right)=\tfrac{d+1}{2}\left((d_{j}+1)ry-(d_{j}-1)\right),

where
2^{-1}
is the multiplicative inverse of
2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
.

Suppose, on the other hand, that
d
is even. Then it follows from
Corollary

4.22
that
r
is odd and from
Lemma

4.23
that
d_{j}
is even. So
\Phi_{t}(x)
is odd for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z}
. If
\Phi_{t}(x)\equiv\Phi_{t}(x^{\prime})\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\bar{d}\right)
, then
2rx\equiv 2rx^{\prime}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d\right)
. Since
r
is odd, it follows that
x\equiv x^{\prime}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
. So
\Phi_{t}
is injective. Since the domain and the set of odd elements in
\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z}
have the same cardinality,
\Phi_{t}
must in fact be a bijection onto the set of odd elements in
\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z}
. To calculate the inverse, suppose
y=\Phi_{t}(x)
for some
x\in\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z}
. Then it follows from Lemma
4.24
that

\displaystyle 2x

\displaystyle\equiv yr^{-1}-(d_{j}-1)-d\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d\right)

\displaystyle\equiv yr(1+d_{j}+d)-(d_{j}-1)-d\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d\right)

(5.74)

\displaystyle\equiv(d_{j}+1)ry-(d_{j}-1)+d(yr-1)\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d\right).

Since
yr-1
is even, this means

(5.75)

\displaystyle 2x

\displaystyle=(d_{j}+1)ry+1-d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d\right).

The fact that
yr
and
d_{j}\pm 1
are odd means the right-hand side is even. So

(5.76)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{(d_{j}+1)r_{j,m}y-(d_{j}-1)}{2}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)

or, equivalently,

(5.77)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\equiv(d+1)\left(\frac{(d_{j}+1)ry-(d_{j}-1)}{2}\right)\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right).

Finally,
\Phi_{t}(x)
is coprime to
\bar{d}
if and only if it is coprime to
d
.
Since
r
is coprime to
d
, this is true if and only if
2x-1+d_{j}
is coprime to
d
.
∎

5.4.
Ghost existence under the Twisted Convolution Conjecture

We now turn to the proof of Theorem
1.45
, asserting that, under the assumption of
Conjecture

1.35
, the
d\times d
matrix
\tilde{\Pi}_{s}
constructed from a fiducial datum is a ghost
r
-SIC fiducial projector.

Proof of Theorem
1.45
.

To prove that
\tilde{\Pi}_{s}
is a ghost projector, we need to show:

(1)

The expression on the RHS of (
1.62
) is well-defined, in that the sum is independent of the set of coset representatives chosen,

(2)

\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
is real for all
\mathbf{p}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
,

(3)

\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\tilde{\nu}_{-\mathbf{p}}(t)=1
for all
\mathbf{p}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
,

(4)

\tilde{\Pi}_{s}^{2}=\tilde{\Pi}^{\vphantom{2}}_{s}
.

The first proposition is proved in
Lemma

1.42
,
and the second and third in
Theorem

5.8
. It remains to prove the last.
We have

(5.78)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}

where the sum is over any set of coset representatives of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}/d\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with the representative of
d\mathbb{Z}^{2}
excluded.
Hence

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s}^{2}-\tilde{\Pi}^{\vphantom{2}}_{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{r(r-d)}{d^{2}}I+\frac{2r-d}{d^{2}\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle\hskip 65.44142pt+\frac{1}{d^{2}(d_{j}+1)}\sum_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\xi_{d}^{\langle G^{-1}\mathbf{p},G^{-1}\mathbf{q}\rangle}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{q}}(t)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q})}

\displaystyle=\frac{r(r-d)}{d^{2}}I+\frac{2r-d}{d^{2}\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}

(5.79)

\displaystyle\hskip 65.44142pt+\frac{1}{d^{2}(d_{j}+1)}\sum_{\mathbf{p}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{q}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2},\\
\mathbf{q}\notin\mathbf{p}+d\mathbb{Z}^{2}\end{subarray}}\xi_{d}^{\Det(G^{-1})\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}}(t)\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{q}}(t)\right)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}},

where
\mathbf{p}
is summed over a complete set of cosets for
\mathbb{Z}^{2}/d\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and
\mathbf{q}
is summed over such a set with
d\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
\mathbf{p}+d\mathbb{Z}^{2}
removed. Comparing
(
1.60
) to (
5.71
), we see that

(5.80)

\displaystyle\Det(G^{-1})

\displaystyle=\left(\Det(G)\right)^{-1}=\Phi_{t}(\lambda)

for some
\lambda\in\mathcal{Z}_{t}
,
while it follows from Theorem
4.20
that

(5.81)

\displaystyle\frac{r(r-d)}{d^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(d^{2}-1)}{d^{2}(d_{j}+1)}.

Inserting these expressions into
Equation

5.79
gives

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s}^{2}-\tilde{\Pi}^{\vphantom{2}}_{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}(d_{j}+1)}\left(-(d^{2}-1)I-(d-2r)\sqrt{d_{j}+1}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}\right.

(5.82)

\displaystyle\hskip 91.04872pt+\left.\sum_{\mathbf{p}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{q}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2},\\
\mathbf{q}\notin\mathbf{p}+d\mathbb{Z}^{2}\end{subarray}}\xi_{d}^{\Phi_{t}(\lambda)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}}(t)\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{q}}(t)\right)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}\right).

Theorem

5.8
and
Lemma

5.9
imply

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{p}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{q}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2},\\
\mathbf{q}\notin\mathbf{p}+d\mathbb{Z}^{2}\end{subarray}}\xi_{d}^{\Phi_{t}(\lambda)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}}(t)\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{q}}(t)\right)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=(d^{2}-1)I+\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{q}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2},\\
\mathbf{q}\notin\mathbf{p}+d\mathbb{Z}^{2}\end{subarray}}\xi_{d}^{\Phi_{t}(\lambda)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}}(t)\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{q}}(t)\right)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=(d^{2}-1)I+\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{q}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2},\\
\mathbf{q}\notin\mathbf{p}+d\mathbb{Z}^{2}\end{subarray}}\xi_{d}^{\Phi_{t}(\lambda)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}\frac{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{q}}(t)}{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p}}(t)}\right)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=(d^{2}-1)I+(d-2r)\sqrt{d_{j}+1}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d_{j,m}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}

(5.83)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{p}}}\xi_{d}^{\Phi_{t}(\lambda)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}\frac{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{q}}(t)}{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p}}(t)}\right)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}

where
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{p}}
is a complete set of coset representatives for
\mathbb{Z}^{2}/d_{j,m}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
which includes
\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{p}
. Hence

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s}^{2}-\tilde{\Pi}^{\vphantom{2}}_{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}(d_{j}+1)}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{p}}}\xi_{d}^{\Phi_{t}(\lambda)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}\frac{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{q}}(t)}{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p}}(t)}\right)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}

(5.84)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}(d_{j}+1)}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{p}}}\xi_{d}^{\Phi_{t}(\lambda)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}\left(\frac{\phi_{\mathbf{q}}(t)}{\phi_{\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p}}(t)}\right)\left(\frac{\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{q}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)}{\shin^{d^{-1}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)}\right)\right)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}

where we used
Definition

1.32
in the second line. It follows from
Definition

1.30
that

(5.85)

\displaystyle\frac{\phi_{\mathbf{q}}(t)}{\phi_{\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p}}(t)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{q})+s_{d}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})}\xi_{d}^{\frac{f_{jm}}{f}(Q(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})-Q(\mathbf{q}))}.

We have

\displaystyle(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{q})+s_{d}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(1+d)((1+q_{1})(1+q_{2})+(1+q_{1}+p_{1})(1+q_{2}+p_{2})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(1+d)(p_{1}(1+q_{2})+p_{2}(1+q_{1})+p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(1+d)(p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{1}p_{2})+(1+d)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{1+d+(1+d)(1+p_{1})(1+p_{2})}\xi_{d}^{d\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}

(5.86)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{1+s_{d}(\mathbf{p})}\xi_{d}^{d\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}.

Also, referring to
Definition

1.28
, we see that

(5.87)

\displaystyle\frac{2f_{j}}{f}\mathbf{p}^{\rm{T}}Q\mathbf{q}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2f_{j}}{f}\langle\mathbf{p},SQ\mathbf{q}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{p},(d_{j}-1-2L_{\rm{z},t})\mathbf{q}\rangle,

implying

\displaystyle\frac{f_{jm}}{f}\left(Q(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})-Q(\mathbf{q})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{rf_{j}}{f}\left(Q(\mathbf{p})-2\mathbf{p}^{\rm{T}}Q\mathbf{q}\right)

(5.88)

\displaystyle=\frac{rf_{j}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})-r(d_{j}-1)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle+2r\langle\mathbf{p},L_{\rm{z},t}\mathbf{q}\rangle.

Hence

(5.89)

\displaystyle\frac{\phi_{\mathbf{q}}(t)}{\phi_{\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p}}(t)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{1+s_{d}(\mathbf{p})}\xi_{d}^{\frac{rf_{j}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})}\xi_{d}^{(d-r(d_{j}-1))\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}\omega_{d}^{r\langle\mathbf{p},L_{\rm{z},t}\mathbf{q}\rangle}.

It follows from
Lemma

2.13
that

(5.90)

\displaystyle\frac{\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{q}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)}{\shin^{d^{-1}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)}

\displaystyle=\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{q}}_{\!A^{\vphantom{-1}}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)\shin^{d^{-1}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})}_{\!A^{-1}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right).

Substituting
(
5.89
) and (
5.90
) into (
5.84
) gives

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s}^{2}-\tilde{\Pi}^{\vphantom{2}}_{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}(d_{j}+1)}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}(-1)^{1+s_{d}(\mathbf{p})}\xi_{d}^{\frac{rf_{j}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{p}}}\xi_{d}^{\left(\Phi_{t}(\lambda)+d-r(d_{j}-1)\right)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}\right.

(5.91)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\left.\omega_{d}^{r\langle\mathbf{p},L_{\rm{z},t}\mathbf{q}\rangle}\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{q}}_{\!A^{\vphantom{-1}}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)\shin^{d^{-1}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})}_{\!A^{-1}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)\right)D_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}.

It follows from
Definition

1.34
that
2\lambda+d_{j}-1
is coprime to
d
. In view of
Lemma

5.11
this means
\Phi_{t}(\lambda)
is coprime to
\bar{d}
. Consequently

(5.92)

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}(\lambda)+d

\displaystyle\equiv(d+1)\Phi_{t}(\lambda)\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\bar{d}\right).

Also, it follows from
Lemma

4.23
that
d_{j}
is even if
d
is even. So

(5.93)

\displaystyle dd_{j}

\displaystyle\equiv 0\quad\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\bar{d}\right)

irrespective of whether
d
is odd or even. Putting these two facts together we conclude

\displaystyle\xi_{d}^{\left(\Phi_{t}(\lambda)+d-r(d_{j}-1)\right)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\xi_{d}^{\left((d+1)\Phi_{t}(\lambda)-r(d_{j}-1)\right)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\xi_{d}^{\left((d+1)r(2z+d+d_{j}-1)-r(d_{j}-1)\right)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\xi_{d}^{r\left(2(d+1)\lambda+d(d+1)+d(d_{j}-1))\right)\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}

(5.94)

\displaystyle=\omega_{d}^{rz\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\rangle}.

Substituting back into (
5.91
) we deduce

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s}^{2}-\tilde{\Pi}_{s}

(5.95)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt=\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\frac{(-1)^{1+s_{d}(\mathbf{p})}\xi_{d}^{\frac{rf_{j}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})}}{d^{2}(d_{j}+1)}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{p}}}\omega_{d}^{r\langle\mathbf{p},(zI+L_{\rm{z},t})\mathbf{q}\rangle}\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{q}}_{\!A^{\vphantom{-1}}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)\shin^{d^{-1}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})}_{\!A^{-1}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)\right)D_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}.

It follows that
\tilde{\Pi}_{s}^{2}=\tilde{\Pi}_{s}
if and only if

(5.96)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{p}}}\omega_{d}^{r\langle\mathbf{p},(zI+L_{\rm{z},t})\mathbf{q}\rangle}\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{q}}_{\!A^{\vphantom{-1}}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)\shin^{d^{-1}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})}_{\!A^{-1}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)=0

for all
\mathbf{p}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
. If
\mathbf{p}\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
, it follows from
Lemma

2.13
that

(5.97)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{p}}}\omega_{d}^{r\langle\mathbf{p},(zI+L_{\rm{z},t})\mathbf{q}\rangle}\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{q}}_{\!A^{\vphantom{-1}}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)\shin^{d^{-1}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})}_{\!A^{-1}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{0}}}\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{q}}_{\!A^{\vphantom{-1}}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)\shin^{d^{-1}(\mathbf{q})}_{\!A^{-1}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)=d^{2}

irrespective of whether
\tilde{\Pi}_{s}
is a ghost fiducial.
So it is in fact the case that
\tilde{\Pi}_{s}^{2}=\tilde{\Pi}_{s}
if and only if

(5.98)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{p}}}\omega_{d}^{r\langle\mathbf{p},(zI+L_{\rm{z},t})\mathbf{q}\rangle}\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{q}}_{\!A^{\vphantom{-1}}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)\shin^{d^{-1}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})}_{\!A^{-1}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right)=d^{2}\delta^{(d)}_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}

for all
\mathbf{p}
.
∎

5.5.
Remarks concerning the set of shifts

In this subsection we make some observations concerning the set of shifts
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
for a given admissible tuple
t
.

The argument in
Section

5.4
actually shows a little more than was required for the proof of
Theorem

1.45
. Specifically, let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
be an admissible tuple, and let
\lambda
be an element of
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
. Then
2z+d_{j}-1
is coprime to
d
, which in view of
Lemma

5.11
means
\Phi_{t}(\lambda)
is coprime to
\bar{d}
. So if we define
G=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&\Phi_{t}(\lambda)^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
(where
\Phi_{t}(\lambda)^{-1}
is the inverse of
\Phi_{t}(\lambda)
modulo
\bar{d}
), then
G\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
, and
s=(d,r,Q,G,g)\sim(K,j,m,Q,G,g)
is a fiducial datum for any appropriate choice of
g
. Consequently

(5.99)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{G\mathbf{p}}(t)D_{\mathbf{p}}

is a ghost fiducial. Consider its complex conjugate

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s}^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{G\mathbf{p}}(t)D_{J\mathbf{p}}

(5.100)

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{JG\mathbf{p}}(t)D_{\mathbf{p}}

(where
J
is defined in
Definition

3.3
). It is easily seen that
\tilde{\Pi}_{s}^{*}
is also a ghost fiducial. The fact that
\Det(JG)=-\Det(G)
means that
\Det(JG)
is coprime to
\bar{d}
, implying that
\bar{\lambda}=\Phi_{t}^{-1}(\Det(JG)^{-1})
is well-defined. Since
\Phi_{t}(\bar{\lambda})=\Det(JG)^{-1}
is coprime to
\bar{d}
it follows from
Lemma

5.11
that
2\bar{\lambda}+d_{j}-1
. The argument in
Section

5.4
shows that the fact that
\tilde{\Pi}_{s}^{*2}=\tilde{\Pi}_{s}^{*}
implies that
\bar{\lambda}
satisfies (
1.58
). It follows that
\bar{\lambda}\in\mathcal{Z}_{t}
.

We have

(5.101)

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{t}^{-1}(-\Det G^{-1})=\Phi_{t}^{-1}(-\Phi_{t}(\lambda)).

This expression leads to a simple, fully explicit expression for
\bar{\lambda}
in terms of
\lambda
:

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
be an admissible tuple, let
\lambda\in\mathcal{Z}_{t}
, and let
\bar{\lambda}=\Phi_{t}^{-1}(-\Phi_{t}(\lambda))
. Then

(5.102)

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}

\displaystyle=-(\lambda+d_{j}-1)

Proof.

Definition

5.10
and
Lemma

5.11
imply

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{t}^{-1}(-\Det G^{-1})

\displaystyle=\Phi_{t}^{-1}(-\Phi_{t}(\lambda))

(5.103)

\displaystyle=\frac{(d+1)\left(-(d_{j}+1)r^{2}(2z+d+d_{j}-1)-(d_{j}-1)\right)}{2}.

It follows from (
4.110
) that

(5.104)

\displaystyle-(d_{j}+1)r^{2}=d-1+k\bar{d}

for some integer
k
. So

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{(d+1)\left((d-1+k\bar{d})(2z+d+d_{j}-1)-(d_{j}-1)\right)}{2}

\displaystyle=(d+1)(d-1+k\bar{d})\lambda+\frac{(d+1)\left((d-1+k\bar{d})(d+d_{j}-1)-(d_{j}-1)\right)}{2}

(5.105)

\displaystyle=-\lambda+\frac{(d+1)\left((d-1+k\bar{d})(d+d_{j}-1)-(d_{j}-1)\right)}{2}.

Suppose
d
is odd. Then
d+1=2m
for some integer
m
, and

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}

\displaystyle=-\lambda+m\left((d-1+k\bar{d})(d+d_{j}-1)-(d_{j}-1)\right)

\displaystyle=-\lambda-2m(d_{j}-1)

\displaystyle=-\lambda-(d+1)(d_{j}-1)

(5.106)

\displaystyle=-\lambda-(d_{j}-1).

Suppose, on the other hand,
d
is even. Then
d=2m
for some integer
m
, and

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}

\displaystyle=-\lambda+\frac{(d+1)\left((2m+4km-1)(2m+d_{j}-1)-(d_{j}-1)\right)}{2}

\displaystyle=-\lambda+\frac{(d+1)\left(2m(1+2k)(2m+d_{j}-1)-2m-2(d_{j}-1)\right)}{2}

\displaystyle=-\lambda+(d+1)\left(m(1+2k)(2m+d_{j}-1)-m-(d_{j}-1)\right)

\displaystyle=-\lambda+m(1+2k)(d_{j}-1)-m-(d_{j}-1)

(5.107)

\displaystyle=-\lambda+md_{j}-(d_{j}-1).

The fact that
d
is even means, in view of
Lemma

4.23
, that
d_{j}
is even. Consequently
md_{j}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
, and

\bar{\lambda}=-\lambda-(d_{j}-1)

in this case too.
∎

It follows from
Lemma

5.12
that possible shifts come in pairs
(\lambda,\bar{\lambda})
. The question then arises how many such pairs there are. Observe that it follows from
Lemma

4.25
that
\lambda=0
and
\lambda=1
both satisfy the requirement that
2z+d_{j}-1
be coprime to
d
. In every case examined it appears, as a matter of empirical observation, that they also satisfy (
1.58
). The corresponding values of
\bar{\lambda}
are
\bar{\lambda}=d-d_{j}+1
and
\bar{\lambda}=d-d_{j}
, respectively. The values of
\Det G
are as given in the table

\lambda

\Det G

0

r^{-1}(d+d_{j}-1)^{-1}

1

r^{-1}(d+d_{j}+1)^{-1}=r

d-d_{j}

-r^{-1}(d+d_{j}+1)^{-1}=-r

d-d_{j}+1

-r^{-1}(d+d_{j}-1)^{-1}

where the symbols
r^{-1}
,
(d+d_{j}\pm 1)^{-1}
denote multiplicative inverses modulo
\bar{d}
, and where the alternative expressions for
\Det G
in the second and third rows are obtained using (
4.110
).
Note that if
m=1
these four values for
\lambda
reduce to the two values
0
,
1
with the corresponding values of
\Det G
being
-1
,
+1
respectively.

It appears (again as a matter of empirical observation) that, if
m\leq 2
, these are the only elements of
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
. However, if
m>2
, there seem to be others. Specifically, in the cases we examined we found three pairs for
m=3
and
m=4
, and five pairs for
m=5
. However, we were not able to discern any obvious pattern to the additional
\lambda
-values. This is a question requiring further investigation.

5.6.
Conditional SIC existence

We now turn to the proof of
Theorem

1.46
, which asserts that Zauner’s conjecture follows from the conditional assumptions of the Twisted Convolution Conjectural and the Stark Conjecture, and more precisely, that those conditional assumptions imply that the
d\times d
matrix
\Pi_{s}
constructed from a fiducial datum
s
is an
r
-SIC fiducial projector.

Proof of
Theorem

1.46
.

We have

(5.108)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}.

Set
\mu_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=g(\tilde{\mu}_{p}(t))
, and apply the Galois automorphism
g
to obtain

(5.109)

\displaystyle\Pi_{s}=g(\tilde{\Pi}_{s})=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mu_{\mathbf{p}}(t)g(D^{\vphantom{t}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}})=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mu_{\mathbf{p}}(t)D_{H_{g}\mathbf{p}},

where
H_{g}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&k_{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and we have used
Theorem

3.7
in the last line. To show that
\Pi_{s}
is a Weyl–Heisenberg
r
-SIC fiducial projector, we must show that:

(1)

\Pi_{s}^{2}=\Pi_{s}
,

(2)

\mu_{p}(t)\mu_{-p}(t)=\frac{1}{d_{j}+1}
for
\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and

(3)

\left|\mu_{p}(t)\right|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}
for
\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

By
Theorem

1.45
(using the assumption of
Conjecture

1.35
),
\tilde{\Pi}_{s}
is a ghost
r
-SIC fiducial, so
\tilde{\Pi}_{s}^{2}=\tilde{\Pi}_{s}
. Applying the Galois automorphism
g
(which commutes with matrix multiplication) shows that
\Pi_{s}^{2}=\Pi_{s}
, giving (1).
Now suppose
\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and rewrite
Theorem

5.8
in terms of the unnormalized overlaps:

(5.110)

\tilde{\mu}_{p}(t)\tilde{\mu}_{-p}(t)=\tfrac{1}{d_{j}+1}.

Applying
g
gives
\mu_{p}(t)\mu_{-p}(t)=\frac{1}{d_{j}+1}
, which is (2).
As in (
6.21
), we have

(5.111)

\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\,\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)\text{ for }\mathbf{p}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right).

By the assumption of
Conjecture

1.36
, we have

(5.112)

\left|g(\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right))\right|=1.

Therefore, writing
g(\sqrt{d_{j}+1})=\pm\sqrt{d_{j}+1}
and using the fact that
\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
is a root of unity and indeed a power of
\xi_{d}
, we have

(5.113)

\mu_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\pm\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\,\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)^{k_{g}}g(\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)).

Taking absolute values, we obtain
\left|\mu_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\right|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}
, which is (3).
∎

6.
Proof of main theorems (2): Class fields attained

We now prove results about which abelian extensions are generated by
r
-SICs according to our conjectural framework.

In
Section

6.3
, we prove results about realizing the field
E_{t}
associated to an admissible tuple
t
as a particular abelian extension of the associated field
K
. We prove
Theorem

1.48
, showing that
E_{t}
is an abelian extension of
K
. We also prove
Theorem

1.49
, relating
E_{t}
to a particular ray class field.
Theorem

6.7
provides a strengthening of
Theorem

1.49
, and
Conjecture

6.8
provides a strengthening of
Conjecture

1.50
.

In
Section

6.4
, we prove results on obtaining arbitrary abelian extensions of a real quadratic field
K
from
r
-SICs.
Theorem

6.14
is an unconditional number-theoretic result on the containment of certain abelian extensions in certain ray class fields, based primarily on a technical elementary number theory result given as
Theorem

6.13
. As a corollary, we obtain
Theorem

1.51
, asserting under Stark–Tate that, when the trace of the fundamental unit of
K
is odd, the fields
E_{t}
are cofinal in the set of all abelian extensions.

We need some preliminary material to set the stage for the proofs of our main theorems on SIC-generated class fields.
Section

6.1
defines three important fields,
E_{s}^{(1)}
,
E_{t}^{(2)}
, and
E_{t}
, attached to a fiducial datum
s=(t,g,G)
.
Section

6.2
proves some key lemmas showing nontriviality of certain extensions of ray class fields.

6.1.
Discussion of SIC fields

Recall that for an
r
-SIC projector
\Pi
, the
SIC field

E_{\Pi}
was defined in
Definition

1.10
to be the field generated over
\mathbb{Q}
by the overlaps
\Tr(\Pi D_{\mathbf{p}})
and the
\bar{d}
-th root of unity
\xi_{d}
. This field is called the
extended projector SIC field
in
[
74
, Defn. 3.1]
; in that paper, it is compared to several other fields that can be associated to a SIC. The literature on SICs contains multiple definitions of fields associated to a SIC, many of which are conjecturally equal but not proven to be so; see
[
74
, Sec. 3]
for further discussion. In this paper, the
SIC field
is always
E_{\Pi}
as defined in
Definition

1.10
.

We also describe fields associated to an admissible tuple
t
or a fiducial datum
s
. Unlike the definition of the SIC field, the formal definition of these fields is in terms of (quantities defined from) special values of the Shintani–Faddeev modular cocycle, not in terms of a SIC.

For an admissible tuple
t
, an associated field
E_{t}
was defined in
Definition

1.40
. We expand that definition to give three fields associated to a fiducial datum
s
or an admissible tuple
t
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
s=(d,r,Q,g,G)\sim(K,j,m,Q,g,G)
be a fiducial datum, and let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
be the corresponding admissible tuple. Define the following fields.

(1)

E_{s}^{(1)}
is the field generated over
\mathbb{Q}
by the numbers
\{\mu_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\colon 0\leq p_{1},p_{2}<d,\,\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}\}
.

(2)

E_{t}^{(2)}
is the field generated over
\mathbb{Q}
by the numbers
\{\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\colon 0\leq p_{1},p_{2}<d,\,\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}\}
.

(3)

E_{t}
is the field generated over
\mathbb{Q}
by the numbers
\{\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\colon 0\leq p_{1},p_{2}<d,\,\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}\}
together with
\xi_{d}
.

(4)

\hat{E}_{t}
is the Galois closure (within
\mathbb{C}
) of the compositum of
K
and
E_{t}
.

Recall that for
\mathbf{p}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
we have by
Lemma

1.44
the formula

(6.1)

\mu_{\mathbf{p}}(s)=g\!\left(\tilde{\mu}_{GH_{g}^{-1}G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}(t)\right)

relating the live and ghost overlaps, with
GH_{g}^{-1}G^{-1}\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
. It follows that
E_{s}^{(1)}=g(E_{t}^{(2)})
.

We have defined three types of fields—SIC fields, fields associated to fiducial data, and ray class fields of orders (the latter in
Section

2.1
)—with no unconditional relationship between them. The remainder of this section concerns the strong conditional relationships and the strong empirical relationships between these three types of fields.

6.2.
Lemmas about class fields

We now turn our attention to ray class fields of orders.
For comparable level data
\mathcal{L}=(\mathcal{O};\mathfrak{m},\Sigma)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{O}^{\prime};\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
, with
\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
,
\mathfrak{m}\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
, and
\Sigma\supseteq\Sigma^{\prime}
, there is a quotient map
\phi:\Cl_{\mathfrak{m},\Sigma}(\mathcal{O})\twoheadrightarrow\Cl_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
and a corresponding field extension
H_{\mathfrak{m},\Sigma}^{\mathcal{O}}/H_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}
with
\Gal(H_{\mathfrak{m},\Sigma}^{\mathcal{O}}/H_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}})\cong\ker(\phi)
. In order to describe the structure of this kernel, we introduce the
\U
-group notation from
[
73
]
for unit groups with congruence and “ray” restrictions.

Definition 6.2
.

For a commutative ring with unity
R
and an ideal
I
of
R
, define the group

(6.2)

\U_{I}(R):=\{\alpha\in R^{\times}:\alpha\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,I\right)\}=(1+I)\cap R^{\times}.

If
R
has real embeddings, and
\Sigma
is a subset of the real embeddings of
R
, define

(6.3)

\U_{I,\Sigma}(R):=\{\alpha\in R^{\times}:\alpha\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,I\right)\mbox{ and }\rho(\alpha)>0\mbox{ for }\rho\in\Sigma\}.

The following exact sequence resolves the map
\phi:\Cl_{\mathfrak{m},\Sigma}(\mathcal{O})\twoheadrightarrow\Cl_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
, describing
\ker(\phi)\cong\coker(\lambda)
for a reduction map
\lambda
from a certain global unit group to a certain
\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\right)
unit group.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
K
be a number field, and consider level data
\mathcal{L}=(\mathcal{O};\mathfrak{m},\Sigma)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{O}^{\prime};\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
for
K
such that
\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
,
\mathfrak{m}\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
, and
\Sigma\supseteq\Sigma^{\prime}
.
Let
\mathfrak{d}
be any
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
-ideal such that
\mathfrak{d}\subseteq\left(\mathfrak{m}:\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\right)
.
We have the following exact sequence.

(6.4)

1\to\frac{\U_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}}\!\left(\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\right)}{\U_{\mathfrak{m},\Sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{O}\right)}\xrightarrow{\lambda}\frac{\U_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\!\left(\mathcal{O}^{\prime}/\mathfrak{d}\right)}{\U_{\mathfrak{m}}\!\left(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{d}\right)}\times\{\pm 1\}^{|\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}|}\xrightarrow{\psi}\Cl_{\mathfrak{m},\Sigma}(\mathcal{O})\xrightarrow{\phi}\Cl_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})\to 1.

Proof.

See
[
73
, Thm. 6.5]
.
∎

The exact sequence (
6.4
) gives us the most concrete description of
\Gal(H_{\mathfrak{m},\Sigma}^{\mathcal{O}}/H_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}})
when the “global units” term
\frac{\U_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}}\!\left(\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\right)}{\U_{\mathfrak{m},\Sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{O}\right)}
is the trivial group. More generally, we need to know something about the global units to use (
6.4
) effectively.

We now turn our attention to real quadratic fields and connect the
\U
-groups to stability groups of
2\times 2
matrices by way of the canonical representations
\chi_{Q}
from
Section

4
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Fix a real quadratic field
K
of discriminant
\Delta_{0}
, and consider any integers
d\geq 3
and
f\geq 1
.
Let
Q
be any primitive binary quadratic form of discriminant
f^{2}\Delta_{0}
, and consider the canonical ring homomorphism
\chi_{Q}
defined by
Definition

4.32
.
Then

(6.5)

\chi_{Q}(d\mathcal{O}_{f})=\chi_{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{f})\cap d\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Z}),

where, following
Definition

4.26
,
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Z})
is the ring of
2\times 2
matrices with integer entries.
Moreover, we have the following equalities of subgroups of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(6.6)

\displaystyle\chi_{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{f}^{\times})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}(Q).

(6.7)

\displaystyle\chi_{Q}(\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{f},\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{f}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{d}(Q).

Proof.

We first prove (
6.5
).
The inclusion
\chi_{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{f})\subseteq\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Z})
holds by
Theorem

4.34
; thus,
\chi_{Q}(d\mathcal{O}_{f})\subseteq\chi_{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{f})\cap d\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Z})
. To prove the reverse inclusion, suppose
M\in\chi_{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{f})\cap d\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Z})
, write
Q=\langle a,b,c\rangle
, and write

(6.8)

M=\chi_{Q}(x+y\sqrt{\Delta_{0}})=xI+\frac{2y}{f}SQ=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}x-\frac{by}{f}&-\frac{2cy}{f}\\
\frac{2ay}{f}&x+\frac{by}{f}\end{array}\right)

for
x,y\in\mathbb{Q}
.
It follows that
\frac{2ay}{f}\in d\mathbb{Z}
,
\frac{2cy}{f}\in d\mathbb{Z}
, and
\frac{2by}{f}=(x+\frac{by}{f})-(x-\frac{by}{f})\in d\mathbb{Z}
. Because
Q
is primitive,
\gcd(a,b,c)=1
, so
\frac{2y}{f}\in d\mathbb{Z}
; that is,
y\in\frac{df}{2}\mathbb{Z}
.
We complete the proof of (
6.5
) by cases.

Case 1:
Suppose
2\!\mid\!f^{2}\Delta_{0}
.
Then, since
b^{2}-4ac=f^{2}\Delta_{0}
, it follows that
b
is even. Thus,
\frac{by}{f}\in d\mathbb{Z}
. It follows from (
6.8
) that
x-\frac{by}{f}\in d\mathbb{Z}
, and thus,
x=(x-\frac{by}{f})+b\frac{y}{f}\in d\mathbb{Z}
. In this case,

(6.9)

d\mathcal{O}_{f}=\left\{x+y\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}:x\in d\mathbb{Z},y\in\tfrac{df}{2}\mathbb{Z}\right\},

and so
x+y\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}\in d\mathcal{O}_{f}
.

Case 2:
Suppose
2\!\nmid\!f^{2}\Delta_{0}
.
Since
b^{2}-4ac=f^{2}\Delta_{0}
, it follows that
b
is odd; also,
f
is odd. It follows from (
6.8
) that
x-\frac{by}{f}\in d\mathbb{Z}
.
Thus,

x=(x-\frac{by}{f})+b\frac{y}{f}\in\frac{d}{2}\mathbb{Z}
,
and
x-y=(x-\frac{by}{f})+(f-b)\frac{y}{f}\in d\mathbb{Z}
. In this case,

(6.10)

d\mathcal{O}_{f}=\left\{x+y\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}:x\in\tfrac{d}{2}\mathbb{Z},y\in\tfrac{df}{2}\mathbb{Z},x-y\in d\mathbb{Z}\right\},

and so
x+y\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}\in d\mathcal{O}_{f}
.

Both case being proven, the proof of (
6.5
) is complete.
Equation (
6.6
) is a restatement of
Theorem

4.43
(2), which we have already proven. It remains to prove (
6.7
).

We first show that
\chi_{Q}(\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{f},\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{f}))
is a subgroup of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.
Consider any
\eta\in\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{f},\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{f})
.
If
\eta^{\prime}
is the nontrivial Galois conjugate of
\eta
, then
\eta\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\mathcal{O}_{1}\right)
and
\eta^{\prime}\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\mathcal{O}_{1}\right)
, so
\Nm(\eta)=\eta\eta^{\prime}\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\mathcal{O}_{1}\right)
. But
\Nm(\eta)=\pm 1
because
\eta
is a unit, and
d\geq 3
, so
\Nm(\eta)=1
.
Thus,
\det(\chi_{Q}(\eta))=\Nm(\eta)=1
. So
\chi_{Q}(\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{f},\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{f}))
is a subgroup of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

We have
\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{f},\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{f})=\mathcal{O}_{f}^{\times}\cap(1+d\mathcal{O}_{f})
.
Because
\chi_{Q}
is an injective homomorphism, it follows that
\chi_{Q}(\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{f},\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{f}))=\chi_{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{f}^{\times})\cap(I+\chi_{Q}(d\mathcal{O}_{f}))
. Thus, by (
6.5
) and (
6.6
),
\chi_{Q}(\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{f},\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{f}))=\mathcal{S}(Q)\cap d\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Z})
. But since
\chi_{Q}(\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{f},\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{f}))
is a subgroup of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, in fact
\chi_{Q}(\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{f},\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{f}))=\mathcal{S}(Q)\cap\Gamma(d)=\mathcal{S}_{d}(Q)
.
∎

The focus now narrows to the ray class fields of interest for the our construction of
r
-SICs. We will fix a real quadratic field
K
and associate to it the sequence of conductors
f_{j}
from
Definition

1.23
and the dimension grid
d_{j,m}
and rank grid
r_{j,m}
from
Definition

1.24
.

The following lemma is the key technical result on global units that will allow us some control over the behavior of the ray class groups and ray class fields of interest for
r
-SICs. It generalizes
[
71
, Lem. 5.3]
and
[
74
, Lem. B.2]
by using
Lemma

6.4
together with results proven in
Section

4
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Fix a real quadratic field
K
of discriminant
\Delta_{0}
.
Let
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
d=d_{j,m}
and
f\!\mid\!f_{j}
.
Let
\Sigma
be a subset of the real embeddings of
K
.
Then

(6.11)

\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{f},\Sigma}(\mathcal{O}_{f})=\langle\varepsilon_{d_{j}}^{2m+1}\rangle.

Proof.

Write
\varepsilon_{d_{j}}=\varepsilon^{j}
, where
\varepsilon
is the smallest totally positive unit of
K
with
\varepsilon>1
.
By (
4.90
) of
Lemma

4.23
,

(6.12)

\varepsilon_{d_{j}}^{2m+1}-1=\varepsilon^{(2m+1)j}-1=d_{j,m}\varepsilon^{mj}(\varepsilon^{j}-1).

Also,
\varepsilon_{d_{j}}\in\mathcal{O}_{f_{j}}
. Thus,
\varepsilon_{d_{j}}^{2m+1}\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d_{j,m}\mathcal{O}_{f_{j}}\right)
, so

(6.13)

\varepsilon_{d_{j}}^{2m+1}\in\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{f},\{\infty_{1},\infty_{2}\}}(\mathcal{O}_{f}).

It follows that
\varepsilon_{d_{j}}^{2m+1}\in\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{f_{j}},\Sigma}(\mathcal{O}_{f_{j}})
, because
\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{f},\{\infty_{1},\infty_{2}\}}(\mathcal{O}_{f})\subseteq\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{f_{j}},\Sigma}(\mathcal{O}_{f_{j}})
.

Since
\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{f_{j}},\Sigma}(\mathcal{O}_{f_{j}})\subseteq\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{1},\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{1})
, It suffices to show that
\varepsilon_{d_{j}}
generates
\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{1},\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{1})
, which we will now do.

Let
Q
be any primitive binary quadratic form of discriminant
\Delta_{0}
, and consider the canonical ring homomorphism
\chi_{Q}
defined by
Definition

4.32
. By (
6.7
) of
Lemma

6.4
,

(6.14)

\chi_{Q}(\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{1},\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{1}))=\mathcal{S}_{d}(Q).

By (
4.194
) of
Theorem

4.50
,

(6.15)

\mathcal{S}_{d}(Q)=\langle A_{t}\rangle=\left\langle\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon_{d_{j}}^{2m+1})\right\rangle=\chi_{Q}\!\left(\langle\varepsilon_{d_{j}}^{2m+1}\rangle\right).

It follows from (
6.14
) and (
6.15
), and the fact that
\chi_{Q}
is an injective homomorphism, that
\U_{d\mathcal{O}_{1},\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{1})=\langle\varepsilon_{d_{j}}^{2m+1}\rangle
.
∎

As a consequence, we deduce the following lemma showing that varying the set of infinite primes in the ray class modulus produces distinct ray class groups and ray class fields.

Lemma 6.6
.

Fix a real quadratic field
K
.
Let
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
d=d_{j,m}
,
f\!\mid\!f_{j}
, and
d^{\prime}\in\{d,\bar{d}\}
.
The all the group homomorphism shown in the following diagram are
2
-to-
1
surjections.

(6.16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cl_{d^{\prime}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}\!(\mathcal{O}_{f})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cl_{d^{\prime}\infty_{1}}\!(\mathcal{O}_{f})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cl_{d^{\prime}\infty_{2}}\!(\mathcal{O}_{f})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cl_{d^{\prime}}\!(\mathcal{O}_{f})}

In the following field diagram, all of the extensions have degree
2
.

(6.17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{d^{\prime}\infty_{1},\infty_{2}}^{\mathcal{O}_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{d^{\prime}\infty_{1}}^{\mathcal{O}_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{d^{\prime}\infty_{2}}^{\mathcal{O}_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{d^{\prime}\mathcal{O}}^{\mathcal{O}_{f}}}

Proof.

We first prove the claim in the case
d^{\prime}=d
.
Let
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{f}
.
Let
\Sigma^{\prime},\Sigma
be subsets of the set of real embeddings of
K
.
We apply the exact sequence of
[
73
, Thm. 6.5]
with level data
\mathcal{L}=(\mathcal{O};d^{\prime}\mathcal{O},\Sigma)
and
\mathcal{L^{\prime}}=(\mathcal{O};d^{\prime}\mathcal{O},\emptyset)
and with
\mathfrak{d}=d\mathcal{O}
:

(6.18)

1\to\frac{\U_{d\mathcal{O},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O})}{\U_{d\mathcal{O},\Sigma}(\mathcal{O})}\to\frac{\U_{d\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}/d\mathcal{O})}{\U_{d\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}/d\mathcal{O})}\times\{\pm 1\}^{\left|\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}\right|}\to\Cl_{d\mathcal{O},\Sigma}(\mathcal{O})\to\Cl_{d\mathcal{O},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O})\to 1.

By
Lemma

6.5
, the “global units” term is

(6.19)

\frac{\U_{d\mathcal{O},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O})}{\U_{d\mathcal{O},\Sigma}(\mathcal{O})}=\frac{\langle\varepsilon_{d_{j}}^{2m+1}\rangle}{\langle\varepsilon_{d_{j}}^{2m+1}\rangle}=1.

We may thus rewrite
Equation

6.18
as the short exact sequence

(6.20)

1\to\{\pm 1\}^{\left|\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}\right|}\to\Cl_{d\mathcal{O},\Sigma}(\mathcal{O})\to\Cl_{d\mathcal{O},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O})\to 1.

It follows that the ray class groups

\Cl_{d}(\mathcal{O})=\Cl_{d\mathcal{O},\emptyset}(\mathcal{O})
,

\Cl_{d\infty_{1}}(\mathcal{O})=\Cl_{d\mathcal{O},\{\infty_{1}\}}(\mathcal{O})
,

\Cl_{d\infty_{2}}(\mathcal{O})=\Cl_{d\mathcal{O},\{\infty_{2}\}}(\mathcal{O})
,

\Cl_{d\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}(\mathcal{O})=\Cl_{d\mathcal{O},\{\infty_{1},\infty_{2}\}}(\mathcal{O})

are all distinct and have the
2
-to-
1
surjections shown in (
6.16
).
The fact that the field extensions in the ray class field diamond (
6.17
) have degree
2
follows by
Theorem

2.2
.

In the case
d^{\prime}=\bar{d}
, note that since
\U_{d\mathcal{O},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O})=\U_{d\mathcal{O},\Sigma}(\mathcal{O})
, it follows that
\U_{\bar{d}\mathcal{O},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O})=\U_{\bar{d}\mathcal{O},\Sigma}(\mathcal{O})
, because the latter two groups are subgroups of the former obtained by imposing the condition
u\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\bar{d}\right)
. Therefore, the same proof applies.
∎

6.3.
SIC fields as class fields

Theorem

1.48
is a straightforward consequence of our conjectures about the RM values of the Shintani–Faddeev cocycle.

Proof of
Theorem

1.48
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
be an admissible tuple. We wish to show that
E_{t}
is an abelian extension of
K
.

The field
E_{t}
is defined to be the field generated over
\mathbb{Q}
by the candidate ghost overlaps
\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
together with
\xi_{d}
. The candidate ghost overlaps are
\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{0}}(t)=1
and

(6.21)

\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\,\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\,\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)\text{ for }\mathbf{p}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right).

Note that
\sqrt{d_{j}+1}
and
\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
(a root of unity) are both contained in abelian extensions of
\mathbb{Q}
, so in particular they are both are contained in an abelian extension of
K
. By the conditional assumption (of
Conjecture

1.37
when
Q
is fundamental and
Conjecture

1.38
when
Q
is not fundamental),
\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)
is also in an abelian extension of
K
. We have

(6.22)

E_{t}\subseteq K\!\left(\sqrt{d_{j}+1},\xi_{d},\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t),\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right):0\leq p_{1},p_{2}<d\right),

and the right-hand field is abelian over
K
, so
E_{t}
is abelian over
K
.
∎

We prove the following theorem, which implies
Theorem

1.49
.

Theorem 6.7
.

Assume
Conjecture

2.8
(the Stark–Tate Conjecture).
Let
s=(d,r,Q,G,g)\sim(K,j,m,Q,G,g)
be a fiducial datum,
let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
,
and let
d=d_{j,m}
.
Suppose that
\disc(Q)
is fundamental.
Choose
\mathbf{p}_{1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p_{11}\\
p_{12}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
such that
(p_{12}\rho_{t}-p_{11})\mathcal{O}_{1}
is coprime to
d\mathcal{O}_{1}
as
\mathcal{O}_{1}
-ideals. (For example, one may take
\mathbf{p}_{1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.)

(1)

The ray class field
H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d\infty_{1}}

is equal to the field extension of
K
generated by the numbers
\{\mu_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\colon 0\leq p_{1},p_{2}<d,\,\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}\}
and is also equal to
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{\mathbf{p_{1}}}(t)^{2})
.
The field
E_{s}^{(1)}\supseteq H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d\infty_{1}}\supseteq K
, the extension
E_{s}^{(1)}/K
is ramified at
\infty_{1}
and unramified
\infty_{2}
, and field
E_{s}^{(1)}
depends only on the pair
(d,r)
.

(2)

The ray class field
H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d\infty_{2}}

is equal to the field extension of
K
generated by the numbers
\{\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)^{2}\colon 0\leq p_{1},p_{2}<d,\,\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}\}
and is also equal to
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p_{1}}}(t)^{2})
.
The field
E_{t}^{(2)}\supseteq H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d\infty_{2}}\supseteq K
, the extension
E_{t}^{(2)}/K
is unramified at
\infty_{1}
and ramified
\infty_{2}
, and field
E_{t}^{(2)}
depends only on the pair
(d,r)
.

(3)

The ray class field
H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{\bar{d}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}

is equal to the field extension of
K
generated by the numbers
\{\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)^{2}\colon 0\leq p_{1},p_{2}<d,\,\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}\}
together with
\xi_{d}
, and it is also equal to
K(\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p_{1}}}(t)^{2},\xi_{d})
.
The field
E_{t}\supseteq H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{\bar{d}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}\supseteq K
, the extension
E_{t}/K
is ramified at both infinite places of
K
, and field
E_{t}
depends only on the pair
(d,r)
.

(4)

Assume
Conjecture

1.35
. Then the SIC field
E_{\Pi_{s}}=E_{s}^{(1)}(\xi_{d})\supseteq H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{\bar{d}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}\supseteq K
.

Proof.

We will prove (2) first, and the others will follow.

Consider
\mathbf{p}\in\left(\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z}\right)^{2}
. As in the proof of
Theorem

5.8
, let
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathbf{p}}
be the unique class in
\overline{\Clm}^{\flat}_{d\infty_{2}}(\mathcal{O}_{1})
that maps to the
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-orbit of
(d^{-1}\mathbf{p},\rho_{t})
under the map
\Upsilon_{d\mathcal{O}_{1}}
described in
[
72
, Thm. 3.12]
and at the end of
Section

2.6
.
By
Equation

5.60
, we have for

(6.23)

(d_{j}+1)\ (\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t))^{2}=(\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t))^{2}=\exp\!\left(nZ_{d\infty_{2}}^{\prime}(0,\mathfrak{A}_{\mathbf{p}})\right)=u_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathbf{p}}}^{-n_{\mathbf{p}}}

for some
n_{\mathbf{p}}\in\{1,2\}
and
u_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathbf{p}}}=\exp\!\left(-Z_{d\infty_{2}}^{\prime}(0,\mathfrak{A}_{\mathbf{p}})\right)
.
By
Proposition

2.10
, our conditional assumptions imply
\MS(\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathfrak{m})
(
Conjecture

2.9
) for all nonzero
\mathcal{O}_{1}
-ideals
\mathfrak{m}
such that
\mathfrak{m}\neq\mathcal{O}_{1}
. In particular, they imply that
u_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathbf{p}}}\in H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d\infty_{2}}
. It follows that
(\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t))^{2}\in H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d\infty_{2}}
, or equivalently
(\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t))^{2}\in H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d\infty_{2}}
.

Now restrict to the special case of
\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{p}_{1}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Let
u:=u_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathbf{p}_{1}}}
.
By definition,
n_{\mathbf{p}_{1}}=\frac{2}{\left|\phi^{-1}(\mathfrak{A}_{\mathbf{p}_{1}})\right|}
, where
\phi:\overline{\Clm}^{\flat}_{\mathfrak{m}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\to\overline{\Clm}^{\flat}_{\mathfrak{m}\infty_{2}}(\mathcal{O}_{1})
is the natural quotient map.
The class
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathbf{p}_{1}}
is primitive, so
\left|\phi^{-1}(\mathfrak{A}_{\mathbf{p}_{1}})\right|
is equal to the cardinality of the kernel of the map
\Cl_{\mathfrak{m}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\to\Cl_{\mathfrak{m}\infty_{2}}(\mathcal{O}_{1})
, which by
Lemma

6.6
is
2
. Thus
n_{\mathbf{p}_{1}}=\frac{2}{2}=1
, and (
6.23
) becomes
(\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}_{1}}(t))^{2}=\varepsilon^{-1}
.
Because
f=1
and
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathbf{p}_{1}}
is primitive, the number
\varepsilon
is a Stark unit in the original sense of
[
101
]
. The nontriviality of the maps between the ray class groups with different ramification at infinite places shown in
Lemma

6.6
implies the non-vanishing condition in the hypotheses of
[
101
, Thm. 1]
. Applying that theorem, which says that the Stark unit generates the ray class field over the rational numbers, we obtain
\mathbb{Q}((\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}_{1}}(t))^{2})=\mathbb{Q}(\varepsilon)=H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d\infty_{2}}
.

The claim that
E_{t}^{(2)}\supseteq H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d\infty_{2}}
follows by the definition of
E_{t}^{(2)}
. The field
H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d\infty_{2}}
is always unramified at
\infty_{1}
, and it is ramified at
\infty_{2}
if and only if it is a nontrivial extension of
H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d}
, which is true by
Lemma

6.6
. The field
E_{t}^{(2)}
is obtained from
H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d\infty_{2}}
by adjoining square roots of numbers
(\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t))^{2}
that are positive in the first real embedding, so it remains unramified at
\infty_{1}
. It also follows from the “Artin map action” part of
Conjecture

2.9
, together with (
6.23
), that
\Gal(H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d\infty_{2}}/K)
permutes the
(\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t))^{2}
with the ghost overlaps
(\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime}))^{2}
of all
t^{\prime}=(d,r,Q^{\prime})
such that
\disc(Q^{\prime})
is fundamental. Thus
E_{t}^{(2)}
depends only on the pair
(d,r)
.

Claim (1) follows from (2), because
H_{d\infty_{1}}^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}=g(H_{d\infty_{2}}^{\mathcal{O}_{1}})
and
E_{s}^{(1)}=g(E_{t}^{(2)})
, and using
Lemma

1.44
; independence from
g
follows from the fact (given by
Theorem

1.48
) that
E_{t}^{(2)}/K
is abelian, so
E_{t}^{(2)}
can have no more that two conjugate fields over
\mathbb{Q}
, those being
E_{s}^{(1)}
and
E_{t}^{(2))}
. Claim (3) also follows, because
H_{\bar{d}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}=H_{d\infty_{1}}^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}\!(\xi_{d})=H_{d\infty_{2}}^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}\!(\xi_{d})
and
E_{t}=E_{t}^{(2)}(\xi_{d})
.

Additionally, if one assumes
Conjecture

1.35
, then
\Pi_{s}
is a SIC fiducial projector by
Theorem

1.46
, so
E_{\Pi_{s}}
is well-defined. Then it follows from the definition of each that
E_{\Pi_{s}}=E_{s}^{(1)}(\xi_{d})
, and so claim (4) follows from (1).
∎

Proof of
Theorem

1.49
.

This is
Theorem

6.7
(3).
∎

Theorem

6.7
does not pin down the fields
E_{s}^{(1)}
,
E_{t}^{(2)}
, and
E_{t}
precisely but only says that they are abelian extensions containing certain ray class fields. This is because the Stark–Tate Conjecture does not provide a precise description of the field generated by the square root of a Stark unit as a particular subfield of a ray class field.
Theorem

6.7
also applies only in the case of fundamental discriminants, because the theory of ray class partial zeta functions for non-maximal orders is insufficiently developed at present to provide the analogue of
[
101
, Thm. 1]
.

Nevertheless, the numerical data (discussed further in
Sections

7
and
8
as well as in
[
74
]
) supports a precise prediction about the structure of the fields
E_{s}^{(1)}
,
E_{t}^{(2)}
, and
E_{t}
; this prediction includes the forms of non-fundamental discriminant. We presented the prediction as a conjecture. We note that this conjecture implies
Conjecture

1.50
and also predicts the shape of the fields
\hat{E}_{t}
and
E_{\Pi_{s}}
.

Conjecture 6.8
.

Let
s=(d,r,Q,G,g)\sim(K,j,m,Q,G,g)
be a fiducial datum,
let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
,
let
d=d_{j,m}
,
and let
f
be the conductor of
Q
. Then:

(1)

E_{s}^{(1)}=H^{\mathcal{O}_{f}}_{\bar{d}\infty_{1}}
.

(2)

E_{t}^{(2)}=H^{\mathcal{O}_{f}}_{\bar{d}\infty_{2}}
.

(3)

E_{t}=H^{\mathcal{O}_{f}}_{\bar{d}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}
.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
s=(d,r,Q,G,g)\sim(K,j,m,Q,G,g)
be a fiducial datum, and let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
.

(1)

Assuming
Conjecture

6.8
, one has
\hat{E}_{t}=E_{t}
, and
Conjecture

1.50
follows.

(2)

Assuming
Conjecture

6.8
and
Conjecture

1.35
, one has
E_{\Pi_{s}}=E_{t}
.

Proof.

The field
H^{\mathcal{O}_{f}}_{\bar{d}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}
contains
K
and is Galois over
\mathbb{Q}
; thus, by
Conjecture

6.8
,
\hat{E}_{t}=E_{t}
.
It is then clear that
Conjecture

1.50
follows by
Conjecture

6.8
(3).
Additionally, if one assumes
Conjecture

1.35
, then
\Pi_{s}
is a SIC fiducial projector by
Theorem

1.46
, so
E_{\Pi_{s}}
is well-defined. Then it follows from the definition of each that
E_{\Pi_{s}}=E_{s}^{(1)}(\xi_{d})
, and so by
Conjecture

6.8
, the SIC field
E_{\Pi_{s}}=H^{\mathcal{O}_{f}}_{\bar{d}\infty_{1}}(\xi_{d})=H^{\mathcal{O}_{f}}_{\bar{d}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}=E_{t}
.
∎

6.4.
The set of SIC-generated abelian extensions

We now examine the implications of our conjectural framework for the generation of arbitrary abelian extensions of real quadratic fields.
We begin with two preliminary results.

Definition 6.10
.

For any prime number
p
and any
r\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
, denote by
v_{p}(r)
the
p
-adic valuation
of
r
; that is, if
r=p^{e}\frac{a}{b}
with
p\!\nmid\!a
and
p\!\nmid\!b
, then
v_{p}(r)=e
.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
p,r,e\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
p
is prime and
1\leq r\leq p^{e}
. Then the following statements of about the
p
-adic valuations of binomial coefficients hold:

(6.24)

\displaystyle v_{p}\!\left(\binom{p^{e}}{r}\right)

\displaystyle=e-v_{p}(r),

(6.25)

\displaystyle v_{p}\!\left(p^{r}\binom{p^{e}}{r}\right)

\displaystyle\geq e+1.

Proof.

We prove (
6.24
) by induction. The statement is immediate if
r=1
. Suppose it is true for arbitrary
1\leq r<p^{e}
. Then

(6.26)

\displaystyle v_{p}\!\left(\binom{p^{e}}{r+1}\right)

\displaystyle=v_{p}\!\left(\frac{p^{e}-r}{r+1}\binom{p^{e}}{r}\right)=e-v_{p}(r)+v_{p}(p^{e}-r)-v_{p}(r+1).

Since
v_{p}(p^{e}-r)=v_{p}(r)
, it follows that

(6.27)

\displaystyle v_{p}\!\left(\binom{p^{e}}{r+1}\right)

\displaystyle=e-v_{p}(r+1).

Equation (
6.25
) follows from (
6.24
) and the fact that
r-v_{p}(r)\geq 1
.
∎

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
\varepsilon
be the totally positive fundamental unit of the quadratic field
K
and
d_{1}=\frac{\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{-1}}{2}+1
the associated root dimension.
The order of
\varepsilon+2\mathcal{O}_{K}
as an element of
\left(\mathcal{O}_{K}/2\mathcal{O}_{K}\right)^{\times}
is

(6.28)

\displaystyle\#\!\left\langle\varepsilon+2\mathcal{O}_{K}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,\quad&\text{if $d_{1}\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)$},\\
2,\quad&\text{if $d_{1}\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)$},\\
3,\quad&\text{if $d_{1}$ is even}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Suppose
d_{1}=4n+3
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

(6.29)

\displaystyle\varepsilon+2\mathcal{O}_{K}

\displaystyle=\frac{4n+2+4\sqrt{n(n+1)}}{2}+2\mathcal{O}_{K}=1+2\mathcal{O}_{K}.

Suppose
d_{1}=4n+1
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\displaystyle\qquad\varepsilon+2\mathcal{O}_{K}

\displaystyle=\frac{4n+\sqrt{(4n-2)(4n+2)}}{2}+2\mathcal{O}_{K}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{4n^{2}-1}+2\mathcal{O}_{K}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\qquad\varepsilon^{2}+2\mathcal{O}_{K}

\displaystyle=4n^{2}-1+2\mathcal{O}_{K}

(6.30)

\displaystyle=1+2\mathcal{O}_{K}.

Finally, suppose
d_{1}
is even. Then

(6.31)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{j}-1

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{j}-3-f_{j}\Delta_{0}}{2}+f_{j}\left(\frac{\Delta_{0}+\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right).

So
\varepsilon^{j}\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\mathcal{O}_{K}\right)
if and only if
(d_{j}-3-f_{j}\Delta_{0})/2
and
f_{j}
are both even. It follows from Lemma
4.10
that
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
are both odd, implying that the order is greater than 2. It also follows that
f_{3}
is even and
\Delta_{0}\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
. Using Lemma
4.9
we have

(6.32)

\displaystyle d_{3}-3-f_{3}\Delta_{0}

\displaystyle=d_{1}^{2}(d_{1}-3)+f_{1}d_{1}(d_{1}-2)\Delta_{0}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right).

Hence
\varepsilon^{3}\in 1+2\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
∎

Theorem 6.13
.

Let
d
be any positive integer, and let
r
be the order of
\varepsilon+d\mathcal{O}_{K}
as an element of
\left(\mathcal{O}_{K}/d\mathcal{O}_{K}\right)^{\times}
, where
\varepsilon
is the totally positive fundamental unit of the quadratic field
K
. Write
d
,
r
in the form

(6.33)

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=2^{\ell_{1}}(2a+1),

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=2^{\ell_{2}}(2b+1),

for suitable non-negative integers
\ell_{1}
,
\ell_{2}
,
a
,
b
. Let
\ell=\max\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}
, and let
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
be such that

(6.34)

\displaystyle 2^{\ell}

\displaystyle\!\mid\!j,

\displaystyle(2a+1)(2b+1)

\displaystyle\!\mid\!(2m+1).

If
d
is even assume in addition that
j
is coprime to 3 and
2m+1
is a multiple of 3. Then

(1)

If
d_{1}
is even, then
d\!\mid\!d_{j,m}
,

(2)

If
d_{1}
is odd, then
d\!\mid\!d_{j,m}
if and only if
d
is odd.

Proof.

Let
p^{e}
be any element in the prime decomposition of
2a+1
. Since
j(2b+1)\!\mid\!r

(6.35)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{j(2b+1)}

\displaystyle=1+dz

for some
z\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
. It then follows from Lemma
6.11
that

(6.36)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{jp^{e}(2b+1)}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{t=1}^{p^{e}}(dz)^{t}\binom{p^{e}}{t}=1+p^{e}dzz^{\prime}=1+p^{e}(\varepsilon^{j(2b+1)}-1)z^{\prime}

for some
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
. In view of Lemma
4.23
this means

(6.37)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{jp^{e}(2b+1)}

\displaystyle=1+p^{e}d_{b,j}\varepsilon^{bj}(\varepsilon^{j}-1)z^{\prime}=1+p^{e}(\varepsilon^{j}-1)z^{\prime\prime}

for some
z^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
. Since
p^{e}(2b+1)\!\mid\!(2m+1)
it follows that

(6.38)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{j(2m+1)}

\displaystyle=1+p^{e}(\varepsilon^{j}-1)z^{\prime\prime\prime}

for some
z^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
. By another application of Lemma
4.23
we deduce

\displaystyle\qquad d_{j,m}\varepsilon^{mj}(\varepsilon^{j}-1)

\displaystyle=p^{e}(\varepsilon^{j}-1)z^{\prime\prime\prime}

(6.39)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\qquad d_{j,m}

\displaystyle=p^{e}w

for some
w\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
. We conclude that
p^{e}
, and consequently
(2a+1)
divides
d_{j,m}
. If
d
is odd this proves
d\!\mid\!d_{j,m}
.

Suppose, on the other hand, that
d
is even. Suppose, first, that
d_{1}
is also even. By assumption
3\!\mid\!(2m+1)
, so it follows from Lemma
6.12
that

(6.40)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2m+1}

\displaystyle=1+2z

for some
z\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
. In view of Lemma
6.11
this means

(6.41)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{(2m+1)2^{\ell}}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{t=1}^{2^{\ell}}(2z)^{t}\binom{2^{\ell}}{t}=1+2^{\ell+1}zz^{\prime}=1+2^{\ell}(\varepsilon^{2m+1}-1)z^{\prime}

for some
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
. Since
2^{\ell}\!\mid\!j
it follows that

(6.42)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{(2m+1)j}

\displaystyle=1+2^{\ell}(\varepsilon^{2m+1}-1)z^{\prime\prime}

for some
z^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
Using Lemma
4.23
we deduce

\displaystyle\qquad d_{j,m}\varepsilon^{mj}(\varepsilon^{j}-1)

\displaystyle=2^{\ell}d_{1,m}\varepsilon^{m}(\varepsilon-1)z^{\prime\prime}

(6.43)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\qquad d_{j,m}(\varepsilon^{j}-1)

\displaystyle=2^{\ell}w

for some
w\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
. Since
j
is coprime to
3
,

(6.44)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{j}-1

\displaystyle=c_{1}+c_{2}\left(\frac{\Delta_{0}+\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right)

for
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
not both even. Since
2^{\ell}
divides both
c_{1}d_{j,m}
and
c_{2}d_{j,m}
it follows that it must divide
d_{j,m}
, which proves statement (
1
).

Suppose, on the other hand, that
d_{1}
is odd. Then it follows from Lemmas
4.10
and
4.23
that
d_{j,m}
is odd. So
d\!\nmid\!d_{j,m}
, which proves statement (
2
).
∎

Theorem 6.14
.

Let
K
be a real quadratic field of discriminant
\Delta_{0}
, let
\varepsilon
be a fundamental totally positive unit in
K
(as in
Definition

1.22
), and let
f_{j}
be as defined in
Definition

1.23
. Then

(1)

If
\Tr(\varepsilon)
is odd, then every abelian extension of
K
is contained in
H_{d_{j,m}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}
for some
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
.

(2)

If
\Tr(\varepsilon)
is even,
d
is a positive odd integer, and
f|f_{j_{0}}
for some positive integer
j_{0}
such that
3\!\nmid\!j_{0}
, then there exists some
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
H_{d\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}^{\mathcal{O}_{f}}\subseteq H_{d_{j,m}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}^{\mathcal{O}_{f}}
.

(2’)

If
\Tr(\varepsilon)
is even, then every abelian extension of
K
that is unramified at the primes of
K
lying over
2
is contained in
H_{d_{j,m}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}
for some
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

We prove (1) first. If
\Tr(\varepsilon)
is odd, then the root dimension
d_{1}
is even. Let
E
be any abelian extension of
K
. By the Takagi Existence Theorem, there is some
d\in\mathbb{N}
such that
E\subseteq H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}
. It follows from
Theorem

6.13
(1) that there are some
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d\!\mid\!d_{j,m}
. Thus,
E\subseteq H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d_{j,m}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}
.

Now we prove (2). If
