\Tr(\varepsilon)
is even, then the root dimension
d_{1}
is odd. Let
r,\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell,a,b\in\mathbb{N}
be as in the statement of
Theorem

6.13
. Set
j:=2^{\ell}j_{0}
and
2m+1:=(2a+1)(2b+1)
. Then by
Theorem

6.13
(2) we have
d\!\mid\!d_{j,m}
. Thus,
H_{d\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}^{\mathcal{O}_{f}}\subseteq H_{d_{j,m}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}^{\mathcal{O}_{f}}
.

Finally, we prove (2’). Let
E
be any abelian extension of
K
that is unramified at the primes of
K
lying over
2
. By the Takagi Existence Theorem, there is some odd
d\in\mathbb{N}
such that
E\subseteq H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}
. Choosing
f=1
in (2), the condition
f\!\mid\!f_{j_{0}}
holds for any choice of
j_{0}
, so we obtain
E\subseteq H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}\subseteq H_{d_{j,m}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}
for some
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

1.51
.

By
Theorem

1.49
, our conditional assumptions imply that, for any admissible tuple
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
, one has the field containments

(6.45)

E_{t}\supseteq H_{\bar{d}_{j,m}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}\supseteq H_{d_{j,m}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}.

Theorem

1.51
thus follows from
Theorem

6.14
.
∎

For real quadratic fields
K
satisfying the hypothesis of
Theorem

6.14
(1), namely that
\Tr(\varepsilon)
is odd,
r
-SICs have the potential to provide a full solution to Hilbert’s twelfth problem. The fields
H^{\mathcal{O}_{1}}_{d_{j,m}\infty_{1}\infty_{2}}
contain every abelian extension of
K
. By
Theorem

6.7
(4), these fields “SIC-generated” in the sense that they are contained in the SIC field
E_{\Pi_{s}}
of a SIC fiducial
\Pi_{s}
, under the assumptions of
Conjecture

2.8
and
Conjecture

1.35
. Proofs of
Conjecture

2.8
and
Conjecture

1.35
could thus be considered a geometric, complex-analytic solution to Hilbert’s twelfth problem for real quadratic fields with a unit of odd trace.

We provide a result of the natural density of such fields in the family of all real quadratic fields ordered by discriminant.

Theorem 6.15
.

When ordered by discriminant, at least 7.4% and at most 33.4% of real quadratic fields
K
have a fundamental unit
\varepsilon_{K}
of odd trace (or equivalently, have a totally positive fundamental unit of odd trace, or have any unit of odd trace). Specifically, as
X\to\infty
, there is an asymptotic inequality

(6.46)

\frac{2}{27}+o(1)\leq\frac{\#\{K:[K:\mathbb{Q}]=2,0<\Delta_{K}<X,2\!\nmid\!\Tr(\varepsilon_{K})\}}{\#\{K:[K:\mathbb{Q}]=2,0<\Delta_{K}<X\}}\leq\frac{1}{3}+O(X^{-1/2}),

where
\Delta_{K}:=\disc K
.

Proof.

See
Appendix

E
.
∎

7.
SIC phenomenology

The purpose of this section is to show how the conjectures and results presented in previous sections explain many of the features of the calculated SIC fiducials that were described in
Section

3.3
.

We begin, in
Section

7.1
, by showing how the action of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
on quadratic forms translates into an action of
\ECS(d)
on the corresponding fiducials.

In
Section

7.2
we discuss the classification of
r
-SIC fiducials. We restrict our attention to what we refer to as
standard fiducials
, by which we mean fiducials corresponding to fiducial data sets
(d,r,Q,G,g)
for which
\Det(G)=\pm 1
. We show that if Conjectures
1.35
,
1.36
, and
1.50
are all true, then one gets the complete set of standard fiducials if one restricts to data sets having some fixed, but arbitrary choice of
G
and
g
. Given two such data sets
(d,r,Q,G,g)
,
(d,r,Q^{\prime},G,g)
we show that the corresponding fiducials are

(1)

in the same
\EC(d)
orbit if
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
are equivalent,

(2)

in the same Galois multiplet if
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
have the same discriminant.

In
Section

7.3
we describe some illustrative examples. We also describe how, on the assumption that Conjectures
1.35
,
1.36
, and
1.50
are all true, the number of
\EC(d)
orbits, and the number of Galois multiplets of standard
1
-SIC fiducials varies with dimension
d
. Finally, we describe how the number of standard
r
-SICs with
r>1
varies with dimension.

In
Section

7.4
we investigate the symmetry group for an
r
-SIC fiducial. We show that if
\Pi_{s}
is the fiducial corresponding to the datum
s=(d,r,Q,G,g)
, then
\mathcal{S}(Q)
, the stabilizer group for
Q
(see
Definition

1.20
), gives rise to a cyclic subgroup of
\mathcal{S}_{\rm{ESL}}(\Pi_{s})
. In every case where it has been checked, one finds in fact that the cyclic group corresponding to
\mathcal{S}(Q)
coincides with
\mathcal{S}_{\rm{ESL}}(\Pi_{s})
. If that is generally true then, given a fiducial data set
s=(d,r,Q,G,g)
,

(1)

we have a criterion for when the symmetry group
\mathcal{S}(\Pi_{s})
has an anti-unitary symmetry;

(2)

we have an expression for the order of
\mathcal{S}(\Pi_{s})
.

In the rank-1 case we can also

(1)

explain why every fiducial has a canonical order 3 symmetry (see
Definition

3.11
),

(2)

explain why one only gets type
z

\EC(d)
orbits when
d\not\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
(see
Definition

3.15
),

(3)

explain why one gets both type
z
and type
a
orbits when
d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
,

(4)

give a criterion for identifying the type
a
orbits when
d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
.

Finally, in
Section

7.5
, we consider the phenomenon of SIC alignment. As discussed in
Section

3.3.4
, it is observed in the empirically calculated solutions
[
8
,
3
]
that, up to a sign, the squares of the normalized overlaps for a
1
-SIC at position
d_{j}
in a dimension tower reappear among the normalized overlaps at position
d_{2j}
. We show that this phenomenon is a consequence of our results. We also show that the phenomenon generalizes to a relation between the normalized overlaps for a
1
-SIC at positions
d_{j}
and
d_{nj}
in a tower, for any positive integer
n
coprime to 3.

7.1.
Transformations of forms and fiducials

Consider the map
s\mapsto\Pi_{s}
, where
s
is a fiducial datum and
\Pi_{s}
is an
r
-SIC fiducial.
We have

(1)

a natural action of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
on the domain of this map, in which
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
takes
s=(d,r,Q,G,g)
to
s_{M}=(d,r,Q_{M},G,g)
,

(2)

a natural action
of
\ECS(d)
on the image of the map, in which
U\in\ECS(d)
takes
\Pi_{s}
to
U\Pi_{s}U^{\dagger}
.

The purpose of this subsection is to show how these two actions are related. Its importance, among other things, is that it leads to the classification of
r
-SICs described in
Section

7.3
.
In particular, it explains the numbers in
Table

2
of
Section

3.3.1
.

Although we do not prove it in this paper,
it appears that live fiducials of the form
\Pi_{s}
, with
s
a fiducial datum, are always strongly centered (see
Definition

3.19
).
It also appears that every
r
-SIC contains at least one strongly centered fiducial.
Conjugating with elements of
\ECS(d)
takes strongly centered fiducials to strongly centered fiducials.
To obtain the full set of
r
-SICs we then conjugate the strongly centered fiducials with elements of
\WH(d)
.

We will now state the main results of this subsection.

It is an immediate consequence of the definition that if
(d,r,Q)
is an admissible tuple, then
(d,r,Q_{M})
is another admissible tuple, for all
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Our first result says that the corresponding fields are the same, and that a similar statement holds for a fiducial datum.

Theorem 7.1
.

Assume
Conjecture

1.35
. Let
(d,r,Q,G,g)
be a fiducial datum and
M
any element of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.
Define
t=(d,r,Q)
,
t_{M}=(d,r,Q_{M})
. Then

(1)

E_{t_{M}}=E_{t}

(2)

(d,r,Q_{M},G,g)
is another fiducial datum.

Theorem

7.1
motivates the following definition.

Definition 7.2

(
M
-transformed tuple and fiducial datum, equivalent tuples and data sets)
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)
be an admissible tuple,
s=(d,r,Q,G,g)
a fiducial datum, and
M
an element of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. We define
t_{M}
to be the
M
-transformed admissible tuple

(d,r,Q_{M})
, and we define
s_{M}
to be the
M
-transformed fiducial datum

(d,r,Q_{M},G,g)
.

We say that two admissible tuples
t=(d,r,Q)
,
t^{\prime}=(d,r,Q^{\prime})
are
equivalent
, and write
t\sim t^{\prime}
, if and only if the forms
Q
,
Q^{\prime}
are equivalent.

Similarly, we say two fiducial datums
s=(d,r,Q,G,g)
,
s^{\prime}=(d,r,Q^{\prime},G,g)
are equivalent, and write
s\sim s^{\prime}
, if and only if the forms
Q
,
Q^{\prime}
are equivalent.

The following homomorphism describes the relation between transformations of
s
, and transformations of
\Pi_{s}
which is the focus of this subsection:

Definition 7.3
.

Let
s=(d,r,Q,G,g)
a fiducial datum. We define
\pi_{s}
to be the map of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
to
\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
defined by

(7.1)

\displaystyle\pi_{s}\colon M\mapsto\sgn\!\left(j_{M^{-1}}(\rho_{t})\right)H^{\vphantom{-1}}_{g}G^{-1}[M]_{\bar{d}}GH_{g}^{-1}

where
t
is the admissible tuple
(d,r,Q)
and
[M]_{\bar{d}}
is the image of
M
under the canonical homomorphism of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
to
\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
.

Remark
.

The factor of
\sgn\!\left(j_{M^{-1}}(\rho_{t})\right)
means
\pi_{s}
is not a homomorphism. It is, however, “almost” a homomorphism, in the sense that
\pi_{s}(M_{1}M_{2})=\pm\pi_{s}(M_{1})\pi_{s}(M_{2})
for all
M_{1},M_{2}\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

We need the following fact.

Theorem 7.4
.

The map
\pi_{s}
is surjective for every fiducial datum
s
.

Proof.

We defer the proof to page
7.1
, at the end of this subsection.
∎

We are now able to state the central result of this subsection:

Theorem 7.5
.

Let
s=(d,r,Q,G,g)
be a fiducial datum,
and let
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
be arbitrary. Then

(7.2)

\displaystyle\Pi^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{s_{M}}

\displaystyle=U^{\dagger}_{F}\Pi^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{s}U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{F},

where
F=\pi_{s}(M)
.

Remark
.

The fact that
\pi_{s}
is surjective means that the converse is also true: Given arbitrary
F\in\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
, there exists
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
for which (
7.2
) holds.

Note, however, that this correspondence between
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-transformations of
s
and (anti-)symplectic transformations of
\Pi_{s}
cannot be a function in either direction.
Indeed,
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
is an infinite group, whereas
\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
is finite. So there must be infinitely many matrices
M
corresponding to a given matrix
F
. Conversely, the existence of the symmetries described in
Section

3.3.2
and
Section

7.4
below means that there is more than one matrix
F
corresponding to a given matrix
M
. This incidentally means that infinitely many different sets of fiducial data
s
must give rise to the same fiducial
\Pi_{s}
.

Proof.

We defer the proof to page
7.1
, at the end of this subsection.
∎

Before proving Theorems

7.4
, and
7.5
we need to establish some preliminary results.

Theorem 7.6
.

Let
t
be an admissible tuple and
M
an element of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Then

(7.3)

\displaystyle L_{t_{M}}

\displaystyle=M^{-1}L_{t}M,

(7.4)

\displaystyle L_{+,t_{M}}

\displaystyle=M^{-1}L_{+,t}M,

(7.5)

\displaystyle L_{\rm{z},t_{M}}

\displaystyle=M^{-1}L_{\rm{z},t}M,

(7.6)

\displaystyle A_{t_{M}}

\displaystyle=M^{-1}A_{t}M,

and

(7.7)

\displaystyle\rho_{t_{M}}

\displaystyle=M^{-1}\rho_{t}

(see Definitions
1.28
,
1.32
for definitions of
L_{t},L_{+,t},L_{\rm{z},t},A_{t},\rho_{t}
).

Proof.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
. It follows from
Theorem

4.50
that

(7.8)

\displaystyle L_{+,t}

\displaystyle=\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)})=\left(\frac{d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-1}{2}\right)I+\frac{f_{j_{\rm{min}}}(f)}{f}SQ

and

(7.9)

\displaystyle L_{+,t_{M}}

\displaystyle=\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)})=\left(\frac{d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-1}{2}\right)I+\frac{f_{j_{\rm{min}}}(f)}{f}SQ_{M}.

It follows from (
1.31
) and
Lemma

4.36
that

(7.10)

\displaystyle SQ_{M}

\displaystyle=\Det(M)SM^{\rm{T}}QM=M^{-1}SQM,

implying

(7.11)

\displaystyle L_{+,t_{M}}

\displaystyle=M^{-1}L_{+,t}M

Equation

7.5
and (
7.6
) follow from this and the fact that

(7.12)

\displaystyle L_{\rm{z},t}

\displaystyle=L_{+,t}^{n},

\displaystyle L_{\rm{z},t_{M}}

\displaystyle=L_{+,t_{M}}^{n},

(7.13)

\displaystyle A_{t}

\displaystyle=L_{+,t}^{n(2m+1)},

\displaystyle A_{t_{M}}

\displaystyle=L_{+,t_{M}}^{n(2m+1)},

where
n=j/j_{\rm{min}}(f)
.
If
\varphi_{f}=\varepsilon_{f}
then
L_{t}=L_{+,t}
,
L_{t_{M}}=L_{+,t_{M}}
, from which (
7.3
) follows.
Otherwise it follows from Theorems
4.6
and
4.15
that
j_{\rm{min}}(f)
is odd,
d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-3
is a perfect square,
f_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}
is divisible by
f\sqrt{d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-3}
, and

(7.14)

\displaystyle L_{t}

\displaystyle=\chi_{Q}(\varphi^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)})=\frac{\sqrt{d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-3}}{2}I+\frac{f_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}}{f\sqrt{d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-3}}SQ,

(7.15)

\displaystyle L_{t_{M}}

\displaystyle=\chi_{Q}(\varphi^{j_{\rm{min}}(f)})=\frac{\sqrt{d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-3}}{2}I+\frac{f_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}}{f\sqrt{d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-3}}SQ_{M}.

Equation

7.3
is then a consequence of this together with (
7.10
).

Finally, it follows from
Definition

1.32
and
Lemma

4.48
that

(7.16)

\displaystyle\rho_{t_{M}}

\displaystyle=\rho_{Q_{M},+}=M^{-1}\rho_{Q,+}=M^{-1}\rho_{t}.

∎

Theorem 7.7
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)
be an admissible tuple, and let
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Then

(7.17)

\displaystyle\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!B_{t_{M}}}\!\left(\rho_{t_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\shin^{d^{-1}lM\mathbf{p}}_{\!B_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)\qquad&\Det M=1,\\
\left(\shin^{d^{-1}lM\mathbf{p}}_{\!B_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)\right)^{*}\qquad&\Det M=-1,\end{cases}

where either
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus d\mathbb{Z}^{2}
or
\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{0}
, where either
B_{t}=A_{t}
,
B_{t_{M}}=A_{t_{M}}
or
B_{t}=A^{-1}_{t}
,
B_{t_{M}}=A^{-1}_{t_{M}}
, and where
l=\sgn\left(j_{M^{-1}}(\rho_{t})\right)
.

Proof.

Proved in
[
72
, Thm. 4.37]
.

∎

Theorem

7.8
tells us how the candidate normalized ghost overlaps transform under the action of an element of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
:

Theorem 7.8
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
be an admissible tuple, and let
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Then

(7.18)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{M})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\nu}_{lM\mathbf{p}}(t)

for all
\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, where
l=\sgn\left(j_{M^{-1}}(\rho_{t})\right)

Proof.

Let
f
be the conductor of
Q
. Then
f
is also the conductor of
Q_{M}
. It follows from
Definition

1.30
and
Proposition

5.1
that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{M})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{p})}e^{-\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(A_{t_{M}})}\xi_{d}^{-\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q_{M}(\mathbf{p})}

(7.19)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{p})}e^{-\frac{\pi i}{12}(\Det M)\Psi(A_{t})}\xi_{d}^{-\frac{f_{jm}}{f}(\Det M)Q(M\mathbf{p})}

Let
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Then

\displaystyle s_{d}(lM\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt=d+(1+d)(1+l(\alpha p_{1}+\beta p_{2}))(1+l(\gamma p_{1}+\delta p_{2}))

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt\equiv d+(1+d)(1+(\alpha+\gamma+\alpha\gamma)p_{1}+p_{1}p_{2}+(\beta+\delta+\beta\delta)p_{2})

\displaystyle\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)

(7.20)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt\equiv d+(1+d)\left(((1+p_{1})(1+p_{2})+(1+\alpha)(1+\gamma)p_{1}+(1+\beta)(1+\delta)p_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)

The fact that
\alpha
is coprime to
\gamma
and
\beta
is coprime to
\delta
means
(1+\alpha)(1+\gamma)
and
(1+\beta)(1+\delta)
are both even. So

(7.21)

\displaystyle s_{d}(lM\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\equiv s_{d}(\mathbf{p})\,\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right).

Also

(7.22)

\displaystyle Q(lM\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=Q(M\mathbf{p}).

Hence

(7.23)

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{M})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\phi_{lM\mathbf{p}}(t)\qquad&\Det M=+1,\\
\left(\phi_{lM\mathbf{p}}(t)\right)^{*}&\Det M=-1.\end{cases}

It follows from
Theorem

7.7
that

(7.24)

\displaystyle\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t_{M}}}\!\left(\rho_{t_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\shin^{d^{-1}lM\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)\qquad&\Det M=+1,\\
\left(\shin^{d^{-1}lM\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)\right)^{*}\qquad&\Det M=-1.\end{cases}

Combining these results gives

(7.25)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{M})

\displaystyle=\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{M})\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t_{M}}}\!\left(\rho_{t_{M}}\right)=\begin{cases}\tilde{\nu}_{lM\mathbf{p}}(t)\qquad&\Det M=+1,\\
\left(\tilde{\nu}_{lM\mathbf{p}}(t)\right)^{*}\qquad&\Det M=-1.\end{cases}

Taking account of the fact that
\tilde{\nu}_{lM\mathbf{p}}(t)
is real we conclude

(7.26)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{M})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\nu}_{lM\mathbf{p}}(t)

irrespective of the sign of
\Det M
.
∎

We are now able to prove the first of our main results:

Proof of
Theorem

7.1
.

It follows from
Theorem

7.8
that the elements of the sets
\{\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\colon 0\leq p_{1},p_{2}<d,\,\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}\}
and
\{\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{M})\colon 0\leq p_{1},p_{2}<d,\,\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}\}
are equal up to a sign, implying
E_{t}=E_{t_{M}}
.

Turning to the second statement, by assumption

(7.27)

\displaystyle\Det(G)r(2\lambda+d_{j}-1+d)

\displaystyle\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\bar{d}\right)

for some for some
\lambda\in\mathcal{Z}_{t}
. To show
\lambda\in\mathcal{Z}_{t_{M}}
, let
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be arbitrary, and let
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{p}}
be any complete set of coset representatives for
\mathbb{Z}^{2}/d\mathbb{Z}^{2}
containing
\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{p}
. Suppose, first of all, that
\det M=+1
. Then it follows from
Theorem

7.6
and
Theorem

7.7
that

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{p}}}\omega_{d}^{r\langle\mathbf{p},(\lambda I+L_{\rm{z},t_{M}})\mathbf{q}\rangle}\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{q}}_{\!A^{\vphantom{-1}}_{t_{M}}}\!\left(\rho_{t_{M}}\right)\shin^{d^{-1}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})}_{\!A^{-1}_{t_{M}}}\!\left(\rho_{t_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{p}}}\omega_{d}^{r\langle\mathbf{p},M^{-1}(\lambda I+L_{\rm{z},t})M\mathbf{q}\rangle}\shin^{d^{-1}lM\mathbf{q}}_{\!A^{\vphantom{-1}}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)\shin^{d^{-1}lM(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})}_{\!A^{-1}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{lM\mathbf{p}}}\omega_{d}^{r\langle M\mathbf{p},(\lambda I+L_{\rm{z},t})\mathbf{q}\rangle}\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{q}}_{\!A^{\vphantom{-1}}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)\shin^{d^{-1}(\mathbf{q}-lM\mathbf{p})}_{\!A^{-1}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)

(7.28)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=d^{2}\delta^{(d)}_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}

where
l=\sgn\left(j_{M^{-1}}(\rho_{t})\right)
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{lM\mathbf{p}}=lM\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{p}}
. It follows that
\lambda\in\mathcal{Z}_{t_{M}}
if
\Det M=+1
. The fact that this is also true of
\Det M=-1
is proved similarly. It follows from the first statement that
\hat{E}_{t_{M}}=\hat{E}_{t}
. So
(d,r,Q_{M},G,g)
is a fiducial datum.
∎

We next prove the following analogue of
Theorem

7.5
, applying to ghost fiducials.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
s=(t,G,g)
be a fiducial datum containing the admissible tuple
t=(d,r,Q)
, and let
M
be any element of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Let

(7.29)

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=\sgn(j_{M^{-1}}(\rho_{t})G^{-1}[M]_{\bar{d}}G

where
[M]_{\bar{d}}
is the image of of
M
under the canonical homomorphism of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
into
\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
. Then
F\in\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
and

(7.30)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{s_{M}}

\displaystyle=U^{\dagger}_{F}\tilde{\Pi}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{s}U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{F}.

Proof.

The fact that
F\in\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
is immediate. It follows from
Definition

1.43
,
Corollary

4.21
and
Theorem

7.8
that

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s_{M}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{G\mathbf{p}}(t_{M})D_{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{\ell MG\mathbf{p}}(t)D_{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)D_{\ell G^{-1}[M]_{\bar{d}}^{-1}\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)U^{\dagger}_{F}D^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{F}

(7.31)

\displaystyle=U^{\dagger}_{F}\tilde{\Pi}_{s}U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{F},

where
\ell=\sgn(j_{M^{-1}}(\rho_{t})
.
∎

Before applying this result to the proof of the main theorem of this subsection, we need to establish two technical results.

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
M
be any element of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
x
any irrational element of
\mathbb{R}
and
d
any dimension greater than 1. Then there exists a matrix
M^{\prime}\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that

(1)

M^{\prime}\equiv M\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\bar{d}\right)
,

(2)

j_{M}(x)j_{M^{\prime}}(x)<0
.

Proof.

The fact that
x\notin\mathbb{Q}
means
j_{M}(x)\neq 0
. Define

(7.32)

\displaystyle F_{\pm 1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-1-\bar{d}&\pm\bar{d}\\
\mp\bar{d}&-1+\bar{d}\end{pmatrix}\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

We have

(7.33)

\displaystyle j_{F_{+}}(-Lx)+j_{F_{-}}(-Lx)

\displaystyle=2(\bar{d}-1)>0.

Consequently we can choose
\theta=\pm 1
such that
j_{F_{\theta}}(-Lx)
is positive. Define
M^{\prime}=-F_{\theta}L
. Then
M^{\prime}\equiv M\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\bar{d}\right)
. Moreover, it follows from
Lemma

2.16
that

(7.34)

\displaystyle j_{M^{\prime}}(x)

\displaystyle=j_{F_{\theta}}(-Lx)j_{-L}(x)=-j_{F_{\theta}}(-Lx)j_{L}(x),

implying that

\sgn(j_{M^{\prime}}(x))=-\sgn(j_{M}(x)).

∎

We next use this result to show that the map
\pi_{s}
is surjective:

Proof of
Theorem

7.4
.

Let
F\in\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}/\mathbb{Z})
be arbitrary. The fact that the canonical homomorphism
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\to\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
is surjective (see, e.g.,
[
31
, Exer. 1.2.2]
for the surjectivity of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\to\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
, from which this follows easily by consideration of the image of
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
) means that there exists
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that

(7.35)

\displaystyle[M]_{\bar{d}}

\displaystyle=GH^{-1}_{g}FH^{\vphantom{-1}}_{g}G^{-1}

In view of
Lemma

7.10
we may assume, without loss of generality, that
j_{M^{-1}}(\rho_{t})
is positive. We then have
\pi_{s}(M)=F
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the main result of this subsection.

Proof of
Theorem

7.5
.

Let
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. It follows from
Lemma

7.9
that

(7.36)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s_{M}}

\displaystyle=U^{\dagger}_{\tilde{F}}\tilde{\Pi}_{s}U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{\tilde{F}}

where

(7.37)

\displaystyle\tilde{F}

\displaystyle=\sgn\left(j_{M^{-1}}(\rho_{t})\right)G^{-1}[M]_{\bar{d}}G.

Applying
g
to both sides and using Definitions
1.43
,
3.6
and
Theorem

3.7
we find

(7.38)

\displaystyle\Pi_{s_{M}}

\displaystyle=U^{\dagger}_{F}\Pi^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{s}U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{F}

where

(7.39)

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=H^{\vphantom{-1}}_{g}\tilde{F}H^{-1}_{g}=\pi_{s}(M),

completing the proof.
∎

7.2.
Classification

In this subsection we show that, subject to certain assumptions,
1
-SICs can be classified in terms of equivalence classes of quadratic forms. We will need the following definition.

Definition 7.11

(equivalence classes of admissible tuples)
.

For each admissible tuple
t=(d,r,Q)
define

(1)

[t]=[d,r,Q]
to be set of all admissible tuples
(d,r,Q^{\prime})
, where
Q^{\prime}
is equivalent to
Q
,

(2)

[\![t]\!]=[\![d,r,Q]\!]
to be the set of all tuples
(d,r,Q^{\prime})
, where
Q^{\prime}
has the same conductor as
Q
.

We will sometimes write
[\![d,r,f]\!]
in place of
[\![d,r,Q]\!]
, where
f
is the conductor of
Q
.

We will show that in the rank 1 case, subject to Conjectures
1.35
,
1.50
and
2.9
, and the three assumptions listed below, that the equivalence classes
[t]
are in bijective correspondence with the set of all
\EC(d)
orbits of
1
-SICs, and that the equivalence classes
[\![t]\!]
are in bijective correspondence with the set of all Galois multiplets of
1
-SICs. In
Appendix

F
we illustrate this statement by listing the classes
[t]
,
[\![t]\!]
along with salient details for the corresponding
1
-SICs for dimensions
4
–
100
.

Assumption 1
.

Let
\Pi
,
\Pi^{\prime}
be any pair of
1
-SIC fiducials in dimension
d
. Then

(7.40)

\displaystyle\Tr(\Pi D_{\mathbf{p}})

\displaystyle=\pm\Tr(\Pi^{\prime}D_{\mathbf{p}})\qquad\forall\mathbf{p}

if and only if
\Pi=\Pi^{\prime}
.

Assumption 2
.

Let
s=(d,1,Q,G,g)
,
s^{\prime}=(d,1,Q^{\prime},G^{\prime},g^{\prime})
be admissible data such that
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
have different discriminants.
Then
\Pi_{s}
and
\Pi_{s^{\prime}}
are
\EC(d)
-inequivalent.

Assumption 3
.

In the rank 1 case, the only shifts are
0
and
1
.

These statements have a different status from the ones we label “conjectures” in that they are only needed for the classification problem. Moreover, even if one or more of them were to fail, it would not necessarily mean that
1
-SICs could not be classified using quadratic forms, only that the classification would be more complicated.

Assumption
2
is worth singling out for special mention, in that it is shown in
[
74
, Thm. 8.2]
that equivalence classes
[\![d,1,f]\!]
,
[\![d,1,f^{\prime}]\!]
can give rise to the same field, even though
f\neq f^{\prime}
. This happens, for instance, with the pairs
[\![47,1,1]\!]
,
[\![47,1,2]\!]
;
[\![67,1,1]\!]
,
[\![67,1,2]\!]
; and
[\![83,1,1]\!]
,
[\![83,1,2]\!]
. Although there is no known instance, it is natural to wonder if there are cases where the corresponding SICs are
\EC(d)
-equivalent.

We confine our analysis to the rank 1 case due to uncertainties concerning the set of shifts when
r>1
, as discussed in
Section

5.5
. In the following it will accordingly be assumed, without comment, that
r=1
. In accordance with assumption 3 it will also be assumed without comment that
\det G=\pm 1
for every twist
G
.

It is immediate that
\Pi_{s}
,
\Pi_{s^{\prime}}
cannot be be equal unless the dimensions are the same. So the classification problem reduces to the question of what can be said of the
1
-SICs corresponding to two sets of fiducial data
s=(d,1,Q,G,g)
,
s^{\prime}=(d,1,Q^{\prime},G^{\prime},g^{\prime})
when
(Q,G,g)
and
(Q^{\prime},G^{\prime},g^{\prime})
are not assumed to be the same. We begin with the following result.

Theorem 7.12
.

Let
s=(d,1,Q,G,g)
,
s^{\prime}=(d,1,Q^{\prime},G^{\prime},g)
be a pair of fiducial datums for which
Q^{\prime}\sim Q
. If Assumption 3 is true there exists
F\in\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
such that

(7.41)

\displaystyle\Pi_{s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{F}\Pi_{s}U^{\dagger}_{F}

Proof.

It follows from
Lemma

7.9
that there exists
F^{\prime}\in\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
such that

(7.42)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{F^{\prime}}\tilde{\Pi}_{s}U^{\dagger}_{F^{\prime}}

where
s^{\prime\prime}=(d,1,Q^{\prime},G,g)
. Let
F^{\prime\prime}
be the image of
G^{-1}G^{\prime}
under the canonical homomorphism
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\to\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
. Writing
t^{\prime}=(d,1,Q^{\prime})
and referring to
Definition

1.43
we see that

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}I+\frac{1}{d\sqrt{d+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime})D_{G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}I+\frac{1}{d\sqrt{d+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime})U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{F^{\prime\prime}}D^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{G^{\prime-1}\mathbf{p}}U^{\dagger}_{F^{\prime\prime}}

(7.43)

\displaystyle=U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{F^{\prime\prime}}\tilde{\Pi}_{s^{\prime}}U^{\dagger}_{F^{\prime\prime}}.

Hence

(7.44)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=U^{\dagger}_{F^{\prime\prime}}U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{F^{\prime}}\tilde{\Pi}_{s}U^{\dagger}_{F^{\prime}}U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{F^{\prime\prime}}.

Applying
g
to both sides we find, in view of Definitions
1.43
,
3.6
and Theorem
3.7
,

(7.45)

\displaystyle\Pi_{s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{F}\Pi_{s}U^{\dagger}_{F}

with
F=H_{g}F^{{}^{\prime\prime}-1}F^{\prime}H^{-1}_{g}
.
∎

We also need to consider the effect of varying the Galois conjugation
g
. Let
t=(d,r,Q)
be an admissible tuple, let
f
be the conductor of
Q
, and let
E_{t}
be the field associated to
t
(as specified in
Definition

1.40
). Then it is easily seen that for every fiducial datum
s=(t,G,g)
extending
t
the matrix elements of
\tilde{\Pi}_{s}
,
\Pi_{s}
are in
E_{t}
.
Also, if
Conjecture

1.50
is true, then
E_{t}
is the ray class field (in the generalized sense of Kopp and Lagarias
[
73
]
) with datum
(\mathcal{O}_{f},\bar{d}\mathcal{O}_{f},(\infty_{1},\infty_{2}))
. In particular, if
Conjecture

1.50
is true, and if
t^{\prime}=(d,r,Q^{\prime})
is any other element of
[\![t]\!]
, then
E_{t^{\prime}}=E_{t}
. We accordingly define
E_{[\![t]\!]}=E_{t}
.

We also need the following subfield of
E_{t}
:

Definition 7.13

(ring class field, class number)
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
be an admissible tuple, and let
f
be the conductor of
Q
. We define the
ring class field
for
t
, denoted
H_{t}
, to be the ray class field (in the generalized sense of Kopp and Lagarias
[
73
]
) with datum
(\mathcal{O}_{f},\mathcal{O}_{f},\emptyset)
. We define the
class number
for
t
, denoted
h_{t}
, to be the class number of
\mathcal{O}_{f}
(or, equivalently, the degree of the extension
H_{t}/K
).

Since
H_{t}
and
h_{t}
only depend on the equivalence class
[\![t]\!]
, we may define
H_{[\![t]\!]}=H_{t}
,
h_{[\![t]\!]}=h_{t}
.

Remark
.

Note that if
f=1
, so that
\mathcal{O}_{f}
is the maximal order, then
H_{t}
is the Hilbert class field.

Also define a fixed, non-canonical choice of a sign-switching automorphism.

Definition 7.14
.

On the assumption that
Conjecture

1.50
is true, for each equivalence class
[\![d,1,Q]\!]
make a once-and-for-all choice of automorphism
g_{[\![d,1,Q]\!]}\in\Gal(E_{[\![d,1,Q]\!]}/\mathbb{Q})
which does not fix
K
.

We will also need the following result.

Theorem 7.15
.

Assume
Conjecture

1.50
.
Let
s=(t,G,g)
be a fiducial datum containing the admissible tuple
t=(d,1,Q)
, and let
f
be the conductor of
Q
. Let
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{h_{t}}
be a set of representatives of the
h_{t}
distinct equivalence classes of forms having the same discriminant as
Q
. Then for each
g^{\prime}\in\Gal(E_{t}/K)
there exists a form
Q(g^{\prime})
having the same discriminant as
Q
and such that

(7.46)

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(\Pi_{s})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{s(g^{\prime})},

\displaystyle s(g^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(d,1,Q(g^{\prime}),G,g).

Moreover

(1)

For each integer
n=1,\dots,h_{t}
, there exists
g^{\prime}\in\Gal(E_{t}/K)
such that
Q(g^{\prime})\sim Q_{n}
.

(2)

Q(g^{\prime})\sim Q
if and only if
g^{\prime}\in\Gal(E_{t}/H_{t})
.

Proof.

To appear in a subsequent publication
[
15
]
.
∎

We can then prove the following theorem.

Theorem 7.16
.

Assume Conjectures
1.35
,
1.50
and Assumptions 1, 2, 3 are true.
Let
s=(d,1,Q,G,g)
be a fiducial datum. There exists a form
Q^{\prime}
, having the same discriminant as
Q
, such that

(7.47)

\displaystyle\Pi_{s}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{s^{\prime}},

\displaystyle s^{\prime}=(d,1,Q^{\prime},I,g_{[\![d,1,Q]\!]}).

Proof.

Let
s^{\prime\prime}=(d,1,Q,G,g_{[\![d,1,Q]\!]})
.
It follows from
Theorem

7.15
that

(7.48)

\displaystyle\Pi_{s}

\displaystyle=gg^{-1}_{[\![d,1,Q]\!]}\left(\Pi_{s^{\prime\prime}}\right)=\Pi_{s^{\prime\prime\prime}},

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=(d,1,Q^{\prime\prime},G,g_{[\![d,1,Q]\!]})

for some
Q^{\prime\prime}
having the same discriminant as
Q
. It then follows from
Theorem

7.12
that

(7.49)

\displaystyle\Pi_{s^{\prime\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{F}\Pi_{s^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}U^{\dagger}_{F},

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=(d,1,Q^{\prime\prime},I,g_{[\![d,1,Q]\!]})

for some
F\in\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
. In view of Theorems
7.4
and
7.5
this means

(7.50)

\displaystyle\Pi_{s^{\prime\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{s^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}_{M}},

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}_{M}

\displaystyle=(d,1,Q^{\prime\prime}_{M},I,g_{[\![d,1,Q]\!]})

for some
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Setting
Q^{\prime\prime}_{M}=Q^{\prime}
,
s^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}_{M}=s^{\prime}
the claim now follows.
∎

It follows from this result together with
Definition

3.18
and Theorems
7.5
,
7.15
that, if we assume Conjectures
1.35
,
1.50
and
2.9
and Assumptions 1, 2, 3, , and if we confine ourselves to
1
-SICs of the kind specified by
Definition

1.43
, then there are bijective maps associating

(1)

to each equivalence class
[t]
, a corresponding
\EC(d)
orbit of
1
-SICs,

(2)

to each equivalence class
[\![t]\!]
, a corresponding Galois multiplet of
1
-SICs.

The procedure for finding all the
1
-SICs corresponding to a given admissible
(d,1)\sim(K,j,m)
is then as follows:

Step 1

Find the set of divisors of
f_{j}
. Each such divisor corresponds to a distinct Galois multiplet of the specified rank and dimension.

Step 2

For each
f\!\mid\!f_{j}
calculate the corresponding class number
h_{[\![d,1,f]\!]}
(see Definitions
7.11
,
7.13
).
This is the number of distinct
\EC(d)
orbits in the multiplet
[\![d,1,f]\!]
.

Step 3

Find
h_{[\![d,1,f]\!]}
inequivalent quadratic forms
Q_{j}
having discriminant
f^{2}\Delta_{0}
, where
\Delta_{0}
is the discriminant of
K
. The
1
-SICs corresponding to the admissible tuples
t_{j}=(d,1,Q_{j})
give us a full set of representatives for the distinct
\EC(d)
orbits in
[\![d,1,f]\!]
.

Step 4

Conjugate the fiducials corresponding to the tuples
t_{1},\dots,t_{h_{[\![d,1,f]\!]}}
with the elements of
\EC(d)
to obtain the full set of
1
-SICs in the Galois multiplet
[\![d,1,f]\!]
.

Notice that equivalence of forms is defined relative to the group
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
rather than
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. So it is the wide class number that is relevant here.

This construction is illustrated in the data tables in
Appendix

F
, which gives a complete listing of
1
-SICs and corresponding equivalence classes
[t]
,
[\![t]\!]
for
d\leq 100
.

7.3.
Illustrative examples

In this subsection we illustrate the discussion in
Section

7.2
with some examples, on the assumption that Conjectures
1.35
,
1.36
, and
1.50
and Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
are all true.

Consider the dimension grid corresponding to the field
K=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5})
, illustrated in (
Example
). Consider first the admissible tuple
(4,1)\sim(K,1,1)
. We have
f_{1}=1
, so there is only one Galois multiplet. The class number is 1, so the multiplet consists of a single
\EC(4)
orbit. A choice of form which is calculationally optimal is
Q=\langle 1,-3,1\rangle
. Comparing with the tables in
[
92
,
91
]
, we see that this agrees with what was previously found by a brute force computational approach, and that Scott–Grassl orbit
4a
is, in our notation, the orbit
[4,1,\langle 1,-3,1\rangle]
.

Moving up the left-hand column of the grid, we come to the admissible tuple
(8,1)\sim(K,2,1)
. We have
f_{2}=3
, so there are two Galois multiplets corresponding to the choices
f=1,3
. The class numbers are both 1, so each multiplet consists of a single
\EC(8)
orbit. Calculationally optimal choices of
Q
are
\langle 1,-3,1\rangle
,
\langle 1,-7,1\rangle
respectively. Again, this is consistent with what was previously found. Comparing with the tables in
[
92
,
91
]
, one finds that the Scott–Grassl orbit
8a
is
[8,3,\langle 1,-7,1\rangle]
in our notation, and orbit
8b
is
[8,1,\langle 1,-3,1\rangle]
. Finally, the fact that
1\!\mid\!3
implies
E_{[\![8,1,1]\!]}\subseteq E_{[\![8,1,3]\!]}
, while the fact that
3\!\nmid\!2\times 1
and
\Delta_{0}\not\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right)
means that
E_{[\![8,1,3]\!]}\nsubseteq E_{[\![8,1,1]\!]}
. So
E_{[\![8,1,1]\!]}
is a proper subfield of
E_{[\![8,1,3]\!]}

Since they only depend on the divisors of
f_{j}
and the class numbers
h_{K,f}
, and since these quantities are constant along the rows of the dimension grid, it follows that the number of Galois multiplets, and the number of
\EC(d)
orbits within each multiplet, are constant along each row. Thus, moving along the bottom row one finds that each of the sequence of admissible pairs
(4,1),(11,3),(29,8),\dots
gives rise to one Galois multiplet, comprising one
\EC(d)
orbit. Similarly, moving along the next-to-bottom row one finds that each of the sequence of admissble pairs
(8,1),(55,7),(377,48),\dots
gives rise to two Galois multiplets, each comprising one
\EC(d)
orbit.

In
Appendix

F
we give,
on the assumption that Conjectures
1.35
,
1.36
, and
1.50

and Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
are all true,
a complete listing of Galois multiplets and
\EC(d)
orbits of
1
-SICs for
d\leq 100
. Using this table one can also find the number of Galois multiplets and
\EC(d)
orbits for any admissible tuple
(d,r)\sim(K,j,m)
such that
d_{j}\leq 100
.

In this way, under our conjectures and assumptions, one can quickly compute the number of multiplets and orbits for much larger dimensions.
For instance when
d=10^{6}
one finds that there is a single Galois multiplet with class number
14\,800
. By contrast, when

one finds that there are
40
Galois multiplets with conductors and class numbers as in

r

. A pair
(d,r)

and

(7.51)

\displaystyle nr(d-r)

1.21

) exist for every

. This is far from being the case when

.

1\,153

d

(d,r)

. Moreover, in almost of all of these cases there is only one pair with

7.4.
Symmetries

The purpose of this subsection is to prove some of the empirical observations in
Section

3.3.2
, regarding the symmetry group of a
1
-SIC, and to generalize them to an arbitrary
r
-SIC. We begin with a summary of our main results.

Definition 7.17

(
R_{s}
,
R_{+,s}
,
R_{\rm{z},s}
)
.

Let
s=(t,G,g)
be a fiducial datum containing the admissible tuple
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
. We define

(7.52)

\displaystyle R_{s}

\displaystyle=\pi_{s}(L_{t}),

(7.53)

\displaystyle R_{+,s}

\displaystyle=\pi_{s}(L_{+,t}),

(7.54)

\displaystyle R_{\rm{z},s}

\displaystyle=\pi_{s}(L_{\rm{z},t})

(see Definitions
1.28
,
7.3

for definitions of
L_{t}
,
L_{+,t}
,
L_{\rm{z},t}
,
\pi_{s}
).

Definition 7.18

(unitary/anti-unitary type)
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
be an admissible tuple. Let
f
the conductor of
Q
. We say the tuple
t
is of
anti-unitary type
if all three of the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

d_{1}-3
is a perfect square,

(2)

j_{\rm{min}}(f)
is odd,

(3)

f\sqrt{d_{j_{\rm{min}}}(f)-3}
divides
f_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}
.

Otherwise, we say it is of
unitary type
.

Remark
.

Note that it follows from
Theorem

4.6
that if
d_{1}-3
is a perfect square and
j_{\rm{min}}(f)
is odd then
d_{j_{\rm{min}}}(f)-3
is also a perfect square.

Our first result says that
\pi_{s}
maps stabilizers of forms into stabilizers of fiducials:

Theorem 7.19
.

Let
s=(t,G,g)
be a fiducial datum containing the admissible tuple
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
. The restriction of
\pi_{s}
to
\mathcal{S}(Q)
is a homomorphism of
\mathcal{S}(Q)
onto
\langle R_{s}\rangle
. Moreover,
\langle R_{s}\rangle\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\rm{ESL}}(\Pi_{s})
.

Remark
.

See
Definition

3.17
for definition of
\mathcal{S}_{\rm{ESL}}(\Pi_{s})
.
In every case where the groups have been calculated one finds in fact
\langle R_{s}\rangle=\mathcal{S}_{\rm{ESL}}(\Pi_{s})
.

Proof.

See below.
∎

Our second result establishes some basic properties of the matrices
R_{s}
,
R_{\rm{z},s}
.

Theorem 7.20
.

Let
s=(t,G,g)
be a fiducial datum containing the admissible tuple
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
. Then

(1)

R_{s}
has the properties

Type of
t

(2)

has the properties

2m+1

(see
Definition

4.49
for the level,
n_{t}
).

Remark
.

If it is true that
\langle U_{R_{s}}\!\langle I\rangle\rangle=\mathcal{S}_{\rm{ESL}}(\Pi_{s})
, as the empirical observations suggest, then this result means:

(1)

\mathcal{S}(\Pi_{s})
contains a projective anti-unitary if and only if
t
is of anti-unitary type;

(2)

\mathcal{S}(\Pi_{s})
is cyclic order
n_{t}(2m+1)
if
t
is of unitary type, and cyclic order
2n_{t}(2m+1)
if
t
is of anti-unitary type.

It follows from the above that, if
m=1
, then
U_{R_{\rm{z},s}}\!\langle I\rangle
is a canonical order 3 projective unitary (see
Definition

3.11
).
Our third main result establishes criteria for type-
z
and type-
a
orbits.

Theorem 7.21
.

Let
s=(t,G,g)
be a fiducial datum containing the admissible tuple
t=(d,1,Q)\sim(K,j,1,Q)
.

(1)

if
d\not\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
, then
R_{\rm{z},s}
is conjugate to
F_{\rm{z}}
;

(2)

if
d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
, then there exist both type-
a
and type-
z
orbits. Specifically:

(a)

if
f_{j}/f\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
, then
R_{\rm{z},s}
is conjugate to
F_{\rm{a}}
;

(b)

if
f_{j}/f\not\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
, then
R_{\rm{z},s}
is conjugate to
F_{\rm{z}}
.

Remark
.

This result explains why one gets both type-
z
and type-
a
orbits when
d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
.
If one makes the additional assumption that
\langle R_{s}\rangle=\mathcal{S}_{\rm{OL}}(\Pi)
(as is the case in every instance where the groups have been explicitly calculated), then it also explains why one never finds type-
a^{\prime}
orbits when
d\equiv 6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
.

Proof.

See below.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

7.19
.

Suppose
M_{1}
,
M_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(Q)
.
Then it follows from Lemmas
2.16
and
4.48
that

(7.55)

\displaystyle j_{(M_{1}M_{2})^{-1}}(\rho_{t})

\displaystyle=j_{M^{-1}_{2}}(M^{-1}_{1}\rho_{t})j_{M^{-1}_{1}}(\rho_{t})=j_{M^{-1}_{2}}(\rho_{t})j_{M^{-1}_{1}}(\rho_{t}),

implying

(7.56)

\displaystyle\pi_{s}(M_{1}M_{2})

\displaystyle=\sgn\left(j_{(M_{1}M_{2})^{-1}}(\rho_{t})\right)H^{\vphantom{-1}}_{g}G^{-1}[M_{1}M_{2}]^{\vphantom{-1}}_{\bar{d}}GH^{-1}_{g}=\pi_{s}(M_{1})\pi_{s}(M_{2}).

So the restriction of
\pi_{s}
to
\mathcal{S}(Q)
is a homomorphism.
It follows from
Theorem

4.50
that
\mathcal{S}(Q)=\langle-I,L_{t}\rangle
.
The fact that
j_{-I}(\rho_{t})=-1
implies
\pi_{s}(-I)=I
.
So
\pi_{s}(\mathcal{S}(Q))=\langle R_{s}\rangle
. Finally, it follows from
Theorem

7.5
that

(7.57)

\displaystyle U^{\dagger}_{R_{s}}\Pi^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{s^{\vphantom{-1}}}U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{R_{s}}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{s^{\vphantom{-1}}_{L_{t}}}=\Pi^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{s}

implying
R_{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm{ESL}}(\Pi_{s})
.
∎

Before proving
Theorem

7.20
, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 7.22
.

Let
s=(t,G,g)
be a fiducial datum containing the admissible tuple
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
. Then

(7.58)

\displaystyle R_{s}

\displaystyle=\pi_{s}(L_{t})=\begin{cases}H^{\vphantom{-1}}_{g}G^{-1}[L_{t}]^{\vphantom{-1}}_{\bar{d}}GH^{-1}_{g}\qquad&\text{if $t$ is of unitary type,}\\
-H^{\vphantom{-1}}_{g}G^{-1}[L_{t}]^{\vphantom{-1}}_{\bar{d}}GH^{-1}_{g}\qquad&\text{if $t$ is of anti-unitary type.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
f
be the conductor of
Q
.
It follows from Theorems
4.15
,
4.33
and
4.50
that
\Det(L_{t})=\Nm(\varphi_{f})=-1
if and only if the tuple
t
is of anti-unitary type.
Also, by assumption,
\Tr(L_{t}^{-1})>0

(see
Definition

1.28
). In view of
Lemma

2.18
, this means
\rho_{t}\in\mathcal{D}_{L_{t}^{-1}}
. Referring to
Definition

1.15
, we deduce
that
\sgn(j_{L_{t}^{-1}}(\rho_{t}))=\Det(L_{t}^{-1})
.
Equation

7.58
follows from this and Definitions
7.3
,
7.17
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

7.20
.

We will first prove statement (
1
).
It follows from
Lemma

7.22
that

(7.59)

\displaystyle\Det(R_{s})

\displaystyle=\Det\left([L_{t}]_{\bar{d}}\right)=\begin{cases}1\qquad&\text{if $t$ is of unitary type,}\\
-1\qquad&\text{if $t$ is of anti-unitary type.}\end{cases}

Taking account of
Lemma

4.3
and Theorems
4.6
,
4.33
and
4.50
, it also follows that

\displaystyle\Tr(R_{s})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\Tr\left(L_{t}\right)\qquad&\text{if $t$ is of unitary type,}\\
-\Tr\left(L_{t}\right)\qquad&\text{if $t$ is of anti-unitary type,}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\Tr\left(\varepsilon_{f}\right)\qquad&\text{if $t$ is of unitary type,}\\
-\Tr\left(\varphi_{f}\right)\qquad&\text{if $t$ is of anti-unitary type,}\end{cases}

(7.60)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-1\qquad&\text{if $t$ is of unitary type,}\\
-\sqrt{d_{j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-3}\qquad&\text{if $t$ is of anti-unitary type.}\end{cases}

It remains to calculate the orders of
R_{s}
,
U_{R_{s}}\!\langle I\rangle
. We consider the four cases separately.

Case 1:
t
is of unitary type and
d
is odd.
It follows from
Lemma

7.22
and
Theorem

4.50
that

\displaystyle R_{s}^{\ell}

\displaystyle=I

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle L_{t}^{\ell}\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle L_{t}^{\ell}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(Q)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle L_{t}^{\ell}\in\langle A_{t}\rangle

(7.61)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\ell n_{t}(2m+1)\mid\ell.

So
R_{s}
is order
n_{t}(2m+1)
. In view of
Theorem

3.5
, this is also the order of
U_{R_{s}}\!\langle I\rangle
.

Case 2:
t
is of unitary type and
d
is even.
Let
\ell
be the order of
R_{s}
.
It follows from (
7.58
) and
Theorem

4.50
that

\displaystyle R_{s}^{\ell}

\displaystyle=I

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle L_{t}^{\ell}\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\bar{d}\right)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle L_{t}^{\ell}\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)

(7.62)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle L_{t}^{\ell}\in\langle A_{t}\rangle

implying
L_{t}^{\ell}
is a power of
A_{t}
. Since
A_{t}\equiv(d+1)I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,\bar{d}\right)
, we must in fact have

L_{t}^{\ell}=A_{t}^{2}=L_{t}^{2n_{t}(2m+1)}
. So
\ell=2n_{t}(2m+1)
. Since
L_{t}^{n_{t}(2m+1)}=(d+1)I
, it follows from
Theorem

3.5
that the order of
U_{R_{s}}\!\langle I\rangle
is
n_{t}(2m+1)
.

Case 3:
t
is of anti-unitary type and
d
is odd.
As in Case 1, we have
R_{s}^{\ell}=I\iff L_{t}^{\ell}\in\langle A_{t}\rangle
. The fact that
t
is of anti-unitary type means
\varepsilon=\varphi^{2}
. In view of
Theorem

4.50
, this means
A_{t}=L_{t}^{2n_{t}(2m+1)}
. So
R_{s}
is order
2n_{t}(2m+1)
. In view of
Theorem

3.5
, this is also the order of
U_{R_{s}}\!\langle I\rangle
.

Case 4:
t
is of anti-unitary type and
d
is even.
Let
\ell
be the order of
R_{s}
. As in Case 2, we must have
L_{t}^{\ell}=A_{t}^{2}
. As in Case 3, we have
A_{t}=L_{t}^{2n_{t}(2m+1)}
. So
L_{s}
is order
4n_{t}(2m+1)
. Since
L_{t}^{n_{t}(2m+1)}=(d+1)I
, it follows from
Theorem

3.5
that the order of
U_{R_{s}}\!\langle I\rangle
is
2n_{t}(2m+1)
.

We will now prove statement (
2
). It follows from
Lemma

7.22
and
Theorem

4.50
that

\displaystyle R_{\rm{z},s}

\displaystyle=\pi_{s}(L_{\rm{z},t})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\pi_{s}(L_{t}^{n_{t}})\qquad&\text{$t$ is of unitary type,}\\
\pi_{s}(L_{t}^{2n_{t}})\qquad&\text{$t$ is of anti-unitary type,}\end{cases}

(7.63)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}H^{\vphantom{-1}}_{g}G^{-1}[L^{n_{t}}_{t}]^{\vphantom{-1}}_{\bar{d}}GH^{-1}_{g}\qquad&\text{$t$ is of unitary type,}\\
H^{\vphantom{-1}}_{g}G^{-1}[L^{2n_{t}}_{t}]^{\vphantom{-1}}_{\bar{d}}GH^{-1}_{g}\qquad&\text{$t$ is of anti-unitary type.}\end{cases}

It follows that

(7.64)

\displaystyle R_{\rm{z},s}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}R_{s}^{n_{t}}\qquad&\text{$t$ is of unitary type,}\\
R_{s}^{2n_{t}}\qquad&\text{$t$ is of anti-unitary type,}\end{cases}

and that
\Det(R_{s})=1
irrespective of whether
t
is of unitary or anti-unitary type. It also follows that

\displaystyle\Tr(R_{s})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\Tr(L^{n_{t}}_{t})\qquad&\text{$t$ is of unitary type,}\\
\Tr(L^{2n_{t}}_{t})\qquad&\text{$t$ is of anti-unitary type,}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\Tr(\varepsilon_{f}^{n_{t}})

\displaystyle=\Tr(\varepsilon^{j})

(7.65)

\displaystyle=d_{j}-1.

Finally,
Equation

7.64
in conjunction with statement
1
implies, firstly, that the order of
R_{\rm{z},s}
is
2m+1
if
d
is odd and
2(2m+1)
if
d
is even; and, secondly, that the order of
U_{R_{\rm{z},s}}
is
2m+1
irrespective of the values of
d
,
t
.
∎

Before proving
Theorem

7.21
, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 7.23
.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
be an admissible tuple. Then

(7.66)

\displaystyle\gcd(f_{j},d_{j})

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}1\qquad&d_{j}\not\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right),\\
3\qquad&d_{j}\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right).\end{cases}

Proof.

Suppose
p
is a prime divisor of
\gcd(f_{j},d_{j})
. Then
p
divides both
d_{j}
and
(d_{j}-3)(d_{j}+1)=f_{j}^{2}\Delta_{0}
where
\Delta_{0}
is the discriminant of
K
. Since
d_{j}
is coprime to
d_{j}+1
, it must in fact be the case that
p
divides both
d_{j}
and
d_{j}+3
. It follows that
p=3
and
d_{j}
is a multiple of 3. We have thus shown that
\gcd(f_{j},d_{j})
is a power of 3. In particular, if
d_{j}
is not a multiple of 3 then
f_{j}
is coprime to
d
.

Suppose
d_{j}
is a multiple of 3.
We can write
d_{j}=3t
for some integer
t>1
. Then
f_{j}^{2}\Delta_{0}=3(t-1)(3t+1)
, from which it can be seen that
f_{j}
is a multiple of
3
if and only if
t-1
is a multiple of 3. Equivalently,
f_{j}
is a multiple of
3
if and only if
d_{j}\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
. Combined with the result proved in the last paragraph this means: (a) if
d_{j}\not\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
, then
\gcd(f_{j},d_{j})=1
, and (b) if
d_{j}\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
, then
\gcd(f_{j},d_{j})=3
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

7.21
.

It follows from
Theorem

7.20
that
\Det(R_{\rm{z},s})=1
and
\Tr(R_{\rm{z},s})=d-1
. In view of
Theorem

3.13
, this means that
R_{\rm{z},s}
is conjugate to
F_{z}
if
d\not\equiv 3
or
6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
.

Suppose, on the other hand, that
d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
(respectively,
d\equiv 6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
). Then it follows from Theorems
3.13
and
3.14
that
R_{\rm{z},s}
is conjugate to
F_{a}
(respectively
F^{\prime}_{a}
) if
R_{\rm{z},s}\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
, and to
F_{z}
otherwise. To find the condition for this to be so, observe that Theorems
4.50
,
7.20
, and
Lemma

7.22
imply

\displaystyle R_{\rm{z},s}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}R_{s}^{n_{t}}\qquad&\text{$t$ is of unitary type,}\\
R_{s}^{2n_{t}}&\text{$t$ is of anti-unitary type,}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}H^{\vphantom{-1}}_{g}G^{-1}[L_{t}^{n_{t}}]_{\bar{d}}GH^{-1}_{g}\qquad&\text{$t$ is of unitary type,}\\
H^{\vphantom{-1}}_{g}G^{-1}[L_{t}^{2n_{t}}]_{\bar{d}}GH^{-1}_{g}&\text{$t$ is of anti-unitary type,}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}H^{\vphantom{-1}}_{g}G^{-1}[\chi_{Q}(\varphi_{f}^{n_{t}}]_{\bar{d}}GH^{-1}_{g}\qquad&\text{$t$ is of unitary type,}\\
H^{\vphantom{-1}}_{g}G^{-1}[\chi_{Q}(\varphi_{f}^{2n_{t}})]_{\bar{d}}GH^{-1}_{g}&\text{$t$ is of anti-unitary type,}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=H^{\vphantom{-1}}_{g}G^{-1}[\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon_{f}^{n_{t}}]_{\bar{d}}GH^{-1}_{g}

(7.67)

\displaystyle=H^{\vphantom{-1}}_{g}G^{-1}[\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon^{j}]_{\bar{d}}GH^{-1}_{g}.

Taking account of
Corollary

4.35
and the fact that
\bar{d}
is divisible by
3
, this means

\displaystyle\qquad R_{\rm{z},s}

\displaystyle\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\qquad[\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon^{j})]_{\bar{d}}

\displaystyle\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\qquad\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon^{j})

\displaystyle\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\qquad\varepsilon^{j}-1

\displaystyle\in 3\mathcal{O}_{f}

(7.68)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\qquad\frac{d-3-f_{j}\Delta_{0}}{2}+f_{j}\left(\frac{\Delta_{0}+\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=3\left(p_{1}+p_{2}f\left(\frac{\Delta_{0}+\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}}{2}\right)\right)

for some
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
. The fact that
f_{j}^{2}\Delta_{0}=(d-3)(d+1)
means
f_{j}\Delta_{0}\equiv d-3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,6\right)
. We conclude that
R_{\rm{z},s}\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
if and only if
f_{j}/f\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
. Since
d\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
, it follows from
Lemma

7.23
that
f_{j}/f

is coprime to
3
if
d\equiv 6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
but divisible by
3
if
d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
. So
R_{\rm{z},s}
is necessarily conjugate to
F_{z}
if
d\equiv 6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
. On the other hand, if
d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
, then there are values of
f
such that
f_{j}/f\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
, implying
R_{\rm{z},s}
is conjugate to
F_{a}
, and others such that
f_{j}/f\not\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
, implying
R_{\rm{z},s}
is conjugate to
F_{z}
.
∎

7.5.
Alignment

We now come to the phenomenon of
SIC alignment
[
8
,
3
]
. In the rank 1 case it is empirically observed, in every case examined, that, up to a sign, the squares of the normalized overlaps at position
d_{j}
in a dimension tower reappear among the normalized overlaps at position
d_{2j}
. We will show that this phenomenon is a consequence of our results within our conjectural framework.
Moreover, we will show that it generalizes to a relation between the normalized overlaps at positions
d_{j}
and
d_{nj}
in the tower, for any integer
n
coprime to
3
.

We first state the main result of this subsection.

Theorem 7.24
.

Let
s=(t,G,g)
be a fiducial datum containing the rank 1 admissible tuple
t=(d_{j},1,Q)\sim(K,j,1,Q)
. Let
n
an a positive integer coprime to
3
. Define
t^{\prime}=(d_{nj},1,Q)\sim(K,nj,1,Q)
,
s^{\prime}=(t^{\prime},G,g)
, and
\kappa=d_{nj}/d_{j}
. Then
t^{\prime}
is an admissible tuple and
\kappa
is an integer. If
s^{\prime}
is also a fiducial datum, then

(7.69)

\displaystyle\nu_{\kappa\mathbf{p}}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell(\mathbf{p})}\nu_{\mathbf{p}}(s)^{n}

for all
\mathbf{p}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d_{j}\right)
,
where

(7.70)

\displaystyle\ell(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}n+(1+p_{1})(1+p_{2})\qquad&\text{if $d_{j}$ is even and $n\equiv\pm 2$ or $\pm 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$}\,,\\
n+1\qquad&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

Remark
.

In the rank
1
case the, empirical observations suggest that, if
t
is an admissible tuple, then
(t,G,g)
is a fiducial datum if and only if
\Det(G)=\pm 1
and
g(\sqrt{\Delta_{0}})=-\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}
. If that is true, then
s^{\prime}
is automatically a fiducial datum, so this requirement does not need to be imposed as an additional assumption.

Before proving
Theorem

7.24
, we need to establish the following technical result:

Lemma 7.25
.

Let
t=(d,1,Q)\sim(K,j,1,Q)
be an admissible tuple, and let
n
be a positive integer coprime to 3. Then
d_{nj}
is divisible by
d_{j}=d
and

(7.71)

\displaystyle\frac{f_{nj}d_{nj}(1+d_{nj})}{f_{j}d_{j}}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}n(1+d_{j})+d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $d_{j}$ is even and $n\equiv\pm 2$ or $\pm 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
n(1+d_{j})\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

The fact that
d_{nj}
is divisible by
d_{j}
follows from
Theorem

4.8
and
Lemma

4.9
.

Suppose
d_{j}
is odd. It follows from
Lemma

4.10
that
d_{nj}
is odd. So
d_{nj}/d_{j}
is odd.
Theorem

4.8
and
Lemma

4.9
imply

(7.72)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{nj}}{d_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{T^{*}_{n}(d_{j})}{d_{j}}\equiv\begin{cases}n\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$,}\\
-n\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$.}\end{cases}

The fact that
d_{nj}/d_{j}
is odd means that, if
n
is also odd, we must have
d_{nj}/d_{j}\equiv\pm n\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)
, while if
n
is even, we must have
d_{nj}/d_{j}\equiv\pm n+d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)
. In other words:

(7.73)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{nj}}{d_{j}}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}n\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,6\right)$,}\\
-n+d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,6\right)$,}\\
n+d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 4\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,6\right)$,}\\
-n\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,6\right)$.}\end{cases}

Turning to the ratio
f_{nj}/f_{j}
,

Theorem

4.8
and
Lemma

4.9
imply

(7.74)

\displaystyle\frac{f_{nj}}{f_{j}}=U^{*}_{n}(d_{j})

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$,}\\
-1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$.}\end{cases}

It follows from
Lemma

4.9
that
U^{*}_{1}(d_{j})
is odd,
U^{*}_{2}(d_{j})
is even, and
U^{*}_{n}(d_{j})\equiv U^{*}_{n-2}(d_{j})\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
for all
n>2
. Consequently
U^{*}_{n}(d_{j})\equiv n\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
for every positive integer
n
. In conjunction with (
7.74
), this means that, if
n
is odd, then
f_{nj}/f_{j}\equiv\pm 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)
, while if
n
is even, then
f_{nj}/f_{j}\equiv\pm 1+d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)
. In other words:

(7.75)

\displaystyle\frac{f_{nj}}{f_{j}}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,6\right)$,}\\
-1+d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,6\right)$, }\\
1+d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 4\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,6\right)$,}\\
-1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,6\right)$.}\\
\end{cases}

Putting these results together, we conclude

\displaystyle\frac{f_{nj}d_{nj}(1+d_{nj})}{f_{j}d_{j}}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}n(1+nd_{j})\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,6\right)$,}\\
(-1+d_{j})(-n+d_{j})(1+d_{j}(-n+d_{j}))\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,6\right)$,}\\
(1+d_{j})(n+d_{j})(1+d_{j}(n+d_{j}))\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 4\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,6\right)$,}\\
n(1-nd_{j}))\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,6\right)$.}\end{cases}

(7.76)

\displaystyle\equiv n(1+d_{j})\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right).

Suppose, on the other hand, that
d_{j}
is even. Then it follows from
Theorem

4.8
and
Lemma

4.9
that

\displaystyle\frac{d_{nj}}{d_{j}}=\frac{T^{*}_{n}(d_{j})}{d_{j}}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}n+\left(\frac{n(n-1)}{6}\right)d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$,}\\
-n+\left(\frac{n(n+1)}{6}\right)d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$,}\end{cases}

(7.77)

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}n\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 1,4\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
-n+d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 2,5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
n+d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 7,10\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
-n\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 8,11\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle\frac{f_{nj}}{f_{j}}=U^{*}_{n}(d_{j})

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}1+\left(\frac{n-1}{3}\right)d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$,}\\
-1+\left(\frac{n+1}{3}\right)d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)$,}\end{cases}

(7.78)

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 1,7\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
-1+d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 2,8\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
1+d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 4,10\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
-1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 5,11\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$.}\end{cases}

Combining these results, we find

\displaystyle\frac{f_{nj}d_{nj}(1+d_{nj})}{f_{j}d_{j}}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}n(1+nd_{j})\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
(-n+d_{j})(-1+d_{j})(1+d_{j}(-n+d_{j}))\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 2\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
n(1+d_{j})(1+nd_{j})\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 4\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
(n-d_{j})(1+d_{j}(-n+d_{j}))\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
(n+d_{j})(1+d_{j}(n+d_{j}))\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 7\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
n(1-d_{j})(1-nd_{j})\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 8\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
(n+d_{j})(1+d_{j})(1+d_{j}(n+d_{j}))\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 10\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
n(1-nd_{j})\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv 11\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\end{cases}

(7.79)

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}n(1+d_{j})\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv\pm 1,\pm 4\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
n(1+d_{j})+d_{j}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2d_{j}\right)\qquad&\text{if $n\equiv\pm 2,\pm 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\end{cases}

completing the proof.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

7.24
.

The fact that
t^{\prime}
is admissible is immediate. Referring to
Definition

1.30
, we see

(7.80)

\displaystyle\phi_{\kappa\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s_{d_{nj}}(\kappa\mathbf{p})}e^{-\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(A_{t^{\prime}})}\xi_{d_{nj}}^{-\frac{f_{nj}}{f}Q(\kappa\mathbf{p})}

where
f
is the conductor of
Q
. We first show

(7.81)

\displaystyle(-1)^{s_{d_{nj}}(\kappa\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}(-1)^{ns_{d_{j}}(\mathbf{p})}.

Indeed, if
d_{j}
is odd, then it follows from
Lemma

4.10
that
d_{nj}
is odd, implying

(7.82)

\displaystyle(-1)^{s_{d_{nj}}(\kappa\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle=-1=(-1)^{n+1}(-1)^{ns_{d_{j}}(\mathbf{p})}.

On the other hand, if
d_{j}
is even, then it follows from Lemmas
4.9
and
4.10
that
d_{nj}
is even and
\kappa=T^{*}_{n}(d_{j})/d_{j}\equiv n\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
, implying

(7.83)

\displaystyle(-1)^{s_{d_{nj}}(\kappa\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(1+np_{1})(1+np_{2})}=(-1)^{n+1}(-1)^{n(1+p_{1})(1+p_{2})}=(-1)^{n+1}(-1)^{ns_{d_{j}}(\mathbf{p})}.

Turning to the second factor on the right hand side of (
7.80
), observe that it follows from
Theorem

4.50
that

(7.84)

\displaystyle A^{\vphantom{n}}_{t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon^{3nj})=A_{t}^{n}

and

(7.85)

\displaystyle\Tr(A_{t})

\displaystyle=\Tr(\varepsilon^{3j})=d_{3j}-1>1.

Proposition

5.1
consequently implies

(7.86)

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(A_{t^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(A_{t}^{n})}=e^{-\frac{n\pi i}{12}\Psi(A_{t})}.

Finally, using
Lemma

7.25
, the last
factor on the right hand side of (
7.80
) becomes

\displaystyle\xi_{d_{nj}}^{-\frac{f_{nj}}{f}Q(\kappa\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle=e^{-\left(\frac{\pi i}{d_{j}}\right)\left(\frac{f_{nj}d_{nj}(1+d_{nj})}{d_{j}f_{j}}\right)\left(\frac{f_{j}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})\right)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}e^{-\left(\frac{\pi i}{d_{j}}\right)\left(n(1+d_{j})+d_{j}\right)\left(\frac{f_{j}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})\right)}\qquad&\text{if $d_{j}$ is even and $n\equiv\pm 2$ or $\pm 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
e^{-\left(\frac{\pi i}{d_{j}}\right)\left(n(1+d_{j})\right)\left(\frac{f_{j}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})\right)}\qquad&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(7.87)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(-1)^{\frac{f_{j}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})}\xi_{d_{j}}^{-\frac{nf_{j}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p}}\qquad&\text{if $d_{j}$ is even and $n\equiv\pm 2$ or $\pm 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,12\right)$,}\\
\xi_{d_{j}}^{-\frac{nf_{j}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p}}\qquad&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

It follows from
Lemma

5.3
that, if
d_{j}
is even, then

(7.88)

\displaystyle(-1)^{\frac{f_{j}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p_{1}^{2}+p_{1}p_{2}+p_{2}^{2}}=(-1)^{n+1}(-1)^{n+(1+p_{1})(1+p_{2})}.

Hence

(7.89)

\displaystyle\xi_{d_{nj}}^{-\frac{f_{nj}}{f}Q(\kappa\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}(-1)^{\ell(\mathbf{p})}\xi_{d_{j}}^{-\frac{nf_{j}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})}.

Combining these results, we deduce

(7.90)

\displaystyle\phi_{\kappa\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell(\mathbf{p})}(-1)^{ns_{d}(\mathbf{p})}e^{-\frac{n\pi i}{12}\Psi(A_{t})}\xi_{d_{j}}^{-\frac{nf_{j}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})}=(-1)^{\ell(\mathbf{p})}\left(\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\right)^{n}.

Using (
7.84
), together with the fact that
\rho_{t^{\prime}}=\rho_{Q,+}=\rho_{t}
(see
Definition

1.32
), we also find

(7.91)

\displaystyle\shin^{d_{nj}^{-1}\kappa\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t^{\prime}}}\!\left(\rho_{t^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=\shin^{d_{j}^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A^{n}_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right).

It follows from (
1.27
) that, for all
\tau\in\mathbb{H}
,

\displaystyle\shin^{d^{-1}_{j}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A^{n}_{t}}\!\left(\tau\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varpi\left(\left\langle\!\left\langle d^{-1}_{j}\mathbf{p},A^{n}_{t}\tau\right\rangle\!\right\rangle,A^{n}_{t}\tau\right)}{\varpi\left(\left\langle\!\left\langle d^{-1}_{j}\mathbf{p},\tau\right\rangle\!\right\rangle,\tau\right)}

(7.92)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varpi\left(\left\langle\!\left\langle d^{-1}_{j}\mathbf{p},A^{n}_{t}\tau\right\rangle\!\right\rangle,A^{n}_{t}\tau\right)}{\varpi\left(\left\langle\!\left\langle d^{-1}_{j}\mathbf{p},A^{n-1}_{t}\tau\right\rangle\!\right\rangle,A^{n-1}_{t}\tau\right)}\times\dots\times\frac{\varpi\left(\left\langle\!\left\langle d^{-1}_{j}\mathbf{p},A_{t}\tau\right\rangle\!\right\rangle,A_{t}\tau\right)}{\varpi\left(\left\langle\!\left\langle d^{-1}_{j}\mathbf{p},\tau\right\rangle\!\right\rangle,\tau\right)}

Taking the limit as
\tau\to\rho_{t}
and using the fact that
\rho_{t}
is a fixed point of
A_{t}
we deduce

(7.93)

\displaystyle\shin^{d_{nj}^{-1}\kappa\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t^{\prime}}}\!\left(\rho_{t^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\shin^{d_{j}^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)\right)^{n}.

Hence

(7.94)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\kappa\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime})=\phi_{\kappa\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime})\shin^{d_{nj}^{-1}\kappa\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t^{\prime}}}\!\left(\rho_{t^{\prime}}\right)=(-1)^{\ell(\mathbf{p})}\left(\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\right)^{n}\left(\shin^{d_{j}^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{t}\right)\right)^{n}=(-1)^{\ell(\mathbf{p})}\left(\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\right)^{n}

It follows from
Corollary

4.21
and
Lemma

4.4
that

(7.95)

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{\kappa\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime})}{\left(\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\right)^{n}}

\displaystyle=q\left(\frac{\tilde{\nu}_{\kappa\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime})}{\left(\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\right)^{n}}\right),

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{\kappa\mathbf{p}}(s^{\prime})}{\left(\mu_{\mathbf{p}}(s)\right)^{n}}

\displaystyle=q\left(\frac{\nu_{\kappa\mathbf{p}}(s^{\prime})}{\left(\nu_{\mathbf{p}}(s)\right)^{n}}\right),

where

(7.96)

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{(d_{j}+1)^{\frac{n}{2}}}{d_{\frac{nj}{2}}-1}\qquad&n\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right),\\
\frac{(d_{j}+1)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}}{1+\sum_{r=1}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}(-1)^{r}d_{rj}}\qquad&n\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right),\\
\frac{(d_{j}+1)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}}{2+\sum_{r=1}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}(-1)^{r}d_{rj}}\qquad&n\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right).\end{cases}

The fact that
q\in\mathbb{Q}
means we can use
Lemma

1.44
in conjunction with (
7.94
) and (
7.95
) to deduce

(7.97)

\displaystyle\frac{\nu_{\kappa\mathbf{p}}(s^{\prime})}{\left(\nu_{\mathbf{p}}(s)\right)^{n}}

\displaystyle=q^{-1}g\left(\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{\kappa GH_{g}^{-1}G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime})}{\left(\tilde{\mu}_{GH_{g}^{-1}G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}(t)\right)^{n}}\right)=g\left(\frac{\tilde{\nu}_{\kappa GH_{g}^{-1}G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime})}{\left(\tilde{\nu}_{GH_{g}^{-1}G^{-1}\mathbf{p}}(t)\right)^{n}}\right)=(-1)^{\ell(GH_{g}^{-1}G^{-1}\mathbf{p})}.

It remains to show that

(7.98)

\displaystyle(-1)^{\ell(GH_{g}^{-1}G^{-1}\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell(\mathbf{p})}.

The statement is immediate if
d_{j}
is odd, since then
\ell(\mathbf{p})=n+1
independently of
\mathbf{p}
. Suppose, on the other hand, that
d_{j}
is even. Let

(7.99)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=GH_{g}^{-1}G^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}.

The fact that
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/2d_{j}\mathbb{Z})
means
\Det M
is odd. It also means that at least one of
\alpha,\gamma
, and at least one of
\beta,\delta
must be odd, implying

(1+\alpha)(1+\gamma)
and
(1+\beta)(1+\delta)
are both even. Hence

\displaystyle(-1)^{(1+(M\mathbf{p})_{1})(1+(M\mathbf{p})_{2})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{1+(\alpha p_{1}+\beta p_{2})+(\gamma p_{1}+\delta p_{2})+(\alpha p_{1}+\beta p_{2})(\gamma p_{1}+\delta p_{2})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{1+(\alpha+\gamma+\alpha\gamma)p_{1}+(\beta+\delta+\beta\delta)p_{2}+(\alpha\delta+\beta\gamma)p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{1+p_{1}+p_{2}+(1+\alpha)(1+\gamma)p_{1}+(1+\beta)(1+\delta)p_{2}+\Det(M)p_{1}p_{2}}

(7.100)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(1+p_{1})(1+p_{2})}.

Equation

7.98
now follows. Hence

\nu_{\kappa\mathbf{p}}(s^{\prime})=(-1)^{\ell(\mathbf{p})}\left(\nu_{\mathbf{p}}(s)\right)^{n}
.
∎

8.
Necromancy and numerical computation

Suppose one wishes to use the preceding conjectures for constructing ghosts to compute an
explicit

r
-SIC fiducial, either exactly or just a numerical approximation.
We call any procedure for doing so
necromancy
, so-named because it “reanimates” the ghost fiducial as a
r
-SIC fiducial.
In this section, we describe a method for necromancy specialized to
1
-SICs.

There is a straightforward brute-force algorithm to achieve necromancy.
Using (
1.55
), we first compute a ghost fiducial to arbitrary precision.
We wish to round this numerical approximation into the closest point in a candidate number field to identify an exact representation of the ghost fiducial.
Powerful tools with polynomial runtimes such as the Lenstra–Lenstra–Lovász (LLL) lattice basis reduction algorithm or other integer relation algorithms can be employed here, though we note that finding a closest vector in a lattice is not believed to be efficient in general.
A conjecture for the specific number field is provided by the existing conjectures
[
14
,
73
,
74
]
.
Then a Galois automorphism that flips the sign of
\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}
can be applied to compute the
1
-SIC fiducial.
If the conjectures are correct, then with enough starting precision and patience for finding the exact representation, the result should be an exact expression for a
1
-SIC fiducial.
This exact expression can then be evaluated to any precision that one likes for numerical approximation.

Unfortunately this approach is impractical for two reasons.
The first difficulty is that the relevant number field typically has very high degree, and the precision required to round into this field would therefore be impractically large for even storing a
1
-SIC fiducial for modestly large
d
.
Second, the convergence of LLL or similar algorithms is either far too slow in practice with such high-degree number fields or yields too weak a guarantee on accuracy with the “true” closest point to allow a direct computation of a ghost in this fashion.

To circumvent these difficulties, we now describe an alternative heuristic approach to necromancy that avoids the complexity bottleneck by working entirely in a (typically much lower degree) ring class field.
One still requires very high precision already for moderately large dimensions.
For example, we required
\sim 10^{5}
digit precision in dimension
d=100
to implement this approach in detail.
However, this is not impractically large for a modern laptop.
The price for this reduced complexity is that the method itself is more involved.

We stress that the procedure for necromancy discussed in this section is at present only a heuristic, even assuming our conjectures.
However, we believe it should be possible to specify a complete algorithm which provably converges to a
1
-SIC assuming only our conjectures.
It is likely that the methods here extend to
r
-SICs for
r>1
, but we have not attempted to systematically approach numerical computation for
r>1
, so we leave this case to future work and focus on
1
-SICs for the rest of this section.

Let us first provide a high-level overview of our method of necromancy.
The first step is to calculate a numerical estimate of a ghost fiducial associated to an admissible tuple
t=(d,1,Q)
.
In
Section

8.2
we show how this can be done using the integral representation of the double sine function
[
95
,
72
]
.
It is not practical to use numerical integration to achieve the high precision required by the subsequent steps.
We therefore use it to calculate an initial, low precision fiducial, and then amplify its precision using Newton’s method, as described in
Section

8.3
.
We then describe in
Section

8.4
how to (numerically) compute a set of invariants that we call
ghost invariants
.
The exact versions of these ghost invariants conjecturally live in the ring class field
H_{t}
associated to
t
(recall
Definition

7.13
).
Since this is a field extension with substantially lower degree than the field containing the ghost overlaps, we can find an exact representation of the ghost invariants without too much difficulty using an integer relation algorithm.
Importantly, the ghost invariants contain enough information about the original ghost overlaps to reconstruct them up to an action of the Galois group.
Thus, as we show in
Section

8.5
, we can find exact Galois conjugates for the ghost invariants and from them obtain the subsequent “SIC invariants”.
The SIC invariants are at this point specified as exact numbers in a number field, but to make the remaining steps computationally tractable, we again resort to numerical approximations after the conversion step of passing from ghost to SIC.
Notably, we no longer need the ultra-high precision required in the initial rounding step.
From the SIC invariants, we can numerically reconstruct the SIC overlaps, again up to an action of the Galois group.
While in principle we can do this on each separate Galois orbit and then combine the results, we can apply an additional heuristic to avoid having to match Galois actions across multiple orbits.
In
Section

8.6
we describe how a convex optimization on a single maximal orbit, in every case tried, avoids the need to search for the unknown Galois action across multiple orbits.
The output of this procedure is a
1
-SIC fiducial vector of moderate precision; to gain confidence that this is a true
1
-SIC fiducial vector, one can again use Newton’s method to enhance the precision to any desired level.

8.1.
Numerical calculations

We have implemented the necromancy method described here in a free open-source Julia package called
Zauner.jl

[
42
]
, which makes essential use of
Hecke.jl

[
40
]
.
Using this implementation, we have calculated numerical approximations to all of the
1
-SICs in every dimension up to
d=20
, with the exception of the
1
-SIC in
d=12
, which has
F_{a}
symmetry.
We have not yet implemented necromancy for the
F_{a}
-symmetric case because of how the Galois group structure is presently computed in our software.
However, including
F_{a}
orbits should be a relatively straightforward extension that we hope to implement soon.

To see if we could achieve a moderately large dimension with our approach, we also used this algorithm to compute four numerical
1
-SICs in dimension
d=100
, three of which are new.
This was somewhat challenging computationally, and it seems likely that our current ideas will need refinement to allow computation of a
1
-SIC in, say,
d=1000
.

The only required input to the
necromancy
function is an admissible tuple
(d,1,Q)
, and in principle this function requires no fine-tuning to output a
1
-SIC.
As a practical matter however, the bottleneck in extending our current implementation to more dimensions is the convergence of the integer relation algorithm.
The efficacy of the method hinges on convergence to the correct element of the ring class field for every ghost invariant.
We find in practice that this is challenging to achieve within our current heuristics.
We hope to improve the speed, convergence, and generality of this code (in particular to
F_{a}
orbits and
r>1
) in future versions.

Throughout the remainder of this section, we are implicitly working with a fixed admissible tuple
(d,1,Q)
. We further assume that the twist
G=I
, so that the fiducial datum is
(d,1,Q,I,g)
for some
g
.
To ease notation, nowhere in this section do we explicitly label the dependence on the tuple or datum. In particular the generators of
\mathcal{S}(Q)
,
\mathcal{S}_{d}(Q)
specified in
Definition

1.28
will simply be denoted
L
,
A
respectively, and the root of
Q
appearing in
Definition

1.32
will simply be denoted
\rho
.

8.2.
Calculating the Shintani–Faddeev modular cocycle

The first step in our necromancy method is to compute a ghost fiducial vector.
In Definitions
1.32
,
1.43
,
a ghost fiducial corresponding to the admissible tuple
(d,r,Q)
is expressed in terms of the SF modular cocycle
\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A}\!\left(\rho\right)
.
This in turn is expressed in terms of the SF Jacobi cocycle
\sigma_{\!A}\!\left(z,\tau\right)
via (
1.26
).
For
\tau\in\mathbb{H}
the latter is given by a ratio of
q
-Pochhammer symbols via (
1.21
).
However, we need its values for
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
.
Although these can be obtained by taking a limit, it is numerically more efficient to calculate them directly, via an integral representation, using a procedure we now describe.

Let
L=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
be arbitrary. Recall (
Definition

4.28
) that
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
is generated by
T=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
S=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
If
\gamma=0
then either
L=T^{k}
, in which case
\sigma_{\!L}\!\left(z,\tau\right)=1
for all
z
,
\tau
, or
L=-T^{k}
, in which case the function is singular for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
.
If
\gamma<0
we can express
\sigma_{L}
in terms of
\sigma_{L^{-1}}
.
It is therefore sufficient to give a procedure for calculating the function when
\gamma>0
.
For this we use Theorem
C.4
to deduce the existence of a sequence of integers
r_{1},\dots,r_{n+1}
such that

(8.1)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=T^{r_{1}}S\dots ST^{r_{n+1}}

where
r_{j}\geq 2
for
1<j<n+1
.
The theorem also shows that if
L_{j}=T^{r_{j}}S\dots ST^{r_{n+1}}
then
\mathcal{D}_{L}=\mathcal{D}_{L_{1}}\subset\mathcal{D}_{L_{2}}\dots\subset\mathcal{D}_{L_{n+1}}
.
Consequently

(8.2)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\!L}\!\left(z,\tau\right)
