\displaystyle=\sigma_{\!T^{r_{1}}S}\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{L_{2}}(\tau)},L_{2}\cdot\tau\right)\dots\sigma_{\!T^{r_{n}}S}\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{L_{n+1}}(\tau)},L_{n+1}\cdot\tau\right)\sigma_{\!L_{n+1}}\!\left(z,\tau\right)

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{L}
.
Using

(8.3)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\!L_{n+1}}\!\left(\tau,z\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\!T^{r_{n+1}}}\!\left(z,\tau\right)=1

and

(8.4)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\!T^{m}S}\!\left(z,\tau\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\!T^{m}}\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{S}(\tau)},S\cdot\tau\right)\sigma_{\!S}\!\left(z,\tau\right)=\sigma_{\!S}\!\left(z,\tau\right),

this becomes

(8.5)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\!L}\!\left(z,\tau\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\!S}\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{L_{2}}(\tau)},L_{2}\cdot\tau\right)\dots\sigma_{\!S}\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{L_{n+1}}(\tau)},L_{n+1}\cdot\tau\right).

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{L}
.
The problem thus reduces to calculating
\sigma_{\!S}\!\left(z,\tau\right)
. This can be done using the formula
[
95
,
72
]

(8.6)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\!S}\!\left(z,\tau\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\frac{\pi i}{12\tau}\left(6z^{2}+6(1-\tau)z+\tau^{2}-3\tau+1\right)}}{\dbs(z+1,\tau)}

where
\dbs(z+1,\tau)
is the double sine function.
Note that the double sine function as usually defined has three arguments; we are employing the shorthand
[
72
]

\dbs(z,\tau,1)=\dbs(z,\tau)
. Note also that we are using the definition of Shintani
[
95
]
and Kurokawa and Koyama
[
77
]
which is prevalent in the mathematics literature, as opposed to the definition of Ponsot
[
84
]
which is more prevalent in the physics literature.
We can calculate
\dbs(z+1,\tau)
explicitly using the integral representation
[
84
,
72
]
,

(8.7)

\displaystyle\dbs(z+1,\tau)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\tau-1-2z}{2}\right)t}{2\sinh\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{\tau t}{2}\right)}-\frac{\tau-1-2z}{\tau t}\right)\frac{dt}{t}\right)

valid for
\re(\tau)>0
and
-1<\re(z)<\re(\tau)
.
To use this integral representation in (
8.5
), one needs, firstly, that
\re(L_{r}\cdot\tau)>0
for
r=2
, …,
n+1
.
A sufficient condition for that to be true is that
\re(j_{L}(\tau))>0
.
In particular, it is true for the case that interests us,
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{L}\cap\mathbb{R}
.
Indeed, let
\tau=x+iy
with
x
,
y\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then, in the notation of Theorem
C.4
,
\re(j_{L}(\tau))>0
implies
x+\delta_{r}/\gamma_{r}>0
for
r=2,\ldots,n+1
and, consequently,

\displaystyle\re\left(L_{r}\cdot\tau\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{\gamma_{r}}{\gamma_{r+1}}\left(\frac{\left(x+\frac{\delta_{r}}{\gamma_{r}}\right)\left(x+\frac{\delta_{r+1}}{\gamma_{r+1}}\right)+y^{2}}{\left(x+\frac{\delta_{r+1}}{\gamma_{r+1}}\right)^{2}+y^{2}}\right)\qquad&r=2,\dots,n\\
\gamma_{n+1}\left(x+\frac{\delta_{n+1}}{\gamma_{n+1}}\right)\qquad&r=n+1\end{cases}

(8.8)

\displaystyle>0

To deal with the problem that
\re\left(z/j_{L_{r}}(\tau)\right)
may not be in the required interval we may use the fact

(8.9)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\!S}\!\left(z+m_{1}\tau+m_{2},\tau\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varpi_{m_{1}}(z,\tau)}{\varpi_{-m_{2}}\left(\frac{z}{\tau},-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)}\sigma_{\!S}\!\left(z,\tau\right)

for all
m_{1}
,
m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
and all
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{S}
.

If we can calculate one
1
-SIC on a given extended Clifford group orbit, then we can easily calculate all the others by applying the appropriate unitary or anti-unitary transformation.
To make the most efficient use of available resources, one should choose the quadratic form
Q
appearing in the admissible tuple
t=(d,1,Q)
in such a way as to minimize the length of the expansion in (
8.1
).
It follows from Theorem
C.7
that to do this we need to choose
Q
to be HJ-reduced and such that the HJ-continued fraction expansion of
\beta_{Q,+}
has minimal period.
The HJ-reduced continued fraction then tells us the integers
r_{1}
, …,
r_{n+1}
in the expansion in (
8.1
) (see Appendix
C
for the definitions and a summary of the relevant properties of HJ-reduced forms and HJ-continued fractions).
Appropriate choices of
Q
are tabulated in Appendix
F
for
d=4
to
100
.

8.3.
Precision enhancement with Newton’s method

The method described below does not require us to calculate the full set of
d^{2}
ghost overlaps.
It does, however, require us to calculate a subset of size
\gg d\log(d)^{c}
for some absolute constant
c
.
To convert this subset into the corresponding subset of SIC overlaps requires the ghost overlaps to be calculated to very high precision (
10^{5}
digit precision in dimension 100).
Doing this using (
8.7
) alone would be prohibitively slow.
We therefore use the alternative method we now describe.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
\tilde{\Pi}
be a ghost
1
-SIC fiducial. Then there exists
|\psi\rangle\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
such that

(8.10)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}

\displaystyle=\lambda|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|U_{P}

where
\lambda=\langle\psi|U_{P}|\psi\rangle=\pm 1
.

Remark
.

We will refer to
|\psi\rangle
as the ghost SIC fiducial vector.

Proof.

The fact that
\tilde{\Pi}
is rank 1 means we can write it in the form

(8.11)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}

\displaystyle=|\psi\rangle\langle\phi|

for some pair of vectors satisfying
\langle\phi|\psi\rangle=1
. The fact that
\tilde{\Pi}_{s}
is a P-projector (see
Definition

1.13
and discussion following) means

(8.12)

\displaystyle|\phi\rangle\langle\psi|

\displaystyle=\left(|\psi\rangle\langle\phi|\right)^{\dagger}=U_{P}|\psi\rangle\langle\phi|U_{P}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle|\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\langle\phi|U_{P}|\psi\rangle}{\langle\psi|\psi\rangle}\right)U_{P}|\psi\rangle.

So

(8.13)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}

\displaystyle=\lambda|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|U_{P}

for some
\lambda
. The fact that
\Tr(\tilde{\Pi})=1
means
\lambda\langle\psi|U_{P}|\psi\rangle=1
. Our freedom to make the replacements
|\psi\rangle\to\sqrt{|\lambda|}|\psi\rangle
and
\lambda\to\lambda/|\lambda|
means we can choose
\lambda
,
|\psi\rangle
so that
\lambda=\langle\psi|U_{P}|\psi\rangle=\pm 1
.
∎

Expressed in terms of the ghost SIC fiducial vector,
(
1.62
) becomes

(8.14)

\displaystyle\lambda|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|U_{P}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\mathbf{p}}\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}D_{\mathbf{p}}.

(setting
G=I
, and dropping the
t
-label).
The simplest case is when none of the components of
|\psi\rangle
is zero.
In that case, in order to calculate the
2d-2
real numbers determining the complex vector
|\psi\rangle
(up to an overall constant) it suffices to know
2d
of the numbers
\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}
.
Specifically, let

(8.15)

\displaystyle\chi_{0,j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\omega_{d}^{jk}\tilde{\mu}_{0,k},

\displaystyle\chi_{1,j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\xi_{d}^{k}\omega_{d}^{jk}\tilde{\mu}_{1,k}.

The vector
|\psi\rangle
is only determined up to an arbitrary phase.
We may therefore assume, without loss of generality, that
\langle 0|\psi\rangle
is positive real.
We then have

(8.16)

\displaystyle\langle j|\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\sqrt{|\chi_{0,0}|}\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}\left(\frac{\chi_{1,k}}{\chi_{0,k}}\right),

with the convention that
\prod_{k=0}^{-1}f(k)=1
.
Our strategy is therefore to calculate low precision approximations to the
2d
numbers
\tilde{\mu}_{0,k}
,
\tilde{\mu}_{1,k}
using the integral representation and then use these to calculate a low precision approximation to the vector
|\psi\rangle
.
We then apply Newton’s method to the system of equations

(8.17)

\displaystyle\langle\psi|U_{P}D_{\mathbf{p}}|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|U_{P}D_{-\mathbf{p}}|\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{d\delta^{(d)}_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}+1}{d+1}

to calculate a high precision approximation to
|\psi\rangle
which in turn can be used to calculate high precision approximations to the numbers
\tilde{\mu}_{\rm{p}}
.
If it should happen that one or more of the components of
|\psi\rangle
is zero then it may be necessary to calculate more than
2d
low precision ghost overlaps.
However in no case is it necessary to calculate the numerical integral to high precision.

8.4.
Ghost invariants

We next describe our method for constructing a set of numbers in the ring class field
H
which fully specify the ghost overlaps defined from the admissible tuple
(d,1,Q)
on a maximal Galois orbit.

The method relies on the following empirical observation
8
8

8

While we do not have a proof of this observation at present, we believe it can be proven from our broader framework of conjectures and hope to show this in an upcoming paper.
:

Empirical Observation 8.2
.

If
\tilde{E}
is the field generated by the ghost overlaps, and if
H
is the ring class field, then there is an isomorphism of
\Gal(\tilde{E}/H)
onto
\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{S}
, where
\mathcal{S}
is the symmetry group (i.e., the set of
G\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
such that
\tilde{\mu}_{G\mathbf{p}}=\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}
for all
\mathbf{p}
) and
\mathcal{M}
is a maximal abelian subgroup of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
containing
\mathcal{S}
.
If
h\in\Gal(\tilde{E}/H)
and
F_{h}\mathcal{S}
is the corresponding element of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
, then

(8.18)

\displaystyle h(\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mu}_{F_{h}\mathbf{p}}.

for all
\mathbf{p}
.

Remark
.

This isomorphism was originally noted empirically by studying the known examples of
1
-SICs
[
7
,
11
]
.
For a type
z
orbit, there is only one maximal abelian subgroup containing
\mathcal{S}
(namely, the centralizer of
\mathcal{S}
).
The characterization of
\mathcal{M}
in the case of a type
a
orbits will appear in future work.

The key to our method is that, using this isomorphism, one can calculate the action of
\Gal(\tilde{E}/H)
on the ghost overlaps without knowing them exactly.

Let
\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}_{1}},\dots,\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}_{n}}
be a maximal orbit of ghost overlaps under the action of
\Gal(\tilde{E}/H)
.
Suppose that each element of the maximal orbit generates the full field
\tilde{E}
, and therefore is not stabilized by a non-identity element of
\Gal(\tilde{E}/H)
.
In the case of a maximal order, this provably follows from the Stark Conjectures (see Subsection
2.3
); for non-maximal orders we heuristically assume it holds.
Choose

L_{1}
, …,
L_{m}\in\mathcal{M}
such that

(1)

each
L_{j}\mathcal{S}
is order
n_{j}=q_{j}^{r_{j}}
in
\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{S}
with
q_{j}
prime and
r_{j}
a positive integer, and

(2)

\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{S}
is isomorphic to the direct product
\left<L_{1}\mathcal{S}\right>\times\dots\times\left<L_{m}\mathcal{S}\right>
.

So
n_{1}\dots n_{m}=n
, where
n
is the order of
\Gal(\tilde{E}/H)
.
Let
\tilde{h}_{j}
be the element of
\Gal(\tilde{E}/H)
such that

(8.19)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{j}(\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}})=\tilde{\mu}_{L_{j}\mathbf{p}}

for all
\mathbf{p}
, and let
\tilde{E}_{j}=\{c\in\tilde{E}\colon\tilde{h}_{k}(c)=c\text{ for all }k\neq j\}
.
Finally choose some fixed element of the orbit, say
\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}_{1}}
, and define

(8.20)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}_{s_{1},\dots,s_{m}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mu}_{L_{1}^{s_{1}}\dots L_{m}^{s_{m}}\mathbf{p}_{1}}.

Then the map
(s_{1},\dots,s_{m})\to\tilde{\mu}_{s_{1},\dots,s_{m}}
is a bijective correspondence between
\mathbb{Z}/n_{1}\mathbb{Z}\times\dots\times\mathbb{Z}/n_{m}\mathbb{Z}
and the orbit.
It follows that if we define sets

(8.21)

\displaystyle\tilde{K}_{j,t}

\displaystyle=\{\tilde{\mu}_{s_{1},\dots,s_{m}}\colon s_{j}=t\}

then their intersection is the single-element set

(8.22)

\displaystyle\bigcap_{j=1}^{m}\tilde{K}_{j,t_{j}}

\displaystyle=\{\tilde{\mu}_{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}}\}.

Let

(8.23)

\displaystyle\tilde{P}_{j,t}(x)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\tilde{\mu}\in\tilde{K}_{j,t}}(x-\tilde{\mu})=\sum_{l=0}^{n/n_{j}}\tilde{c}_{j,t,l}x^{l}.

Then the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{j,t,l}
are all in
\tilde{E}_{j}
.
9
9

9

In what follows we define
\tilde{c}_{j,t,l}
to be (essentially) the elementary symmetric polynomials of the ghost overlaps.
However, any basis of symmetric polynomials would suffice, and in our numerical calculations we have used power sums instead.
Using power sums offers some computational advantages, but adds extra steps of changing bases to an already lengthy procedure, so we omit these details.

So one approach to the problem of calculating the SIC overlaps would be to calculate exact versions of the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{j,t,l}
using an integer-relation algorithm, transform them using a
\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}
–sign switching automorphism, find the roots of the transformed polynomial, and then find the SIC overlaps using the transformed version of (
8.22
).
Provided
m>1
, this method would be more efficient than using an integer relation algorithm to calculate exact expressions for the
\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}
since
\tilde{E}_{j}
is then lower degree than
\tilde{E}
.
However, we can do better than that, by defining a set of numbers which are all in
H
, instead of
\tilde{E}_{j}
, and which are therefore easier to calculate from their numerical counterparts using an integer relation algorithm.
We refer to these numbers as
ghost invariants
.

Our method relies on the following fact.

Lemma 8.3
.

For each
j
there exists at least one index
\ell
such that the numbers
\{\tilde{c}_{j,t,\ell}\colon t=0,1,\dots,n_{j}-1\}
are distinct non-zero.
If
\ell
is such an index, then
\tilde{E}_{j}=H(\tilde{c}_{j,0,\ell})
.

Proof.

Suppose there were no such index
\ell
.
Since
\Gal(\tilde{E}_{j}/H)=\langle\tilde{h}_{j}\rangle
is cyclic order
n_{j}
, where
n_{j}
is a power of a prime, and since
\tilde{c}_{j,t,\ell}=\tilde{h}_{j}^{t}(\tilde{c}_{j,0,\ell})
, it would follow that for each
\ell
there existed a positive integer
r(\ell)<r_{j}
such that

(8.24)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{j}^{q_{j}^{r(\ell)}}(\tilde{c}_{j,t,\ell})=\tilde{c}_{j,t,\ell}

for all
t
. Defining
r=\max_{\ell}r(\ell)
, this would mean that

(8.25)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{j}^{q_{j}^{r}}(\tilde{c}_{j,t,\ell})=\tilde{c}_{j,t,\ell}

for all
t
,
\ell
,
This in turn would mean
\tilde{K}_{j,q_{j}^{r}}=\tilde{K}_{j,0}
, contradicting the fact that the sets
\tilde{K}_{j,0\vphantom{q_{j}^{r_{j}}}},\ldots,\tilde{K}_{j,q_{j}^{r_{j}}-1}
are disjoint.

To prove the second statement, suppose
H(\tilde{c}_{j,0,\ell})
were a proper subfield of
\tilde{E}_{j}
.
Then it would be fixed by
\tilde{h}_{j}^{q_{j}^{r}}
, for some positive integer
r<r_{j}
.
But that would mean
\tilde{c}_{j,q_{j}^{r},\ell}=\tilde{c}_{j,0\vphantom{q_{j}^{r}},\ell}
, contradicting the fact that the coefficients are distinct.
∎

In view of Lemma
8.3
, we can choose for each
j
an index
\ell_{j}
such that
\tilde{E}_{j}=H(\tilde{c}_{j,0,\ell_{j}})
.
We then have, for each
\ell
, a set of numbers
\tilde{a}_{j,r,\ell}\in H
such that

(8.26)

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{j,0,\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{n_{j}-1}\tilde{a}_{j,r,\ell}\tilde{c}_{j,0,\ell_{j}}^{r}.

Repeatedly applying
\tilde{h}_{j}
to both sides, it follows that

(8.27)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{c}_{j,0,\ell}\\
\vdots\\
\tilde{c}_{j,n_{j}-1,\ell}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\tilde{V}_{j}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{a}_{j,0,\ell}\\
\vdots\\
\tilde{a}_{j,n_{j}-1,\ell}\end{pmatrix}

where
\tilde{V}_{j}
is the Vandermonde matrix

(8.28)

\displaystyle\tilde{V}_{j}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&\tilde{c}_{j,0,\ell_{j}}&\dots&\tilde{c}^{n_{j}-1}_{j,0,\ell_{j}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
1&\tilde{c}_{j,n_{j}-1,\ell_{j}}&\dots&\tilde{c}^{n_{j}-1}_{j,n_{j}-1,\ell_{j}}\end{pmatrix}.

In the case
\ell=\ell_{j}
we also have

(8.29)

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{j,1,\ell_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{n_{j}-1}\tilde{b}_{j,r}\tilde{c}_{j,0,\ell_{j}}^{r}

for some
\tilde{b}_{j,r}\in H
, from which it follows that

(8.30)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{c}_{j,1,\ell_{j}}\\
\vdots\\
\tilde{c}_{j,n_{j}-1,\ell_{j}}\\
\tilde{c}_{j,0,\ell_{j}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\tilde{V}_{j}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{b}_{j,0}\\
\vdots\\
\tilde{b}_{j,n_{j}-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Equation (
8.30
) implies

(8.31)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{j}\big(\tilde{c}_{j,t,\ell_{j}}\big)=\tilde{Q}_{j}\big(\tilde{c}_{j,t,\ell_{j}}\big)

for all
t
, where

(8.32)

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u=0}^{n_{j}-1}\tilde{b}_{j,u}x^{u}

Following the terminology of
[
10
]
, we refer to the
\tilde{Q}_{j}(x)
as
Galois polynomials
.

Numerical approximations to the numbers
\tilde{a}_{j,t,\ell}
,
\tilde{b}_{j,t}
can be obtained from (
8.27
) and (
8.30
) by solving the linear system.
Stable solution of Vandermonde linear systems can be done with only
O(n_{j}^{2})
operations
[
18
,
57
]
.

Finally, define numbers
\tilde{e}_{j,t}
by

(8.33)

\displaystyle\tilde{e}_{j,0}+\tilde{e}_{j,1}x+\dots+\tilde{e}_{j,n_{j}-1}x^{n_{j}-1}+x^{n_{j}}=\prod_{t=0}^{n_{j}-1}(x-\tilde{c}_{j,t,\ell_{j}})

The numbers
\tilde{a}_{j,t,\ell}
,
\tilde{b}_{j,t}
,
\tilde{e}_{j,t}
are the ghost invariants we introduced earlier.
They are all in the ring class field
H
, and exact versions can be calculated using an integer relation algorithm starting from high precision numerical approximations.
It will be seen that there are
n_{1}+\dots+n_{m}
invariants
\tilde{e}_{j,t}
,
n_{1}+\dots+n_{m}
invariants
\tilde{b}_{j,t}
, and
nm-(n_{1}+\dots+n_{m})
invariants
\tilde{a}_{j,t,\ell}
with
\ell\neq\ell_{j}
, or
nm+n_{1}+\dots n_{m}
invariants in total. By contrast there are only
n
ghost overlaps
\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}_{1}},\dots,\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}_{n}}
on the orbit. However, the numbers
\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}_{k}}
are in the much larger field
\tilde{E}
, and exact values are correspondingly harder to calculate. Note that we lose some information in going from the ghost overlaps to the ghost invariants. However, from a knowledge of the Galois invariants it is possible to narrow down the corresponding ghost overlaps to a manageable set of alternatives, from which the correct set can be extracted by a process of trial and error. The procedure is described in the next section, when we describe the process of constructing a SIC from the appropriate Galois conjugates of the invariants.

8.5.
Constructing the SIC overlaps

Now let
E
be the field generated by the SIC overlaps, and let
g
be any automorphism which switches the sign of
\sqrt{\Delta_{0}}
.
For the sake of simplicity, assume
g(\omega_{d})=\omega_{d}
(although it is straightforward to construct a modified version of the argument which works when this condition is not satisfied).
Under our conjectures and assumptions (see also
[
12
]
), the automorphism
g
maps
\tilde{E}
onto
E
, and the map
h\mapsto ghg^{-1}
is an isomorphism of
\Gal(\tilde{E}/H)
onto
\Gal(E/H)
.
Define

(8.34)

\displaystyle\Pi

\displaystyle=g(\tilde{\Pi}),

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=g(\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}),

\displaystyle\mu_{s_{1},\dots,s_{m}}

\displaystyle=g(\tilde{\mu}_{s_{1},\dots,s_{m}}),

\displaystyle c_{j,t,l}

\displaystyle=g(\tilde{c}_{j,t,l}),

\displaystyle h_{j}

\displaystyle=g\tilde{h}_{j}g^{-1},

where
\tilde{\Pi}
is the ghost projector with which we started. Then
\Pi
is a
1
-SIC projector, and
\mu_{\mathbf{p}}=\Tr(\Pi D^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}})
are the corresponding SIC overlaps. Furthermore, it follows from (
8.19
), (
8.20
) that

(8.35)

\displaystyle h_{j}(\mu_{\mathbf{p}})

\displaystyle=\mu_{L_{j}\mathbf{p}}

for all
j
,
\mathbf{p}
, and

(8.36)

\displaystyle\mu_{s_{1},\dots,s_{m}}

\displaystyle=h_{1}^{s_{1}}\dots h_{m}^{s_{m}}(\mu_{\mathbf{p}_{1}})=\mu_{L_{1}^{s_{1}}\dots L_{m}^{s_{m}}\mathbf{p}_{1}}

for all
s_{1},\dots,s_{m}
.
Also define
K_{j,t}
to be the set of roots of the equation

(8.37)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{n/n_{j}}c_{j,t,\ell}x^{\ell}=0.

Then it follows from (
8.22
) and (
8.23
) that

(8.38)

\displaystyle\bigcap_{j=1}^{m}K_{j,t_{j}}

\displaystyle=\{\mu_{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}}\}.

Of course, the fact that we only have numerical approximations for
\tilde{\Pi}
,
\tilde{\mu}_{\mathbf{p}}
, and
c_{j,t,\ell}
means that we cannot calculate
\Pi
,
\mu_{\mathbf{p}}
, or
c_{j,t,\ell}
directly.
However, we do have exact expressions for the ghost invariants. Moreover, the fact that the ghost invariants are all in
H
means it is straightforward to calculate the corresponding
SIC invariants

(8.39)

\displaystyle a_{j,t,\ell}

\displaystyle=g(\tilde{a}_{j,t,\ell}),

\displaystyle b_{j,t}

\displaystyle=g(\tilde{b}_{j,t}),

\displaystyle e_{j,t}

\displaystyle=g(\tilde{e}_{j,t}).

The fact that one loses some information in going from the ghost fiducial to the ghost invariants means the SIC invariants do not specify
\Pi
unambiguously.
However, they do contain enough information to enable us to construct a set of candidate operators, from which a set of
1
-SIC fiducial projectors which includes
\Pi
can be extracted without too much difficulty, using the method we now describe.

Let

(8.40)

\displaystyle Q_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u=0}^{n_{j}-1}b_{j,u}x^{u}.

Then

(8.41)

\displaystyle h_{j}\big(c_{j,t,\ell_{j}}\big)

\displaystyle=Q_{j}\big(c_{j,t,\ell_{j}}\big).

We now find the
c_{j,t,\ell_{j}}
using the fact that they are the roots of the equation

(8.42)

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{n_{j}-1}e_{j,t}x^{t}

\displaystyle=0.

The problem is, of course, that we do not
ab initio
know which particular root is equal to which particular coefficient
c_{j,t,\ell_{j}}
. To deal with this problem, we choose a root at random and label it
c^{\prime}_{j,0,\ell_{j}}
. We then define

c^{\prime}_{j,t,\ell_{j}}
recursively by

(8.43)

\displaystyle c^{\prime}_{j,t+1,\ell_{j}}

\displaystyle=Q_{j}\big(c^{\prime}_{j,t,\ell_{j}}\big)

so that

(8.44)

\displaystyle c_{j,t,\ell_{j}}

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}_{j,t+r_{j},\ell_{j}}

for all
t
,
j
and some unknown
j
-dependent constant
r_{j}
. We then extend the definition of
c^{\prime}_{j,t,\ell}
to arbitrary values of
\ell
by setting

(8.45)

\displaystyle c^{\prime}_{j,t,\ell}

\displaystyle=c_{j,t-r_{j},\ell}

for all
j,t,\ell
. We then have

(8.46)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}c^{\prime}_{j,0,\ell}\\
\vdots\\
c^{\prime}_{j,n_{j}-1,\ell}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=V^{\prime}_{j}\begin{pmatrix}a_{j,0,\ell}\\
\vdots\\
a_{j,n_{j}-1,\ell}\end{pmatrix}

for all
j,\ell
, where

(8.47)

\displaystyle V^{\prime}_{j}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&c^{\prime}_{j,0,\ell_{j}}&\dots&c^{\prime n_{j}-1}_{j,0,\ell_{j}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
1&c^{\prime}_{j,n_{j}-1,\ell_{j}}&\dots&c^{\prime n_{j}-1}_{j,n_{j}-1,\ell_{j}}\end{pmatrix}

Equation

8.46
together with our knowledge of the
c^{\prime}_{j,t,\ell_{j}}
and
a_{j,t,\ell}
enables us to calculate
c^{\prime}_{j,t,\ell}
for all triples
(j,t,\ell)
.

Now let
K^{\prime}_{j,t}
be the roots of the equation

(8.48)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{n/n_{j}}c^{\prime}_{j,t,\ell}x^{\ell}=0.

Then
K_{j,t}=K^{\prime}_{j,t+r_{j}}
. In view of (
8.38
), this means that if we define
\mu^{\prime}_{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}}
to be the unique member of the set

(8.49)

\displaystyle\bigcap_{j=1}^{m}K^{\prime}_{j,t_{j}},

then
\mu_{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}}=\mu^{\prime}_{t_{1}+r_{1},\dots,t_{m}+r_{m}}
for all
t_{1},\dots,t_{m}
.

Using the values of the
c^{\prime}_{j,t,\ell}
, we can calculate the values of the quantities
\mu^{\prime}_{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}}
, which in turn fixes the quantities
\mu_{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}}
up to the unknown shifts
r_{1},\dots,r_{m}
.
It is convenient to rephrase this slightly. Given an arbitrary element
\mathbf{p}
of the
\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{S}
-orbit of
\mathbf{p}_{1}
, choose
t_{1},\dots,t_{m}
and
M\in\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathbf{p}=L_{1}^{t_{1}}\dots L_{m}^{t_{m}}M\mathbf{p}_{1}
and define

(8.50)

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}_{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=\mu^{\prime}_{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}}.

In view of (
8.36
) we then have

(8.51)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=\mu_{L_{1}^{t_{1}}\dots L_{m}^{t_{m}}M\mathbf{p}_{1}}=\mu_{L_{1}^{t_{1}}\dots L_{m}^{t_{m}}\mathbf{p}_{1}}=\mu_{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}}=\mu^{\prime}_{t_{1}+r_{1},\ldots,t_{m}+r_{m}}=\mu^{\prime}_{L\mathbf{p}}

where

(8.52)

\displaystyle L=L^{r_{1}}\dots L^{r_{m}}

is an element of
\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{S}
, which we determine by trial-and-error.

There are two ways in which we can reduce the size of the search space. In the first place, if we only want
some

1
-SIC on the same
\EC(d)
orbit as
\Pi
, not necessarily
\Pi
itself, we can use the fact that if
L^{\prime}L^{-1}\in\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
then
\{\mu^{\prime}_{L^{\prime}\mathbf{p}}\}
is a set of SIC overlaps if and only if
\{\mu^{\prime}_{L\mathbf{p}}\}
is. It is consequently only necessary to test one element from each coset
\mathcal{M}/\bigl(\mathcal{M}\cap\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})\bigr)
.

The search space can be further reduced using the following condition. Let
\mathcal{P}
be the set
\{M\mathbf{p}_{1}\colon M\in\mathcal{M}\}
, and define

(8.53)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}}\mu_{\mathbf{p}}D_{\mathbf{p}}.

The fact that
\mathcal{M}
is maximal abelian means
-I\in\mathcal{M}
, which in turn implies
B
is Hermitian, and
\Tr(B(\Pi-B))=0
. Let
\lambda_{\rm{max}}
be the largest eigenvalue of
B
. Then

(8.54)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\rm{max}}

\displaystyle\geq\Tr(B\Pi)=\Tr(B^{2})=\frac{|\mathcal{P}|}{d(d+1)}

where
|\mathcal{P}|
is the cardinality of
\mathcal{P}
. We may therefore remove from consideration any set of candidate overlaps which do not satisfy this requirement.

8.6.
Convex optimization

At this point, we have managed to construct an orbit of
\mathcal{M}
acting on
\mathbf{p}\in(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})^{2}
and for which we know numerical approximations of the associated
\mu_{\mathbf{p}}
up to a shift by the action of an unknown element
M\in\mathcal{M}
.
In general, the group action of
\mathcal{M}
will split the
\mathbf{p}
into more than one orbit.
In that case, the
k
th orbit can be learned in the above fashion.
However, the unknown matrix shift will in general be different for each orbit, so it must be guessed
simultaneously
across all orbits if one wishes to reconstruct all
d^{2}-1
nontrivial values of
\mu_{\mathbf{p}}
.
The total number of possibilities will in general grow exponentially in the number of orbits, which is an undesirably large search space.
It might be that there are efficient ways to search this space, but we are not aware of any.
We will instead describe an alternative method based on convex optimization that requires only knowing a single (correctly shifted) orbit of sufficient size.

Our idea is to use
low-rank matrix recovery
, which is a matrix analog of the better-known method of compressive sensing.
For simplicity, consider a square
d\times d
matrix
X\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
.
In the low-rank matrix recovery problem, one is given a collection of
measurements

A_{k}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
and one learns the value of the
observations

b_{k}
, which are linear functions
b_{k}=\Tr(A_{k}X)
obtained by acting on an unknown matrix
X
.
Written in a vector notation, we have
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{A}(X)
where the measurements are now a linear map
\mathbf{A}:\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d})\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
and the observations are
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
.
One wishes to recover
X
from knowledge of measurements
\mathbf{A}
and observations
\mathbf{b}
.
If
m=d^{2}=\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{A})
, then the solution is trivially
X=\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{b}
.

Suppose that our measurements are expensive to implement, and we wish to recover
X
from
m\ll d^{2}
observations, and hence
\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{A})\leq m
.
In this case, it is clear that there is no unique solution since
X
and
X+Y
are indistinguishable for nonzero
Y
in the null space of
\mathbf{A}
.

In many cases of interest, one has the additional knowledge that the unknown matrix
X
has rank
r\ll d
.
One might hope that this fact would allow efficiently recovering
X
from far fewer measurements, despite the original problem being ill-posed.
After all, information-theoretically there are only
O(rd)
parameters specifying
X
.
This intuition turns out to be correct: It suffices to make
m=O\bigl(rd(\log d)^{c}\bigr)
observations from a typical set of certain randomized measurements
[
54
,
53
]
, where
c
is an absolute constant.
The algorithm which efficiently reconstructs
X
from only
m
observations is a convex relaxation of the naive algorithm that finds a minimal-rank matrix among those consistent with the observations.
The convex relaxation (which can be cast as a semidefinite program) returns the answer:

(8.55)

\displaystyle X^{\star}=\arg\min_{Y}\|Y\|_{1}\mbox{ s.t.\ }\mathbf{A}(Y)=\mathbf{b},

where
\|Y\|_{1}
is the Schatten 1-norm, or the sum of the singular values of
Y
.

The final step in our necromancy procedure is now clear.
Choose a specific large orbit
\mathcal{P}
and reconstruct the
\mu_{\mathbf{p}}
along that orbit, up to an unknown matrix
M\in\mathcal{M}
, using the methods above.
In our examples, we have always chosen a maximal orbit, and in every case we’ve observed this orbit was unique and had a size many times larger than
d
.
If our prior computations and conjectures are correct, then our unknown
1
-SIC fiducial projector
\Pi
satisfies the constraints

(8.56)

\displaystyle\mu_{M\mathbf{p}}=\Tr\bigl(D^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}}\Pi\bigr),\ \mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}.

We also know that
\Pi=\Pi^{\dagger}
and
\Pi\succeq 0
.
Since
\Pi
is positive semidefinite, the Schatten 1-norm becomes simply the trace, so we do not enforce the constraint
\Tr(\Pi)=1
to avoid trivializing the objective function.
We then output the matrix that minimizes
\Tr(\Pi)
subject to the constraints (
8.56
) as well as the Hermitian and positive semidefinite constraints.
If the result is not (numerically) a rank-1 matrix, we simply try another candidate matrix
M
and run the semidefinite program again.
One can gain further confidence in a numerical solution by applying Newton’s method, similar to
Section

8.3
, to enhance the precision to any desired level.

The number of candidate solutions that must be checked is always at most the order of
\left|\mathcal{M}\right|
, which is at most polynomial in
d
.
The semidefinite program also runs in polynomial time in
d
.
Therefore, at least in this formal sense, this procedure is efficient with respect to the dimension.

Appendix A
Alternative fiducial data

In
Definition

1.30
, we defined the SF phase corresponding to the admissible tuple
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
by

(A.1)

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{p})}e^{-\tfrac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(A_{t})}\xi_{d}^{-\tfrac{f_{jm}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})}

with

(A.2)

\displaystyle s_{d}(\mathbf{p})=d+(1+d)(1+p_{1})(1+p_{2}).

However, as we will see below, it would have been possible to have replaced
s_{d}(\mathbf{p})
with the more general expression

(A.3)

\displaystyle s_{d}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=d+(1+d)(1+p_{1})(1+p_{2})+(1+d)\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{p}\rangle

for arbitrary
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
Similarly, in
Definition

1.32
, we defined the candidate normalized ghost overlaps corresponding to the admissible tuple
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
by

(A.4)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,+}\right).

However, as we will see below, it would have been possible to replace
\rho_{Q,+}
with
\rho_{Q,-}
. The reason we did not make either of these choices in the main text is because they do not lead to new
r
-SICs, as we now show.

In this appendix, we modify the notation used elsewhere in the paper, so as to include an explicit dependence on
\mathbf{w}
and root
\rho_{Q,\pm}
. Let
s=(t,G,g)
be a fiducial datum containing the admissible tuple
t=(d,r,Q)\sim(K,j,m,Q)
, let
f
be the conductor of
Q
, let
\rho
be either of the two roots of
Q
, and let
\rho,\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. We define

(A.5)

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{p})}e^{-\tfrac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(A_{t})}\xi_{d}^{-\tfrac{f_{jm}}{f}Q(\mathbf{p})}

where
s_{d}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{p})
is as given by (
A.3
),

(A.6)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w},\rho)

\displaystyle=\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w})\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho\right),

(A.7)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s}(\mathbf{w},\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{G\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w},\rho)D^{\vphantom{t}}_{\mathbf{p}}

(A.8)

\displaystyle\Pi_{s}(\mathbf{w},\rho)

\displaystyle=g\left((\tilde{\Pi}_{s}(\mathbf{w},\rho)\right).

The purpose of this appendix is to prove

Theorem A.1
.

Assume Conjectures
1.35
and
1.50
are true.
Let
s=(d,r,Q,G,g)
be a fiducial datum, let
\rho
be either of the two roots of
Q
, and let
\mathbf{w}
be any element of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
Then there exists a form
Q^{\prime}
such that

(A.9)

\displaystyle\Pi_{s}(\mathbf{w},\rho)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{s^{\prime}}(\mathbf{0},\rho_{Q^{\prime},+})

where
s^{\prime}=(d,r,Q^{\prime},G,g)
.

It follows from this result that there is no loss of generality if, as in the rest of the paper, we confine ourselves to the case
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{0}
,
\rho=\rho_{Q,+}
.

In order to prove
Theorem

A.1
we need first to prove the following lemma.

Lemma A.2
.

Assume Conjectures
1.35
and
1.50
are true.
Let
s=(d,r,Q,G,g)
be a fiducial datum, and let
\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Then
s^{\prime}=(d,r,-Q,G,g)
is also a fiducial datum, and

(A.10)

\displaystyle U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{P}\Pi_{s}(\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})U^{\dagger}_{P}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{s^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w},\rho_{-Q,\mp})

(A.11)

\displaystyle U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}}\Pi_{s}(\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})U^{\dagger}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{s}(\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{w}^{\prime},\rho_{Q,\pm})

where

M_{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&w^{\prime}_{1}d\\
w^{\prime}_{2}d&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
P
is the parity matrix (see
Definition

3.4
).

Proof.

Let
t=(d,r,Q)
,
t^{\prime}=(d,r,-Q)
. Assuming
Conjecture

1.35
is true, the fact that
Q
and
-Q
have the same discriminant means
\mathcal{Z}_{t}=\mathcal{Z}_{t^{\prime}}
(see
Definition

1.34
). Also it follows from
Conjecture

1.50
that
E_{t}=E_{t^{\prime}}
. So
G
satisfies (
1.60
) for some
\lambda\in\mathcal{Z}_{t^{\prime}}
and
g\in\Gal(E_{t}/\mathbb{Q})
. It follows that
s^{\prime}
is a fiducial datum.

It is easily seen that
A^{\vphantom{-1}}_{t^{\prime}}=A^{-1}_{t}
and
\rho_{-Q,\pm}=\rho_{Q,\mp}
. In view of
Proposition

5.1
this means

(A.12)

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime},\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{p})}e^{-\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(A^{-1}_{t})}\xi_{d}^{\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q}(\mathbf{p})=\left(\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w})\right)^{-1},

for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, while it follows from
Lemma

2.13
that

(A.13)

\displaystyle\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t^{\prime}}}\!\left(\rho_{-Q,\mp}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,\pm}\right)\right)^{-1}

for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Hence

(A.14)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime},\mathbf{w},\rho_{-Q,\mp})

\displaystyle=\phi_{\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime},\mathbf{w})\shin^{d^{-1}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t^{\prime}}}\!\left(\rho_{-Q,\mp}\right)=\left(\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})\right)^{-1}.

Taking account of
Theorem

5.8
we deduce

(A.15)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime},\mathbf{w},\rho_{-Q,\mp})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\nu}_{-\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})

for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that
\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Consequently

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{s^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w},\rho_{-Q,\mp})

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{G\mathbf{p}}(t^{\prime},\mathbf{w},\rho_{-Q,\mp})D_{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{-G\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})D_{\mathbf{p}}

(A.16)

\displaystyle=U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{P}\tilde{\Pi}_{s}(\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})U^{\dagger}_{P}.

It follows from (
3.17
) that the matrix elements of
U_{P}
are all in
\mathbb{Z}
. So (
A.16
) and
(
A.8
) imply

(A.17)

\displaystyle\Pi_{s^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w},\rho_{-Q,\mp})

\displaystyle=g\left(\tilde{\Pi}_{s^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w},\rho_{-Q,\mp})\right)=U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{P}\Pi_{s}(\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})U^{\dagger}_{P},

thereby proving (
A.10
). Turning to (
A.11
), observe that the statement is trivial if
d
is odd. Suppose, on the other hand, that
d
is even. Then it follows from (
A.7
) that

(A.18)

\displaystyle U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}}\tilde{\Pi}_{s}(\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})U^{\dagger}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{GM^{-1}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})D_{\mathbf{p}}.

Observe that
M^{-1}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=M^{\vphantom{-1}}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}
as an element of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/2d\mathbb{Z})
. Since
M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\right)
, it follows that

(A.19)

\displaystyle(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{w},GM^{-1}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{w},G\mathbf{p})}.

It follows from
Corollary

5.4
that

(A.20)

\displaystyle\xi_{d}^{-\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q\left(GM^{-1}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p}\right)}

\displaystyle=\xi_{d}^{-a(GM_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p})_{1}^{2}-b(GM_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p})_{1}(GM_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p})_{2}-c(GM_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p})_{2}^{2}}

where
a
,
b
,
c
are odd. Setting
G=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, we have

(A.21)

\displaystyle\xi_{d}^{-a(GM_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p})_{1}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\xi_{d}^{-a\left((G\mathbf{p})_{1}+d(\beta(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{2}p_{1}+\alpha(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{1}p_{2})\right)^{2}}=\xi_{d}^{-a(G\mathbf{p})_{1}^{2}},

(A.22)

\displaystyle\xi_{d}^{-c(GM_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p})_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\xi_{d}^{-c\left((G\mathbf{p})_{2}+d(\delta(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{2}p_{1}+\gamma(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{1}p_{2})\right)^{2}}=\xi_{d}^{-c(G\mathbf{p})_{2}^{2}},

and

\displaystyle\xi_{d}^{-b(GM_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p})_{1}(GM_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p})_{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\xi_{d}^{-b\left((G\mathbf{p})_{1}+d(\beta(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{2}p_{1}+\alpha(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{1}p_{2})\right)\left((G^{-1}\mathbf{p})_{2}+d(\delta(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{2}p_{1}+\gamma(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{1}p_{2})\right)}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=(-1)^{(G\mathbf{p})_{1}(\delta(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{2}p_{1}+\gamma(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{1}p_{2})+(G\mathbf{p})_{2}(\beta(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{2}p_{1}+\alpha(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{1}p_{2})}\xi_{d}^{-b(G\mathbf{p})_{1}(G\mathbf{p})_{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=(-1)^{(\alpha p_{1}+\beta p_{2})(\delta(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{2}p_{1}+\gamma(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{1}p_{2})+(\gamma p_{1}+\delta p_{2})(\beta(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{2}p_{1}+\alpha(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{1}p_{2})}\xi_{d}^{-b(G\mathbf{p})_{1}(G\mathbf{p})_{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=(-1)^{(\alpha\delta+\beta\gamma)((G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{2}p_{1}^{2}+(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{1}p_{2}^{2})}\xi_{d}^{-b(G\mathbf{p})_{1}(G\mathbf{p})_{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=(-1)^{\Det(G)((G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{2}p^{\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}}_{1}+(G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime})_{1}p^{\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}}_{2})}\xi_{d}^{-b(G\mathbf{p})_{1}(G\mathbf{p})_{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=(-1)^{\Det(G)\langle G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime},\mathbf{p}\rangle}\xi_{d}^{-b(G\mathbf{p})_{1}(G\mathbf{p})_{2}}

(A.23)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=(-1)^{\langle\mathbf{w}^{\prime},G\mathbf{p}\rangle}\xi_{d}^{-b(G\mathbf{p})_{1}(G\mathbf{p})_{2}}.

Putting all this together, we conclude

(A.24)

\displaystyle\xi_{d}^{-\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q(GM^{-1}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\langle\mathbf{w}^{\prime},G\mathbf{p}\rangle}\xi_{d}^{-\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q(G\mathbf{p})},

and, consequently,

\displaystyle\phi_{GM^{-1}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s_{d}\left(\mathbf{w},GM^{-1}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p}\right)}e^{-\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(A_{t})}\xi_{d}^{-\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q\left(GM^{-1}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p}\right)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{w},G\mathbf{p})+\langle\mathbf{w}^{\prime},G\mathbf{p}\rangle}e^{-\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(A_{t})}\xi_{d}^{-\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q\left(G\mathbf{p}\right)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s_{d}(\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{w}^{\prime},G\mathbf{p})}e^{-\frac{\pi i}{12}\Psi(A_{t})}\xi_{d}^{-\frac{f_{jm}}{f}Q\left(G\mathbf{p}\right)}

(A.25)

\displaystyle=\phi_{G\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{w}^{\prime}).

The fact that
M^{-1}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
implies, in view of
Lemma

2.14
, that

(A.26)

\displaystyle\shin^{d^{-1}GM^{-1}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,\pm}\right)

\displaystyle=\shin^{d^{-1}G\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,\pm}\right)

for all
\mathbf{p}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d\right)
.
Hence

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{GM^{-1}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})

\displaystyle=\phi_{GM^{-1}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w})\shin^{d^{-1}GM^{-1}_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,\pm}\right)

\displaystyle=\phi_{G\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\shin^{d^{-1}G\mathbf{p}}_{\!A_{t}}\!\left(\rho_{Q,\pm}\right)

(A.27)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\nu}_{G\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{w}^{\prime},\rho_{Q,\pm}).

Consequently

\displaystyle U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}}\tilde{\Pi}_{s}(\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})U^{\dagger}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{d}I+\frac{1}{d\sqrt{d_{j}+1}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\notin d\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\tilde{\nu}_{G\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{w}^{\prime},\rho_{Q,\pm})D_{\mathbf{p}}

(A.28)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Pi}_{s}(\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{w}^{\prime},\rho_{Q,\pm}).

Write

(A.29)

\displaystyle G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}\\
\ell_{2}\end{pmatrix}

for
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z}
. It follows from
[
4
, Thm. 1]
that

(A.30)

\displaystyle U_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=e^{i\theta}X^{-\frac{d\ell_{1}}{2}}Z^{-\frac{d\ell_{2}}{2}}

for some
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
such that
e^{i\theta}\in\mathbb{Q}(\xi_{d})
(see
Definition

1.5
for
X
,
Z
). We have

(A.31)

\displaystyle X^{-\frac{d\ell_{1}}{2}}Z^{-\frac{d\ell_{2}}{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{d}(-1)^{\ell_{2}j}\left|j+\tfrac{\ell_{1}d}{2}\right>\left<\vphantom{\tfrac{\ell_{1}d}{2}}j\right|.

So the matrix elements of
X^{-\frac{d\ell_{1}}{2}}Z^{-\frac{d\ell_{2}}{2}}
are all in
\mathbb{Z}
.
Hence

\displaystyle\Pi_{s}(\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{w}^{\prime},\rho_{Q,\pm})

\displaystyle=g\left(\tilde{\Pi}_{s}(\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{w}^{\prime},\rho_{Q,\pm})\right)

\displaystyle=g\left(U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}}\tilde{\Pi}_{s}(\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})U^{\dagger}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}}\right)

\displaystyle=g\left(\left(X^{-\frac{d\ell_{1}}{2}}Z^{-\frac{d\ell_{2}}{2}}\right)\tilde{\Pi}_{s}(\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})\left(X^{-\frac{d\ell_{1}}{2}}Z^{-\frac{d\ell_{2}}{2}}\right)^{\dagger}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(X^{-\frac{d\ell_{1}}{2}}Z^{-\frac{d\ell_{2}}{2}}\right)g\left(\tilde{\Pi}_{s}(\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})\right)\left(X^{-\frac{d\ell_{1}}{2}}Z^{-\frac{d\ell_{2}}{2}}\right)^{\dagger}

(A.32)

\displaystyle=U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}}\Pi_{s}(\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})U^{\dagger}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}},

completing the proof.
∎

We are now ready to prove the main result of this Appendix.

Proof of
Theorem

A.1
.

We first show that, given an arbitrary fiducial datum
s=(d,r,Q,G,g)
there exists a datum
s^{\prime}=(d,r,Q^{\prime},G,g)
such that

(A.33)

\displaystyle\Pi_{s}(\mathbf{0},\rho_{Q,-})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{s^{\prime}}(\mathbf{0},\rho_{Q^{\prime},+}).

Indeed,
Lemma

A.2
implies

(A.34)

\displaystyle\Pi_{s}(\mathbf{0},\rho_{Q,-})

\displaystyle=U^{\dagger}_{P}\Pi_{s^{\prime\prime}}(\mathbf{0},\rho_{-Q,+})U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{P}

where
s^{\prime\prime}
is the datum
(d,r,-Q,G,g)
. It then follows from Theorems
7.4
and
7.5
that there exists
R\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that

(A.35)

\displaystyle U^{\dagger}_{P}\Pi_{s^{\prime\prime}}(\mathbf{0},\rho_{-Q,+})U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{P}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{s^{\prime\prime}_{R}}(\mathbf{0},\rho_{-Q_{R},+}).

Equation

A.33
then follows by combining these statements and setting
Q^{\prime}=-Q_{R}
,
s^{\prime}=s^{\prime\prime}_{R}
.

Now consider the general case. Given an arbitrary datum
s=(d,r,Q,G,g)
, an arbitrary root
\rho_{Q,\pm}
, and arbitrary
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, it follows from
Lemma

A.2
and the result just proved that

\displaystyle\Pi_{s}(\mathbf{w},\rho_{Q,\pm})

\displaystyle=U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}}}\Pi_{s}(\mathbf{0},\rho_{Q,\pm})U^{\dagger}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}}}

(A.36)

\displaystyle=U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}}}\Pi_{s^{\prime\prime}}(\mathbf{0},\rho_{Q^{\prime\prime},+})U^{\dagger}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}}}

for some fiducial datum
s^{\prime\prime}=(d,r,Q^{\prime\prime},G,g)
. It then follows from Theorems
7.4
and
7.5
that there exists
R\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that

(A.37)

\displaystyle U^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}}}\Pi_{s^{\prime\prime}}(\mathbf{0},\rho_{Q^{\prime\prime},+})U^{\dagger}_{M_{G^{-1}\mathbf{w}}}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{s^{\prime\prime}_{R}}(\mathbf{0},\rho_{Q^{\prime\prime}_{R},+}).

Setting
s^{\prime}=s^{\prime\prime}_{R}
,
Q^{\prime}=Q^{\prime\prime}_{R}
the result follows.
∎

Appendix B
Canonical order
3
unitaries

The purpose of this appendix is to prove
Theorem

3.13
, characterizing the conjugacy classes of the elements of
\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
having trace equal to
d-1
.
Our starting point is Lemma 9.2 in Bos and Waldron
[
20
]
, which describes the conjugacy classes of the elements of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z})
having trace equal to
-1
.
We proceed in two steps:

(1)

We first use Bos and Waldron’s result to prove an analogous result for the elements of
\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z})
having trace equal to
-1
.

(2)

We then use this to prove the result for the elements of
\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
having trace equal to
d-1
.

Let
F_{z}
,
F_{a}
,
F^{\prime}_{a}
be as specified in
Definition

3.12
, and let
\bar{F}_{z}
,
\bar{F}_{a}
,
\bar{F}^{\prime}_{a}
be their reductions modulo
d
.
Thus

(B.1)

\displaystyle\bar{F}_{z}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\bar{F}_{a}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&3\\
\frac{d-3}{3}&-2\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\bar{F}^{\prime}_{a}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&3\\
\frac{2d-3}{3}&-2\end{pmatrix}.

We begin by stating the result of Bos and Waldron on which we rely.
As in the rest of this paper we restrict ourselves to the case
d\geq 4
.

Lemma

(Lemma 9.2 in Bos and Waldron
[
20
]
)
.

The set of matrices in
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z})
having trace equal to
-1
consists of

(1)

The single conjugacy class
[\bar{F}_{z}]
if
d\not\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
,

(2)

The two disjoint conjugacy classes
[\bar{F}^{\vphantom{-1}}_{z}]
,
[\bar{F}^{-1}_{z}]
if
d\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
,

(3)

The three disjoint conjugacy classes
[\bar{F}^{\vphantom{-1}}_{z}]
,
[\bar{F}^{-1}_{z}]
,
[\bar{F}^{\vphantom{-1}}_{a}]
if
d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
,

(4)

The three disjoint conjugacy classes
[\bar{F}^{\vphantom{-1}}_{z}]
,
[\bar{F}^{-1}_{z}]
,
[\bar{F}^{\prime}_{a}]
if
d\equiv 6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
,

where the notation
[G]
means “conjugacy class of
G
considered as an element of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z})
”.

Remark
.

Note that Bos and Waldron state a more general version of the lemma applicable to all
d\geq 2
; in particular to
d=3
, which requires special treatment.

The next result says that extending to
\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z})
reduces the number of conjugacy classes.

Lemma B.1
.

The set of matrices in
\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z})
having determinant equal to
+1
and trace equal to
-1
consists of

(1)

The single conjugacy class
[\bar{F}_{z}]
if
d\not\equiv 3,6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
.

(2)

The two disjoint conjugacy classes
[\bar{F}^{\vphantom{-1}}_{z}]
,
[\bar{F}^{\vphantom{-1}}_{a}]
if
d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
,

(3)

The two disjoint conjugacy classes
[\bar{F}^{\vphantom{-1}}_{z}]
,
[\bar{F}^{\prime}_{a}]
if
d\equiv 6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
,

where the notation
[G]
means “conjugacy class of
G
considered as an element of
\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z})
”.

Proof.

Let

(B.2)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}

Then

(B.3)

\displaystyle M\bar{F}^{\vphantom{-1}}_{z}M^{-1}

\displaystyle=\bar{F}^{-1}_{z},

implying
[\bar{F}^{-1}_{z}]=[\bar{F}^{\vphantom{-1}}_{z}]
for all
d
.

To see that
[\bar{F}_{z}]
and
[\bar{F}_{a}]
are disjoint when
d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
, assume on the contrary that

(B.4)

\displaystyle\bar{F}_{z}

\displaystyle=G\bar{F}_{a}G^{-1}

for some
G\in\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z})
. Since
\bar{F}_{a}\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
, it would follow that
\bar{F}_{z}\equiv I\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
, which is a contradiction.

The fact that
[\bar{F}^{\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}}_{z}]
and
[\bar{F}^{\prime}_{a}]
are disjoint when when
d\equiv 6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
is proved similarly.
∎

We now use this to prove
Theorem

3.13
.
If
d
is odd, then
\bar{F}^{\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}}_{z}=F^{\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}}_{z}
,
\bar{F}^{\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}}_{a}=F^{\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}}_{a}
,
\bar{F}^{\prime}_{a}=F^{\prime}_{a}
, and the theorem is an immediate consequence of
Lemma

B.1
.

Suppose, on the other hand, that
d
is even. Making the replacement
d\mapsto 2d
in
Lemma

B.1
, and using the fact that
d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)\iff 2d\equiv 6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
and
d\equiv 6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)\iff 2d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
, we find
that the set of matrices in
\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})

having determinant equal to
+1
and trace equal to
-1
consists of:

(1)

The single conjugacy class

(B.5)

\displaystyle\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\right]

if
d\not\equiv 3,6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
;

(2)

The two disjoint conjugacy classes

(B.6)

\displaystyle\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\right],

\displaystyle\left[\begin{pmatrix}1&3\\
\frac{4d-3}{3}&-2\end{pmatrix}\right]

if
d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
;

(3)

The two disjoint conjugacy classes

(B.7)

\displaystyle\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\right],

\displaystyle\left[\begin{pmatrix}1&3\\
\frac{2d-3}{3}&-2\end{pmatrix}\right]

if
d\equiv 6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
.

Now suppose that
F\in\ESL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/\bar{d}\mathbb{Z})
has determinant
+1
and trace equal to
d-1
. Then
G=(d+1)F
has trace equal to
-1
, so the result just proved implies that

(B.8)

\displaystyle F\in[(d+1)G]

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&d-1\\
d+1&d-1\end{pmatrix}\right]

if
d\not\equiv 3,6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
,

(B.9)

\displaystyle F\in[(d+1)G]

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&d-1\\
d+1&d-1\end{pmatrix}\right]

or

\displaystyle\left[\begin{pmatrix}d+1&d+3\\
\frac{d-3}{3}&-2\end{pmatrix}\right]

if
d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
, and

(B.10)

\displaystyle F\in[(d+1)G]

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&d-1\\
d+1&d-1\end{pmatrix}\right]

or

\displaystyle\left[\begin{pmatrix}d+1&d+3\\
\frac{5d-3}{3}&-2\end{pmatrix}\right]

if
d\equiv 6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
. Using

(B.11)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
d&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}d+1&d+3\\
\frac{d-3}{3}&-2\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
d&1\end{pmatrix}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&d+3\\
\frac{4d-3}{3}&d-2\end{pmatrix}=F_{a},

(B.12)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
d&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}d+1&d+3\\
\frac{5d-3}{3}&-2\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
d&1\end{pmatrix}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&d+3\\
\frac{2d-3}{3}&d-2\end{pmatrix}=F^{\prime}_{a},

we deduce

(B.13)

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\in[F_{z}]

\displaystyle\text{if $d\not\equiv 3,6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)$},

(B.14)

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\in[F_{z}]\text{ or }[F_{a}]

\displaystyle\text{if $d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)$},

(B.15)

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\in[F_{z}]\text{ or }[F^{\prime}_{a}]

\displaystyle\text{if $d\equiv 6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)$}.

The fact that
[F_{z}]
,
[F_{a}]
are disjoint when
d\equiv 3\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
follows from the fact that
F_{a}=I\not\equiv F_{z}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
. Similarly, if
d\equiv 6\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,9\right)
, then
F^{\prime}_{a}=I\not\equiv F^{\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}}_{z}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,3\right)
, implying
[F^{\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}}_{z}]
,
[F^{\prime}_{a}]
are disjoint.

Theorem

3.13
now follows.

Appendix C
Hirzebruch–Jung continued fractions

The purpose of this appendix is to show how continued fraction expansions can be used to choose a quadratic form which minimizes the length of the expansion on the right hand side of (
8.1
)
Expansions of the form

(C.1)

\displaystyle[k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4},\dots]_{+}

\displaystyle=k_{1}+\cfrac{1}{k_{2}+\cfrac{1}{k_{3}+\cfrac{1}{k_{4}+\cdots}}}

are extremely well-known and are described in considerable detail in standard texts such as
[
55
,
28
]
. Following Popescu-Pampu
[
85
]
we refer to them as
Euclidean (E-) continued fractions
.
In this paper we need a different kind of expansion, which Popescu-Pampu refers to as a
Hirzebruch–Jung (HJ-) continued fraction
, of the form

(C.2)

\displaystyle[k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4},\dots]_{-}

\displaystyle=k_{1}-\cfrac{1}{k_{2}-\cfrac{1}{k_{3}-\cfrac{1}{k_{4}-\cdots}}}\,.

In the literature
[
65
,
59
,
61
,
60
,
94
,
1
,
82
,
41
,
67
,
68
,
85
,
79
,
78
,
19
]
such fractions are also described as backwards, negative-regular, minus, reduced regular, and by-excess continued fractions.
For the convenience of the reader in this appendix we collect their essential properties.
Since we are only concerned with HJ-continued fractions in this paper, we will drop the subscript, and simply denote them
[k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4},\dots]
.
We also review the related concept of a
Hirzebruch–Jung (HJ-) reduced form
.

For all
x\in\mathbb{Q}
(respectively
x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
), there exists a unique finite (respectively infinite) sequence of integers
k_{j}
such that
x=[k_{1},k_{2}\dots]
, the
k_{j}\geq 2
for all
j\geq 2
, and (in case the sequence is infinite) there is no integer
m
such that
k_{j}=2
for all
j\geq m
.

Define =
(k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{n})
recursively by

(C.3)

\displaystyle(k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{n})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}k_{1}\qquad&n=1,\\
k_{1}k_{2}-1\qquad&n=2,\\
(k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{n-1})k_{n}-(k_{1},k_{2}\dots,k_{n-2})\qquad&n>2.\end{cases}

We refer to these quantities as
HJ-convergents
. They can be calculated using the following modified version of Euler’s rule: First take the product of all
n
numbers
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}
, then subtract all products obtained by omitting a pair of adjacent numbers, then add all products obtained by omitting two different pairs of adjacent numbers, and so on.
In particular, they are symmetric under reversal:

(C.4)

\displaystyle(k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{n-1},k_{n})

\displaystyle=(k_{n},k_{n-1},\dots,k_{2},k_{1}).

One has

(C.5)

\displaystyle[k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{n}]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(k_{1})\qquad&n=1,\\
\frac{(k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{n})}{(k_{2},\dots,k_{n})}\qquad&n\geq 2,\end{cases}

for finite HJ-expansions,

(C.6)

\displaystyle[k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{n},\dots]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{(k_{1})[k_{n+1},k_{n+2},\dots]-1}{[k_{n+1},k_{n+2},\dots]}\vphantom{\Big(}\qquad&n=1,\\
\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})[k_{n+1},k_{n+2},\dots]-(k_{1})}{(k_{2})[k_{n+1},k_{n+2},\dots]-1}\vphantom{\Big(}\qquad&n=2,\\
\frac{(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})[k_{n+1},k_{n+2},\dots]-(k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1})}{(k_{2},\dots,k_{n})[k_{n+1},k_{n+2},\dots]-(k_{2},\dots,k_{n-1})}\vphantom{\Big(}\qquad&n>2,\end{cases}

for infinite HJ-expansions, and

(C.7)

\displaystyle T^{k_{1}}ST^{k_{2}}S\cdots T^{k_{n}}S

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}(k_{1})&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\qquad&n=1,\\
\begin{pmatrix}(k_{1},k_{2})&-(k_{1})\\
(k_{2})&-1\end{pmatrix}\qquad&n=2,\\
\begin{pmatrix}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})&-(k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1})\\
(k_{2},\dots,k_{n})&-(k_{2},\dots,k_{n-1})\end{pmatrix}\qquad&n\geq 3.\end{cases}

In particular

(C.8)

\displaystyle T^{k_{1}}ST^{k_{2}}S\cdots T^{k_{n}}S.\left([k_{n+1},k_{n+2},\dots]\right)

\displaystyle=[k_{1},k_{2},\dots]

for all
n
.
This last relation is the reason HJ-continued fractions are relevant to this paper.

A continued fraction is said to be
periodic
if it is infinite and of the form

(C.9)

\displaystyle[j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{m},\overline{k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{n}}]

\displaystyle=[j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{m},k_{1},\dots,k_{n},k_{1},\dots,k_{n},k_{1},\dots,k_{n},\dots]

It is said to be
purely periodic
if it is of the form
[\overline{k_{1},\dots,k_{n}}]
. If
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}
doesn’t break into two or more identical subsequences, then we say that
n
is the
period
of
[\overline{k_{1},\dots,k_{n}}]
.

The HJ-continued fraction expansion of a real number is periodic if and only if it is an irrational element of a real quadratic field. Let
\rho
be such a number. Then its HJ-continued fraction expansion is purely periodic if and only if
\rho>1>\rho^{\prime}>0
, where
\rho^{\prime}
is its Galois conjugate.

There is a close connection between purely periodic HJ-continued fractions and a class of quadratic forms that we now define. A form
Q=\langle a,b,c\rangle
with discriminant
\Delta=b^{2}-4ac
is reduced in the ordinary sense
[
21
,
22
]
, or (as we will say)
Euclidean (E-) reduced
, if

(C.10)

\displaystyle 0<\sqrt{\Delta}-b<2|a|<\sqrt{\Delta}+b.

Forms of this type have a connection with Euclidean continued fractions. Specifically, the number
\tfrac{b+\sqrt{\Delta}}{2|a|}
has a purely periodic E-continued fraction expansion if and only if
Q
is E-reduced. Correspondingly we say
Q
is
Hirzebruch–Jung (HJ-) reduced
if

(C.11)

\displaystyle 0<-\sqrt{\Delta}-b<2|a|<\sqrt{\Delta}-b.

The number
\tfrac{-b+\sqrt{\Delta}}{2|a|}
has a purely periodic HJ-continued fraction expansion if and only if
Q
is HJ-reduced.

Let
Q=\langle a,b,c\rangle
and
J=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Then
Q_{J}=\langle-a,b,-c\rangle
(see
Section

1.3
).
It follows that
Q_{J}
is E-reduced (respectively HJ-reduced) if and only if
Q
is E-reduced (respectively HJ-reduced), in which case they define the same purely periodic E-continued (respectively HJ-continued) fraction. We may therefore, without loss of generality, confine ourselves to E-reduced (respectively HJ-reduced) forms
\langle a,b,c\rangle
for which
a>0
.
In the following this restriction will be assumed without comment.
The map taking
Q
to
-\rho_{Q,-}
(respectively
\rho_{Q,+}
) is then a bijective correspondence of the set of E-reduced (respectively HJ-reduced) forms onto the set of purely periodic E-continued (respectively HJ-continued) fractions.

There is a well-known algorithm
[
21
,
22
]
for calculating the complete set of E-reduced forms on a given
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
orbit.
We now show how this can be used to construct the complete set of HJ-reduced forms on the same orbit.
Let
W
be the forms
\langle a,b,c\rangle
on a
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
orbit for which
a>0
, and let

(C.12)

\displaystyle W_{\rm{E}}

\displaystyle=\{Q\in W\colon\rho_{Q,-}<-1<0<\rho_{Q,+}<1\}

(C.13)

\displaystyle W_{\rm{HJ}}

\displaystyle=\{Q\in W\colon 0<\rho_{Q,-}<1<\rho_{Q,+}\}

(where
\rho_{Q,\pm}
are given by (
1.35
)).
Then
W_{\rm{E}}
(respectively
W_{\rm{HJ}}
) is precisely the set of E-reduced (respectively HJ-reduced) forms in
W
. Also define, for
n=0,1,\dots
,

(C.14)

\displaystyle W_{\rm{E}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\left\{Q\in W\colon\rho_{Q,-}<-1<0<\rho_{Q,+}<\frac{1}{n+1}\right\},

(C.15)

\displaystyle W_{\rm{HJ}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\left\{Q\in W\colon\frac{n}{n+1}<\rho_{Q,-}<\frac{n+1}{n+2}<1<\rho_{Q,+}\right\}.

Then

(C.16)

\displaystyle W_{\rm{E}}=W_{\rm{E}}^{(0)}\supseteq W_{\rm{E}}^{(1)}\supseteq\dots,

\displaystyle\bigcap_{n=0}^{\infty}W_{\rm{E}}^{(n)}=\emptyset,

(C.17)

\displaystyle W_{\rm{HJ}}^{(n)}\cap W_{\rm{HJ}}^{(n^{\prime})}=\emptyset\qquad\text{if $n\neq n^{\prime}$},

\displaystyle\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}W_{\rm{HJ}}^{(n)}=W_{\rm{HJ}}.

Since
W_{\rm{E}}
is finite, non-empty there must exist
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
W_{\rm{E}}^{(n)}=\emptyset
if and only if
n\geq n_{0}
.
Let

(C.18)

\displaystyle L_{n}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}n&-1\\
n+1&-1\end{pmatrix}.

Then
x<-1
if and only if
n/(n+1)<L_{n}\cdot x<(n+1)/(n+2)
, and
0<x<1/(n+1)
if and only if
1<L_{n}\cdot x
. In view of
Lemma

4.48
, this means the map
Q\to Q_{L^{-1}_{n}}
is a bijection of
W_{\rm{E}}^{(n)}
onto
W_{\rm{HJ}}^{(n)}
. It follows that the cardinality of
W_{\rm{HJ}}
is at most
n_{0}
times the cardinality of
W_{\rm{E}}
. It also provides an algorithm for calculating the set
W_{\rm{HJ}}
, given the set
W_{\rm{E}}
.

Lemma C.1
.

Suppose
k_{j}\geq 2
for all
j
. Then

(C.19)

\displaystyle(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})>(k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1})>\dots>(k_{1},k_{2})>(k_{1})>1

Proof.

Straightforward consequence of the definition.
∎

Lemma C.2
.

Suppose

(C.20)

\displaystyle T^{k_{1}}ST^{k_{2}}S\cdots T^{k_{n}}S

\displaystyle=L^{m}

for some sequence of integers
k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{n}
all greater than 1 and some positive integer
m
. Then
n=\ell m
for some positive integer
\ell
,
k_{j+\ell}=k_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,n-\ell
, and

(C.21)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}T^{k_{1}}S\cdots T^{k_{\ell}}S\qquad&\text{if $m$ is odd},\\
\pm T^{k_{1}}S\cdots T^{k_{\ell}}S\qquad&\text{if $m$ is even}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
\bar{T}
,
\bar{S}
be the images of
T
,
S
under the canonical projection
h\colon\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\to\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, and let
\bar{R}=\bar{T}\bar{S}
. Then (see for example
[
2
]
)
\bar{S}
is order
2
,
\bar{R}
is order
3
, and every element of
\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
is either the identity or else has a unique alternating expansion of the form
\bar{M}_{1}\cdots\bar{M}_{n}
where: (1) each
\bar{M}_{j}
is either
\bar{S}
or
\bar{R}^{k}
for
k=1
or
2
, and (2) terms equal to
\bar{S}
alternate with terms equal to a non-zero power of
\bar{R}
. Also define
\bar{S}_{1}=\bar{S}
,
\bar{S}_{2}=\bar{S}\bar{R}\bar{S}
,
\bar{S}_{2}=\bar{S}\bar{R}\bar{S}\bar{R}\bar{S}
, ….
Now let
\bar{L}=h(L)
and let
\bar{L}=\bar{M}_{1}\cdots\bar{M}_{q}
be its expansion in terms of alternating powers of
\bar{S}
and
\bar{R}
. It follows from (
C.20
) that

(C.22)

\displaystyle\bar{L}^{m}=\bar{R}\bar{S}_{k_{1}-1}\bar{R}^{2}\bar{S}_{k_{2}-1}\bar{R}^{2}\cdots\bar{R}^{2}\bar{S}_{k_{n}-1}\bar{R}.

So
\bar{M}_{1}=\bar{M}_{q}=\bar{R}
, and

(C.23)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\bar{R}\bar{S}_{\kappa_{1}-1}\bar{R}^{2}\cdots\bar{S}_{\kappa_{\ell}-1}\bar{R}

for some sequence of integers
\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{\ell}
all greater than one. Equation (
C.22
) and the uniqueness of the alternating expansion then imply
n=m\ell
,
k_{j}=\kappa_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,\ell
, and
k_{j+\ell}=k_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,n-\ell
. Equation (
C.21
) then follows.
∎

Theorem C.3
.

Let
Q=\langle a,b,c\rangle
be a form with
a>0
, let
f
be its conductor, and let

(C.24)

\displaystyle\rho_{Q,+}

\displaystyle=[j_{1},\dots,j_{q},\overline{k_{1},\dots,k_{p}}]

(where we set
q=0
if
\rho_{Q,+}
has a purely periodic expansion equal to
[\overline{k_{1},\dots,k_{p}}]
).
Assume the sequences
j_{1},\dots,j_{q}
and
k_{1},\dots,k_{p}
are as short as possible (i.e.,
k_{1},\dots,k_{p}
is not the conjunction of two or more identical subsequences, and
j_{q}\neq k_{p}
). Then

(C.25)

\displaystyle\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon_{f})

\displaystyle=(T^{j_{1}}S\cdots T^{j_{q}}S)(T^{k_{1}}S\cdots T^{k_{p}}S)(T^{j_{1}}S\cdots T^{j_{q}}S)^{-1}.

Proof.

Assume, to begin with, that
q=0
and
\rho_{Q,+}=[\overline{k_{1},\dots,k_{p}}]
. Let

(C.26)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=T^{k_{1}}S\cdots T^{k_{p}}S.

Then it follows from (
C.7
) that

(C.27)

\displaystyle M\rho_{Q,+}

\displaystyle=\rho_{Q,+}.

In view of
Lemma

4.48
, this means

M\in\mathcal{S}(Q)
.
It then follows from
Theorem

4.50
that

(C.28)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=s_{1}\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon_{f}^{s_{2}\lambda})=s_{1}\left(\frac{d_{\lambda j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-1}{2}I+\frac{s_{2}f_{\lambda j_{\rm{min}}(f)}}{f}SQ\right),

where
\lambda
is a positive integer, and
s_{1},s_{2}
are signs.
Comparing this expression with (
C.7
),
one sees:

(C.29)

\displaystyle s_{1}(d_{\lambda j_{\rm{min}}(f)}-1)=\Tr(M)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(k_{1})\qquad&p=1,\\
(k_{1},k_{2})-1\qquad&p=2,\\
(k_{1},\dots,k_{p})-(k_{2},\dots,k_{p-1})\qquad&p\geq 3;\end{cases}

(C.30)

\displaystyle\frac{s_{1}s_{2}f_{\lambda j_{\rm{min}}(f)}}{f}=M_{21}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1\qquad&p=1,\\
(k_{2},\dots,k_{p})\qquad&p\geq 2.\end{cases}

In view of Lemma
C.1
, this means
s_{1}=s_{2}=+1
. So
M=\left(\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon_{f})\right)^{\lambda}
.
It then follows from Lemma
C.2
and the assumption that the sequence
k_{1},\dots,k_{p}
is not the conjunction of two or more identical subsequences that
\lambda=1
and
M=\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon_{f})
.

Now suppose
q\geq 1
.
Let

(C.31)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=T^{k_{1}}S\cdots T^{k_{p}}S,

(C.32)

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=T^{j_{1}}S\cdots T^{j_{q}}S,

and let
Q^{\prime}=\langle a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}\rangle
be the unique form such that
[\overline{k_{1},\dots,k_{p}}]=\rho_{Q^{\prime},+}
and
a^{\prime}>0
.
It follows from the result just proved that
M=\chi_{Q^{\prime}}(\varepsilon_{f})
, while it follows from (
C.8
) that

(C.33)

\displaystyle N\rho_{Q^{\prime},+}

\displaystyle=\rho_{Q,+}.

In view of
Lemma

4.48
. this means
Q=Q^{\prime}_{N^{-1}}
. Hence

(C.34)

\displaystyle\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon_{f})

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{r_{f}}-1}{2}I+\frac{f_{r_{f}}}{f}S(N^{-1})^{\rm{T}}Q^{\prime}N^{-1}=NMN^{-1},

completing the proof.
∎

Theorem C.4
.

Let
L=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma_{1}&\delta_{1}\\
\gamma_{2}&\delta_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
be any element of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\gamma_{2}>0
.

(2)

There exists an integer
n\geq 1
and sequence of integers
r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{n+1}
such that
r_{i}\geq 2
unless
i=1
or
n+1
, and

(C.35)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=T^{r_{1}}ST^{r_{2}}S\cdots ST^{r_{n}}ST^{r_{n+1}}.

If these conditions are satisfied, the integer
n
and sequence
r_{1},\dots,r_{n+1}
are unique.

Continue to assume the conditions are satisfied. Define

(C.36)

\displaystyle L_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{i}&\delta_{i}\\
\gamma_{i+1}&\delta_{i+1}\end{pmatrix}=T^{r_{i}}S\cdots T^{r_{n+1}}.

Then

(C.37)

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}>\gamma_{3}>\dots>\gamma_{n+1},

(C.38)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{2}}{\gamma_{2}}<\frac{\delta_{3}}{\gamma_{3}}<\dots<\frac{\delta_{n+1}}{\gamma_{n+1}},

(C.39)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{L}=\mathcal{D}_{L_{1}}\subset\mathcal{D}_{L_{2}}\subset\dots\subset\mathcal{D}_{L_{n+1}}=\mathbb{C}.

Proof.

Aside from uniqueness this is proved in
[
72
]
. To prove uniqueness suppose

(C.40)

\displaystyle T^{r_{1}}ST^{r_{2}}S\cdots ST^{r_{n}}ST^{r_{n+1}}

\displaystyle=T^{r^{\prime}_{1}}ST^{r^{\prime}_{2}}S\cdots ST^{r^{\prime}_{n}}ST^{r^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}+1}}.

Assume to begin with that
r_{i}
,
r^{\prime}_{i}\geq 2
for all
i
, including
i=1
,
n+1
.
Let
h\colon\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\to\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\bar{S}
,
\bar{T}
,
\bar{R}
and
\bar{S}_{i}
be as in the proof of Lemma
C.2
. Then

(C.41)

\displaystyle\bar{R}\bar{S}_{r_{1}-1}\bar{R}^{2}\cdots\bar{R}^{2}\bar{S}_{r_{n+1}-1}\bar{R}\bar{S}_{1}

\displaystyle=\bar{R}\bar{S}_{r^{\prime}_{1}-1}\bar{R}^{2}\cdots\bar{R}^{2}\bar{S}_{r^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}+1}-1}\bar{R}\bar{S}_{1},

which, in view of the uniqueness of the alternating expansion
[
2
]
, means
n=n^{\prime}
and
r_{i}=r^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i
. In the general case, choose integers
m
,
\ell
such that
r_{1}+m
,
r^{\prime}_{1}+m
,
r_{n+1}+\ell
,
r^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}+1}+\ell\geq 2
.
Then multiplying both sides of (
C.40
) by
T^{m}
on the left and
T^{\ell}
on the right gives

(C.42)

\displaystyle T^{r_{1}+m}ST^{r_{2}}S\cdots ST^{r_{n}}ST^{r_{n+1}+\ell}

\displaystyle=T^{r^{\prime}_{1}+m}ST^{r^{\prime}_{2}}S\cdots ST^{r^{\prime}_{n}}ST^{r^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}+1}+\ell}.

Applying the result just proved, the claim follows.
∎

Definition C.5

(canonical expansion; length)
.

We refer to the expression on the right hand side of (
C.35
) as the
canonical expansion
of
L
, and we refer to the integer
n
as its
length
.

Lemma C.6
.

Let
R=TS
. Then

(C.43)

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=-RS,

(C.44)

\displaystyle T^{-1}

\displaystyle=-SR^{2},

and, for every positive integer
m
,

(C.45)

\displaystyle ST^{-m}S

\displaystyle=-(TST)^{m}

Proof.

Straightforward consequences of the relations
R^{3}=S^{2}=-I
.
∎

Theorem C.7
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
orbit of forms, let
\mathcal{F}_{+}
be the subset consisting of
\langle a,b,c\rangle\in\mathcal{F}
for which
a>0
, and let
\mathcal{F}_{\rm{HJ}}
be the set of HJ-reduced forms in
\mathcal{F}_{+}
. Let
p_{\rm{min}}
be the minimum value of the period
p
of
\rho_{Q,+}=[\overline{k_{1},\dots,k_{p}}]
as
Q
ranges over the set
\mathcal{F}_{\rm{HJ}}
.
Then for all
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{+}
, the length of
\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon_{f})
is greater than or equal to
p_{\rm{min}}
.

Remark
.

For a given admissible tuple
t
we can use this result together with

Theorem

4.50
to find a matrix
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
for which
L_{+,t_{M}}
has minimum length.

Proof.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{+}
be arbitrary.
The statement is immediate if
Q
is HJ-reduced, so assume not.
Let
L=\chi_{Q}(\varepsilon_{f})
, and
\rho_{Q,+}=[j_{1},\dots,j_{q},\overline{k_{1},\dots,k_{p}}]
where the sequences
j_{1},\dots,j_{q}
and
k_{1},\dots,k_{p}
are chosen as short as possible.
It follows from Theorem
C.3
that

(C.46)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=NMN^{-1}

where

(C.47)

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=T^{j_{1}}S\cdots T^{j_{q}}S

(C.48)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=T^{k_{1}}S\cdots T^{k_{p}}S

and from Theorem
C.4
that

(C.49)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=T^{r_{1}}S\cdots T^{r_{n}}ST^{r_{n+1}}

where
r_{i}\geq 2
for
1<i\leq n
.
We have

(C.50)

\displaystyle LN

\displaystyle=NM,

(C.51)

\displaystyle LN

\displaystyle=T^{r_{1}}S\cdots T^{r_{n}}ST^{m}ST^{j_{2}}S\cdots T^{j_{q}}S

where
m=r_{n+1}+j_{1}
, and

(C.52)

\displaystyle NM

\displaystyle=T^{j_{1}}S\cdots T^{j_{q}}ST^{k_{1}}S\cdots T^{k_{p}}S

The expression on the right hand side of
C.52
is the canonical expansion of
NM
with length equal to
\length(N)+\length(M)
.
If
m\geq 2
, then the expression on the right hand side of (
C.51
) is the canonical expansion of
LN=NM
, which, in view of Theorem
C.4
, means
\length(L)+\length(N)=\length(N)+\length(M)
, implying
\length(L)=\length(M)\geq p_{\rm{min}}
.
Suppose
m<2
.
There are three cases to consider:

Case 1.

m=1
. Then

(C.53)

\displaystyle LN

\displaystyle=T^{r_{1}}S\cdots T^{r_{n}}STST^{j_{2}}S\cdots T^{j_{q}}S.

Using (
C.45
) this becomes

(C.54)

\displaystyle LN

\displaystyle=T^{r_{1}}S\cdots T^{r_{n}-1}ST^{j_{2}-1}S\cdots T^{j_{q}}S.

One goes on in this way, making repeated applications of (
C.45
), until one obtains an expansion in canonical form.
Each application of (
C.45
) reduces the number of
S
operators, so the length of the resulting expansion will be less than
n+q
.
It follows that
\length(L)+\length(N)>\length(LN)=\length(NM)=\length(N)+\length(M)
, implying
\length(L)>\length(M)\geq p_{\rm{min}}
.

Case 2.

m=0
. Then

(C.55)

\displaystyle LN

\displaystyle=-T^{r_{1}}S\cdots T^{r_{n-1}}ST^{r_{n}+j_{2}}ST^{j_{3}}S\cdots T^{j_{q}}S.

Writing
LN=NM=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, Theorem
C.4
applied to the expansion on the right hand side of (
C.52
) implies
\gamma>0
, while the same theorem applied to the expansion on the right hand side of (
C.55
) implies
\gamma<0
. It follows that this case is not possible.

Case 3.

m<0
. It follows from Lemma
C.6
that

(C.56)

\displaystyle LN

\displaystyle=-T^{r_{1}}S\cdots T^{r_{n}+1}S(T^{2}S)^{|m|-1}T^{j_{2}+1}S\cdots T^{j_{q}}S,

which is not possible for the same reason that Case 2 is not possible.
∎

Appendix D
Shintani–Faddeev Jacobi cocycle

The domain of the Shintani–Faddeev Jacobi cocycle given in
Definition

1.16
is a slight extension of that given in
[
72
]
. The purpose of this Appendix is, firstly, to explain that extension; secondly, to explain how
\sigma_{M}(z,\tau)
is defined when
\tau\notin\mathbb{H}
; and thirdly, to prove the cocycle condition (
1.22
).

Let
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. In
[
72
]
the domain of
\sigma_{M}\!\left(z,\tau\right)
is denoted
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{D}_{M}
. If
\gamma\neq 0
, or if
\gamma=0
and
\delta>0
, then
\mathbb{D}_{M}=\mathcal{D}_{M}
. If, on the other hand,
\gamma=0
and
\delta<0
, then
\mathbb{D}_{M}=\mathbb{H}
, whereas
\mathcal{D}_{M}=\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{H}\cup(-\mathbb{H})
.
This discrepancy is due to the fact that, in
[
72
]
, the Shintani–Faddeev Jacobi cocycle was defined by analytic continuation in the
\tau
variable from the upper half-plane, so it is defined for
\tau
in a connected subset of
\mathbb{C}
.

We may define
\sigma_{M}\!\left(z,\tau\right)
on
\mathbb{C}\times\mathcal{D}_{M}
by first extending the definition of the infinite variant
q
-Pochhammer symbol (
Definition

1.14
) from
\tau\in\mathbb{H}
to
\tau\mathbb{H}\cup(-\mathbb{H})
. As it turns out, sending
n\to-\infty
in the definition of the finite
q
-Pochhammer symbol
Equation

1.18
naturally produces a convergent series on the lower half-plane, just as sending
n\to\infty
produces a convergent series on the upper half-plane.

Definition D.1
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{-I}=\mathbb{H}\cup(-\mathbb{H})
. Set

(D.1)

\varpi(z,\tau)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(1-e^{2\pi i(z+j\tau)}\right)&\mbox{ if }\tau\in\mathbb{H},\\
\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(1-e^{2\pi i(z-j\tau)}\right)^{-1}&\mbox{ if }\tau\in-\mathbb{H}.\end{cases}

We now define the Shintani–Faddeev Jacobi cocycle in two cases, where the definition in the first case is the same as in
[
72
, Defn. 4.17]
. The main theorems of this appendix,
Theorem

D.5
and
Theorem

D.6
, will then establish the compatibility of the two cases in this definition.

Definition D.2
.

Let
M\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
, and
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{M}
.

(1)

If
M\neq-T^{k}
for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define
\sigma_{M}\!\left(z,\tau\right)
to be the meromorphic continuation of (
1.21
) from
\mathbb{H}
to
\mathcal{D}_{M}
, which exists by
[
72
, Thm. 4.29]
.

(2)

If
M=-T^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define it by

(D.2)

\sigma_{M}\!\left(z,\tau\right)=\frac{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{M}(\tau)},M\cdot\tau\right)}{\varpi(z,\tau)}=\frac{\varpi(-z,\tau)}{\varpi(z,\tau)}.

The next lemma establishes a basic functional equation relating the behavior of the function
\varpi
on the upper and lower half-planes under the transformation
\tau\mapsto-\tau
.

Lemma D.3
.

The identity of meromorphic functions

(D.3)

\varpi(z,\tau)=\varpi(z-\tau,-\tau)^{-1}

holds for
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{-I}=\mathbb{H}\cup(-\mathbb{H})
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
Definition

D.1
.
∎

We also establish a functional equation that relates the behavior of
\sigma_{S}
on the the upper and lower half-planes, this time under the transformation
\tau\mapsto\frac{1}{\tau}
.

Lemma D.4
.

The identity of meromorphic functions

(D.4)

\sigma_{S}\!\left(\frac{z}{\tau},\frac{1}{\tau}\right)=\frac{e^{2\pi iz}-1}{e^{2\pi iz/\tau}-1}\cdot\sigma_{S}\!\left(z,\tau\right)

holds for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{S}
.

Proof.

Using the identities (given in
[
72
, Sec. 4.8]
)

(D.5)

\displaystyle\dbs(z+1,\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{\dbs(z,\tau)}{2\sin\left(\frac{\pi z}{\tau}\right)}

(D.6)

\displaystyle\dbs(z+\tau,\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{\dbs(z,\tau)}{2\sin\left(\pi z\right)}

(D.7)

\displaystyle\dbs\!\left(\frac{z}{\tau},\frac{1}{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=\dbs(z,\tau)

and the fact that
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{S}\iff\tau^{-1}\in\mathcal{D}_{S}
in (
8.6
), we find

\displaystyle\sigma_{S}\!\left(\frac{z}{\tau},\frac{1}{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\frac{\pi i\tau}{12}\left(\frac{6z^{2}}{\tau^{2}}+6\left(1-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)\frac{z}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}-\frac{3}{\tau}+1\right)}}{\dbs\left(\frac{z}{\tau}+1,\frac{1}{\tau}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\frac{\pi i}{12\tau}\left(6z^{2}-6(1-\tau)z+\tau^{2}-3\tau+1\right)}}{\dbs(z+\tau,\tau)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\sin(\pi z)e^{\frac{\pi i(\tau-1)z}{\tau}}e^{\frac{\pi i}{12\tau}\left(6z^{2}+6(1-\tau)z+\tau^{2}-3\tau+1\right)}}{\dbs(z,\tau)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(\pi z)}{\sin\!\left(\frac{\pi z}{\tau}\right)}e^{\frac{\pi i(\tau-1)z}{\tau}}\sigma_{S}\!\left(z,\tau\right)

(D.8)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{2\pi iz}-1}{e^{2\pi iz/\tau}-1}\cdot\sigma_{S}\!\left(z,\tau\right)

for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
,
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{S}
, which establishes (
D.4
).
∎

Using the above lemmas, we will now establish that the condition in
Definition

D.2
(2) holds true for all
M\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, not just those of the form
M=-T^{k}
, giving a simple expression for
\sigma_{M}\!\left(z,\tau\right)
on
\mathbb{C}\times(\mathbb{H}\cup-\mathbb{H})
.

Theorem D.5
.

Let
M\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.
The identity of meromorphic functions

(D.9)

\sigma_{M}\!\left(z,\tau\right)=\frac{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{M}(\tau)},M\cdot\tau\right)}{\varpi(z,\tau)}

holds for
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{-I}=\mathbb{H}\cup(-\mathbb{H})
.

Proof.

The identity (
D.9
) holds for
\tau\in\mathbb{H}
by
Definition

1.16
, so it suffices to prove this identity for
\tau\in-\mathbb{H}
.
In the case
M=S
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{S}\!\left(z,\tau\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{2\pi iz/\tau}-1}{e^{2\pi iz}-1}\cdot\sigma_{S}\!\left(\frac{z}{\tau},\frac{1}{\tau}\right)

(by
Lemma

D.4
)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{2\pi iz/\tau}-1}{e^{2\pi iz}-1}\cdot\frac{\varpi(z,-\tau)}{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{\tau},\frac{1}{\tau}\right)}

(by (
1.21
))

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{2\pi iz/\tau}-1}{e^{2\pi iz}-1}\cdot\frac{\varpi(z+\tau,\tau)^{-1}}{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{\tau}-\frac{1}{\tau},-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)^{-1}}

(by
Lemma

D.3
)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{2\pi iz/\tau}-1}{e^{2\pi iz}-1}\cdot\frac{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{\tau}-\frac{1}{\tau},-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)}{\varpi(z+\tau,\tau)}

(D.10)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{\tau},-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)}{\varpi(z,\tau)},

proving (
D.9
) in the case
M=S
.
We now prove (
D.9
) for arbitrary
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)
by splitting into cases based on the sign of
\gamma
.

Case 1.
Suppose
\gamma=0
.
Then either
M=T^{k}
or
M=-T^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The identity (
D.9
) holds trivially in the case
M=T^{k}
, because

(D.11)
