\displaystyle\sigma_{T^{k}}\!\left(z,\tau\right)=1=\frac{\varpi(z,\tau)}{\varpi(z,\tau)}=\frac{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{1},\tau+k\right)}{\varpi(z,\tau)}=\frac{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{T^{k}}(\tau)},T^{k}\cdot\tau\right)}{\varpi(z,\tau)}.

It also holds for
M=-T^{k}
by
Definition

D.2
(2).

Case 2.
Suppose
\gamma>0
.
We proceed as in the proof of
[
72
, Thm. 4.29]
.
Divide
\gamma
by
\alpha
with negative remainder to obtain
\alpha=\gamma k-\gamma^{\prime}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and some
\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
0\leq\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma
. Set

(D.12)

M^{\prime}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\alpha^{\prime}&\beta^{\prime}\\
\gamma^{\prime}&\delta^{\prime}\end{array}\right)=S^{-1}T^{-k}M=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\gamma&\delta\\
\gamma k-\alpha&\delta k-\beta\end{array}\right).

Thus
M=T^{k}SM^{\prime}
, and the cocycle condition known to be valid for
\tau\in\mathbb{H}
tells us that

\displaystyle\sigma_{M}\!\left(z,\tau\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{T^{k}}\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{SM^{\prime}}(\tau)},SM^{\prime}\cdot\tau\right)\sigma_{S}\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{S}(\tau)},M^{\prime}\cdot\tau\right)\sigma_{M^{\prime}}\!\left(z,\tau\right)

(D.13)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{S}\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{M^{\prime}}(\tau)},M^{\prime}\cdot\tau\right)\sigma_{M^{\prime}}\!\left(z,\tau\right).

By analytic continuation, this relation holds for
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{M}\cap(M^{\prime})^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{D}_{S}\cap\mathcal{D}_{M}
. We have
\gamma\delta^{\prime}-\delta\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma(\delta k-\beta)-\delta(\gamma k-\alpha)=\alpha\delta-\beta\gamma=1
, so (unless
\gamma^{\prime}=0
)
\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{\gamma^{\prime}}-\frac{\delta}{\gamma}=\frac{1}{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}>0
, and thus
\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{\gamma^{\prime}}>\frac{\delta}{\gamma}
. Therefore

(D.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{M}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}\setminus\left(-\infty,-\frac{\delta}{\gamma}\right],

(D.15)

\displaystyle(M^{\prime})^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{D}_{S}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}\setminus\left(-\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{\gamma^{\prime}},-\frac{\delta}{\gamma}\right]\supseteq\mathcal{D}_{M},

(D.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{M^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}\setminus\left(-\infty,-\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{\gamma^{\prime}}\right]\supseteq\mathcal{D}_{M},

so in fact (
D.13
) holds for
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{M}
. (In the omitted case
\gamma^{\prime}=0
, we must have
M^{\prime}=T^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and again the relation holds for
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{M}
.) In particular, it holds on
-\mathbb{H}
, so it may be used to prove (
D.9
) by induction on
\gamma
, using Case 1 as the base case and (
D.10
) to prove the inductive step. To be explicit, applying the inductive hypothesis and (
D.10
) to (
D.13
) gives

\displaystyle\sigma_{M}\!\left(z,\tau\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{SM^{\prime}}(\tau)},SM^{\prime}\cdot\tau\right)}{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{M^{\prime}}(\tau)},M^{\prime}\cdot\tau\right)}\cdot\frac{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{M^{\prime}}(\tau)},M^{\prime}\cdot\tau\right)}{\varpi\!\left(z,\tau\right)}=\frac{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{SM^{\prime}}(\tau)},SM^{\prime}\cdot\tau\right)}{\varpi\!\left(z,\tau\right)}

(D.17)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{T^{k}SM^{\prime}}(\tau)},T^{k}SM^{\prime}\cdot\tau\right)}{\varpi\!\left(z,\tau\right)}=\frac{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{M}(\tau)},M\cdot\tau\right)}{\varpi\!\left(z,\tau\right)}.

Case 3.
Suppose
\gamma<0
.
The relation

(D.18)

1=\sigma_{M^{-1}M}\!\left(z,\tau\right)=\sigma_{M^{-1}}\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{M}(\tau)},M\cdot\tau\right)\sigma_{M}\!\left(z,\tau\right)

is valid for
\tau\in M^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{D}_{M^{-1}}\cap\mathcal{D}_{M}=\mathcal{D}_{M}
. Also,
M^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\delta&-\beta\\
-\gamma&\alpha\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, so Case 2 applies to
M^{-1}
. Thus,

(D.19)

\sigma_{M}\!\left(z,\tau\right)=\sigma_{M^{-1}}\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{M}(\tau)},M\cdot\tau\right)^{-1}=\frac{\varpi\!\left(z,\tau\right)^{-1}}{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{M}(\tau)},M\cdot\tau\right)^{-1}}=\frac{\varpi\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{M}(\tau)},M\cdot\tau\right)}{\varpi\!\left(z,\tau\right)}

for
\tau\in-\mathbb{H}
.
∎

Finally, we prove that the cocycle relation for the Shintani–Faddeev Jacobi cocycle holds true everywhere, including in those cases not covered in
[
72
]
.

Theorem D.6
.

Let
M,M^{\prime}\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. The identity of meromorphic functions

(D.20)

\sigma_{MM^{\prime}}\!\left(z,\tau\right)=\sigma_{M}\!\left(\frac{z}{j_{M^{\prime}}},M^{\prime}\cdot\tau\right)\sigma_{M^{\prime}}\!\left(z,\tau\right)

holds for
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{M,M^{\prime}}:=\mathcal{D}_{MM^{\prime}}\cap(M^{\prime})^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{D}_{M}\cap\mathcal{D}_{M^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

The cocycle relation holds for
\tau\in\mathbb{H}\cup(-\mathbb{H})
as an immediate consequence of
Theorem

D.5
. It then holds for
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{MM^{\prime}}
by analytic continuation.
∎

Appendix E
Real quadratic fields with an odd-trace unit

Theorem

1.51
splits the characterization of abelian extensions of real quadratic fields (conjecturally) generated by
r
-SICs into two cases, and it would be nice to know how often each case occurs. We give a partial result, showing that the trace of the fundamental unit is odd (the case when the full maximal abelian extension is conjecturally attained) a positive proportion of the time.
Thus, for a positive proportion of real quadratic fields,
r
-SICs may be viewed as a geometric solution to Hilbert’s twelfth problem (albeit a conjectural one, depending on the Stark–Tate Conjecture and the Twisted Convolution Conjecture).

If a quadratic field contains an odd trace unit, then elementary methods show that its discriminant must obey a congruence restriction modulo
8
.

Lemma E.1
.

If
K
is a real quadratic field containing a unit of odd trace, then
\disc K\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right)
.

Proof.

Let
\Delta=\disc{K}
. If
\Delta
is even, then
\mathcal{O}_{K}=\mathbb{Z}+\frac{\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}\mathbb{Z}
, so
\Tr(\alpha)
is even for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
Thus,
\Delta
must be odd; indeed,
\Delta\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
because
\Delta
is a discriminant.

The ring of integers of
K
is therefore

(E.1)

\mathcal{O}_{K}=\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1+\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}\mathbb{Z}.

The quotient ring
\mathcal{O}_{K}/2\mathcal{O}_{K}
is represented by the congruence classes of
0,1,\frac{-1+\sqrt{\Delta}}{2},
and
\frac{1+\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}
. If
\varepsilon
is a unit of odd trace in
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
, then

(E.2)

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\pm 1+\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\mathcal{O}_{K}\right);

(E.3)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\prime}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\pm 1-\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\mathcal{O}_{K}\right).

Thus,
\Nm(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon\varepsilon^{\prime}\equiv-\frac{1-\Delta}{4}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,2\mathcal{O}_{K}\right)
.
If
\Delta\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right)
, then it follows that
2|\Nm(\varepsilon)
, which contradicts the fact that
\varepsilon
is a unit. Thus,
\Delta\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right)
.
∎

Lemma E.2
.

Let
K
be a real quadratic field such that
\disc K\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right)
. Consider the following conditions:

(1)

There exists no cubic number field
L
such that
\disc L=\disc K
or
\disc L=4\disc K
.

(2)

The field
K
has a unit of odd trace.

(3)

There exists no cubic number field
L
such that
\disc L=4\disc K
.

Then (1) implies (2), and (2) implies (3).

Proof.

Consider the exact sequence of ray class groups

(E.4)

1\to\frac{\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}}{\U_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})}\xrightarrow{\iota}(\mathcal{O}_{K}/2\mathcal{O}_{K})^{\times}\to\Cl_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\xrightarrow{\pi}\Cl(\mathcal{O}_{K})\to 1.

This is the exact sequence given in
[
73
, Thm. 5.4]
, specialized to the case when
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
,
\mathfrak{m}=2\mathcal{O}_{K}
, and
\Sigma=\Sigma^{\prime}=\{\}
. The group
(\mathcal{O}_{K}/2\mathcal{O}_{K})^{\times}\cong\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
because
\disc K\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right)
. The unit group
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
has an element of odd trace if and only if the the map
\iota
is an isomorphism, that is, if and only if the map
\pi
is an isomorphism. Setting
h_{K}=\left|\Cl(\mathcal{O}_{K})\right|
, then exactly one of the following is true:

(A)

\left|\Cl_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\right|=h_{K}
, and
K
has a unit of odd trace; or

(B)

\left|\Cl_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\right|=3h_{K}
, and
K
does not have a unit of odd trace.

Let
\phi
be any nontrivial group homomorphism
\phi:\Cl_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\to\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
.
By the existence theorem of class field theory and the Galois correspondence, there exists a cubic subextension
M/K
of the ray class field
H_{2}/K
corresponding to
\ker(\phi)
under the Galois correspondence. The field
M
is sextic over
\mathbb{Q}
, with
\Gal(M/\mathbb{Q})\cong S_{3}
. Pick a cubic subfield
L
of
M
;
L
is unique up to isomorphism. Using the conductor-discriminant formula, one can show that
\disc L=\disc K
if
\phi
factors through the map
\pi
, and
\disc L=4\disc K
otherwise. Moreover, this correspondence defines bijections

(E.5)

\displaystyle\{\phi:\Cl(\mathcal{O}_{K})\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}\}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\{\mbox{cubic }L/\mathbb{Q}:\disc(L)=\disc(K)\},

(E.6)

\displaystyle\{\phi:\Cl_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z},\phi\neq\pi\circ\phi^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\{\mbox{cubic }L/\mathbb{Q}:\disc(L)=4\disc(K)\}.

We now prove that (1) implies (2). Suppose that there is no cubic number field
L
with
\disc L=\disc K
or
\disc L=3\disc K
. Thus, there is no nontrivial group homomorphism
\phi:\Cl_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\to\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
. Since
\Cl_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
is abelian, this means that
3\!\nmid\!\left|\Cl_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\right|
. It then follows that we are in case (A), and
K
has a unit of odd trace.

Finally, we prove that (2) implies (3). Suppose that
K
has a unit of odd trace, so we are in case (A):
\left|\Cl_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\right|=h_{K}
. Thus, every nontrivial group homomorphism
\phi:\Cl_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\to\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
factors through
\Cl(\mathcal{O}_{K})
. By (
E.6
), there is no cubic number field
L
with
\disc(L)=4\disc(K)
.
∎

In the authors’ understanding, analytic number theory and arithmetic statistics have not yet produced techniques capable of finding the exact asymptotic density of the number of real quadratic fields with a unit of odd trace.
However, techniques for counting quadratic and cubic fields may be used to give upper and lover bounds using
Lemma

E.1
and
Lemma

E.2
.
The following proof is based on ideas suggested by Frank Thorne
[
109
]
and Jiuya Wang
[
111
]
.

Proof of
Theorem

6.15
.

In the proof, we will use the notation
\Delta_{K}=\disc K
for the field discriminant and

(E.7)

\displaystyle N_{2}(X;\text{[conditions]})

\displaystyle=\#\{K:[K:\mathbb{Q}]=2,0<\Delta_{K}<X,\text{ [conditions]}\},

(E.8)

\displaystyle N_{3}(X;\text{[conditions]})

\displaystyle=\#\{L:[L:\mathbb{Q}]=3,0<\Delta_{L}<X,\text{ [conditions]}\}

for counts of quadratic and cubic fields of positive discriminant satisfying certain conditions. We write
N_{2}(X)
and
N_{3}(X)
if there are no conditions.

An integer
\Delta>1
is the discriminant of a quadratic field if and only if it satisfies the congruence conditions
\Delta\equiv 1,5,8,9,12,13\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,16\right)
and
p^{2}\!\nmid\!\Delta
for all odd primes
p
.
By the following standard sieve-theoretical calculation, taking
\mu(d)
to be the Möbius function and taking
r_{d}
to be the smallest positive solution to
r_{d}\equiv n_{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,16\right)
and
r_{d}\equiv 0\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,d^{2}\right)
,

(E.9)

\displaystyle\{n\leq X:n\equiv n_{0}\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,16\right),p^{2}\!\nmid\!n\mbox{ for }p\mbox{ odd}\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq d\leq X^{1/2}\\
2\,\!\nmid\!\,d\end{subarray}}\mu(d)\left\lfloor\frac{X-r_{d}}{16d^{2}}\right\rfloor

(E.10)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq d\leq X^{1/2}\\
2\,\!\nmid\!\,d\end{subarray}}\mu(d)\frac{X}{16d^{2}}+O(X^{1/2})

(E.11)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq d\\
2\,\!\nmid\!\,d\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu(d)}{16d^{2}}+O(X^{-1/2})\right)X+O(X^{1/2})

(E.12)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{16}\prod_{p\neq 2}\left(1-\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)\right)X+O(X^{1/2})

(E.13)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{12\zeta(2)}X+O(X^{1/2}).

Thus, taking
n_{0}\in\{1,5,8,9,12,13\}
and
n_{0}\in\{5,13\}
, respectively, we have

(E.14)

\displaystyle N_{2}(X)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\zeta(2)}X+O(X^{1/2}),\mbox{ and}

(E.15)

\displaystyle N_{2}(X;\Delta_{K}\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6\zeta(2)}X+O(X^{1/2}).

Thus, by using
Lemma

E.1
and dividing these two asymptotic equalities (
E.15
) and (
E.14
), we obtain the upper bound

(E.16)

\frac{N_{2}(X;2\!\nmid\!\Tr(\varepsilon_{K}))}{N_{2}(X)}\leq\frac{N_{2}(X;\Delta_{K}\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right))}{N_{2}(X)}=\frac{1}{3}+O(X^{-1/2}).

To obtain the lower bound, we appeal to the results of Taniguchi and Thorne
[
106
]
on counting cubic fields with local restrictions. If
L
is a cubic field, then
\mathcal{O}_{L}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
a “maximal cubic ring over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
,” that is, a product of valuation rings of finite extensions of
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
whose degrees sum to
3
. There are exactly
10
maximal cubic rings over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, shown in the following table, which is based on the tables given in
[
106
, p. 2487–2488]
and on the database of Jones and Roberts
[
64
]
. In the table,
\omega
is a root of
x^{2}+x+1=0
, and
\alpha
is a root of
x^{3}-x-1=0
.

\mathcal{O}_{L}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}

forced congruence conds.

density wt.

\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}

\Delta_{L}\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right)

\Delta_{L}\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right)

1/2

\Delta_{L}\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right)

1/3

\Delta_{L}\equiv 20\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,32\right)

1/4

\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\sqrt{-1}]

\Delta_{L}\equiv 28\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,32\right)

1/4

\Delta_{L}\equiv 12\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,32\right)

1/4

\Delta_{L}\equiv 56\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,64\right)

1/8

\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\sqrt{6}]

\Delta_{L}\equiv 24\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,64\right)

1/8

1/8

In particular, the congruence condition
\Delta_{L}\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right)
is equivalent to the local restriction
\mathcal{O}_{L}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\omega]
, and the congruence condition
\Delta_{L}\equiv 20\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,32\right)
is equivalent to the local restriction
\mathcal{O}_{L}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\sqrt[3]{2}]
. The following asymptotics are thus special cases of
[
106
, Thm. 1.3]
10
10

10

Note that the more general result
[
106
, Thm. 6.2]
also allows one to impose additional congruence restrictions, but we don’t need to do this.
, with the secondary term absorbed into the error term:

(E.17)

\displaystyle N_{3}(X;\Delta_{L}\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right))

\displaystyle=\frac{C^{+}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\omega]})}{12\zeta(3)}X+O(X^{5/6}),

(E.18)

\displaystyle N_{3}(X;\Delta_{L}\equiv 20\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,32\right))

\displaystyle=\frac{C^{+}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\sqrt[3]{2}]})}{12\zeta(3)}X+O(X^{5/6}).

The constants
C^{+}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\omega]})
and
C^{+}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\sqrt[3]{2}]})
are computed from the “density weights” in the table:

(E.19)

\displaystyle C^{+}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\omega]})

\displaystyle=\frac{1/2}{1/6+1/2+1/3+1/4+1/4+1/4+1/8+1/8+1/8+1/8}=\frac{2}{9},

(E.20)

\displaystyle C^{+}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\sqrt[3]{2}]})

\displaystyle=\frac{1/4}{1/6+1/2+1/3+1/4+1/4+1/4+1/8+1/8+1/8+1/8}=\frac{1}{9}.

However, these are not actually the asymptotics we want—we should also be
removing
non-fundamental discriminants by imposing the condition that
p^{2}\!\nmid\!\Delta_{L}
for odd primes
p
.
The condition that
p^{2}\!\nmid\!\Delta_{L}
is equivalent to the condition that
\mathcal{O}_{L}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is not totally ramified at
p
; see
[
106
, Sec. 6.1]
.
Let
C^{+}_{\rm ntr}(p)
denote the local density of non-totally ramified
\mathcal{O}_{L}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.
The following asymptotic formulas are also special cases of
[
106
, Thm. 1.3]
, treating
Y
as a constant.

\displaystyle N_{3}\!\left(X;\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta_{L}\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right),\\
p^{2}\!\nmid\!\Delta_{L}\mbox{ for odd }p\leq Y\end{array}\!\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{C^{+}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\omega]})}{12\zeta(3)}\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq Y\\
2\,\!\nmid\!\,p\end{subarray}}C^{+}_{\rm ntr}(p)\right)X+O_{Y}(X^{5/6}),

\displaystyle N_{3}\!\left(X;\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta_{L}\equiv 20\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,32\right),\\
p^{2}\!\nmid\!\Delta_{L}\mbox{ for odd }p\leq Y\end{array}\!\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{C^{+}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\sqrt[3]{2}]})}{12\zeta(3)}\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq Y\\
2\,\!\nmid\!\,p\end{subarray}}C^{+}_{\rm ntr}(p)\right)X+O_{Y}(X^{5/6}).

The
C^{+}_{\rm ntr}(p)
are calculated using the table in
[
106
, Sec. 6.2]
to be

(E.25)

C^{+}_{\rm ntr}(p)=\frac{1/6+1/2+1/3+1/p}{1/6+1/2+1/3+1/p+1/p^{2}}=\frac{1+p^{-1}}{1+p^{-1}+p^{-2}}=\frac{(1-p^{-3})^{-1}}{(1-p^{-2})^{-1}},

and hence

(E.26)

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq Y\\
2\,\!\nmid\!\,p\end{subarray}}C^{+}_{\rm ntr}(p)=\frac{1-2^{-3}}{1-2^{-2}}\cdot\frac{\zeta(3)}{\zeta(2)}+O(Y^{-1})=\frac{7\zeta(3)}{6\zeta(2)}+O(Y^{-1}).

Thus, we obtain the asymptotic formulas

\displaystyle N_{3}\!\left(X;\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta_{L}\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right),\\
p^{2}\!\nmid\!\Delta_{L}\mbox{ for odd }p\leq Y\end{array}\!\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{7}{162\zeta(2)}X+O(X/Y)+O_{Y}(X^{5/6}),

\displaystyle N_{3}\!\left(X;\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta_{L}\equiv 20\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,32\right),\\
p^{2}\!\nmid\!\Delta_{L}\mbox{ for odd }p\leq Y\end{array}\!\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{7}{324\zeta(2)}X+O(X/Y)+O_{Y}(X^{5/6}).

By sending
Y\to\infty
(sufficiently slowly compared to
X
), we have

\displaystyle N_{3}\!\left(X;\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta_{L}\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right),\\
\Delta_{L}\mbox{ fundamental}\end{array}\!\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{7}{162\zeta(2)}X+o(X),

\displaystyle N_{3}\!\left(X;\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta_{L}\equiv 20\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,32\right),\\
\Delta_{L}\mbox{ fundamental}\end{array}\!\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{7}{324\zeta(2)}X+o(X).

By
Lemma

E.2
(specifically the fact that (1) implies (2)), we have the bound

(E.35)

\displaystyle N_{2}(X;

\displaystyle 2|\Tr(\varepsilon_{K}),\Delta_{K}\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right))

\displaystyle\leq N_{3}\!\left(X;\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta_{L}\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right),\\
\Delta_{L}\mbox{ fundamental}\end{array}\!\right)+N_{3}\!\left(X;\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta_{L}\equiv 20\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,32\right),\\
\Delta_{L}\mbox{ fundamental}\end{array}\!\right)

(E.40)

\displaystyle=\frac{7}{162\zeta(2)}X+\frac{7}{324\zeta(2)}(4X)+o(X)

(E.41)

\displaystyle=\frac{7}{54\zeta(2)}X+o(X).

Thus, using
Lemma

E.1
,

(E.42)

\displaystyle N_{2}(X;2\!\nmid\!\Tr(\varepsilon_{K}))

\displaystyle=N_{2}(X,\Delta_{K}\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right))-N_{2}(X;2\!\mid\!\Tr(\varepsilon_{K}),\Delta_{K}\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right))

(E.43)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{6\zeta(2)}X-\frac{7}{54\zeta(2)}X+o(X)

(E.44)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{27\zeta(2)}X+o(X).

By dividing the asymptotic formulas (
E.44
) and (
E.14
), we obtain the lower bound

(E.45)

\displaystyle\frac{N_{2}(X;2\!\nmid\!\Tr(\varepsilon_{K}))}{N_{2}(X)}\geq\frac{2}{27}+o(1).

Combining (
E.16
) and (
E.45
) completes the proof.
∎

The upper bound in
Theorem

6.15
is fairly trivial, as it only uses the congruence restriction
\disc K\equiv 5\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,8\right)
; one might hope to get a better upper bound using the fact that “(2) implies (3)” from
Lemma

E.2
, which we did not use at all! It is not clear how to so do at present, as we would need some additional result to tell us that the cubic fields with
\disc L\equiv 20\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,32\right)
hit enough
distinct
discriminants.

Numerical evidence suggests that the true asymptotic density of real quadratic fields with a unit of odd trace is about
2/9
(or
22.2\%
), that is, about
2/3
(or
66.7\%
) of the real quadratic fields with discriminant congruent to
5
modulo
8
. According to a calculation performed in Mathematica, among real quadratic fundamental discriminants
\Delta=8k-3
for integers
k\in[10^{10},10^{10}+10^{6}]
, about
66.9\%
have a unit of odd trace. Calculations involving smaller discriminants suggest a positive bias in the count of such discriminants up to
X
that is going to zero slower than
X^{1-\delta}
for any
\delta>0
.

Finally, we give a brief comparison to the problem of solubility of the negative “Pell” equation.
The existence of a unit of negative norm in the real quadratic field
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D})
is equivalent to the existence of an integer solution to the equation
x^{2}-Dy^{2}=-1
, whereas the existence of a unit of odd trace in the real quadratic field
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D})
with
D\equiv 1\,\left(\operatorname{mod}\,4\right)
is equivalent to the existence of an integer solution to the equation
x^{2}+xy+\frac{1-D}{4}y^{2}=1
with
y
odd.
In the former case, however, the asymptotic density of such
D
is zero, and this leads to additional complications.
Nonetheless, as in our problem, upper and lower bounds of the same order of magnitude can be given on the number of such
D
up to
X
; this was done by Fouvry and Klüners
[
43
, Thm. 1]
.
The narrow class group of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
plays a similar role in their work as does the ray class group modulo
2
in our
Lemma

E.2
.

Appendix F
1
-SIC data tables

In this appendix, we collect tables containing the algebraic data canonically specifying ghost
1
-SICs, and non-canonically specifying
1
-SICs, in dimensions
d=4
–
100
.
This list is conjecturally complete for all Weyl–Heisenberg covariant
1
-SICs; there is exactly one row corresponding to each predicted
\EC(d)
-orbit.

We have numerically computed an approximate ghost SIC using the Shintani–Faddeev modular cocycle and checked that the ghost overlaps satisfy

(F.1)

\displaystyle\biggl|\Tr(\tilde{\Pi}_{\mathbf{p}}\tilde{\Pi}_{\mathbf{0}})-\frac{(1-\delta^{d}_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}})+\delta^{d}_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{0}}(d+1)}{d+1}\biggr|<10^{-66}

in all of the following cases:

(1)

For the 251 rows corresponding to
d\leq 60
;

(2)

For the 39 rows with
60<d<100
where
Q=\langle 1,1-d,1\rangle
;

(3)

For the 4 rows with
d=100
.

For
d\leq 20
and for
d=100
we have also used our necromancy procedure to numerically compute the set of associated
1
-SICs.
See
section

8
for more details about our numerical calculations.

Each ghost fiducial is specified by an admissible tuple
t=(d,1,Q)
with dimension
d
and integer binary quadratic form
Q
.
The relations between
t
and the remaining data in the table are as follows.
First factorize
(d+1)(d-3)=f_{d}^{2}\Delta_{0}
where
\Delta_{0}
is a fundamental discriminant.
Then
Q
(conjecturally) yields a valid
1
-SIC if
\disc(Q)=f^{2}\Delta_{0}
where
f\!\mid\!f_{d}
.
While
d
and
Q
determine all other data needed for constructing a ghost, the additional columns are included for convenience, since they contain additional algebraic data that may be “difficult” to compute, for example requiring integer factoring or finding a fundamental unit.
The column
\Delta_{0}
contains the fundamental discriminant of
Q
and the column
f
the conductor of
Q
.
The columns
h
and
\Cl(\mathcal{O}_{f})
contain the class number and class group respectively.
The column
\Gal(E_{s}^{(1)}/H)
is the Galois group of the candidate overlap field ramified at the first infinite place.
(This is isomorphic to
\Gal(E_{t}^{(2)}/H)
, the Galois group of the candidate
ghost
overlap field ramified at the
second
infinite place.)
The column
L^{n}
is of the form where
L
is the positive-trace generator of the stability group of
Q
with the same sign as
Q
, as defined in
Definition

1.28
, and
A=L^{n}
.
The column ‘
a.u.
’ is marked ‘
Y
’ if there is an anti-unitary symmetry.
Finally,
\ell(A)
is the length of the word expansion of
A
using the Hirzebruch–Jung (negative regular) reduction into the standard (
S
and
T
) generators of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.
This is one measure of the complexity of constructing the actual ghost fiducial vector for that input.
The forms
Q
in this list were selected among class representatives to minimize this complexity, although this choice is not generally unique.
In order to write down a ghost
1
-SIC explicitly, one must also choose a twist
G
, which may canonically be taken to be the identity matrix
G=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
; the choice of twist does not affect the
\EC(d)
-orbit.
The data in each row are sufficient to compute a ghost fiducial, but to fully specify a
1
-SIC, one must additionally make an arbitrary, non-canonical choice of a sign-switching Galois automorphism
g
.
